# Chapter 1: The North Wall

Ila had never been wrong about a distance she had walked herself, so when the tower came up short she walked it again.

Two and a half spans to a pace. She had checked that stride against the brass rule at Taravan when she was nineteen, and against a chalk line her father laid every spring after. The brass had drifted twice. Her legs had not.

The yard of Garh Neshar lay grey and trampled under a sky the colour of old pewter. It was the eve of the Snow Fair, the thirty-first winter of Queen Sarala's reign. Down on the river meadow someone was testing a drum, one slow boom at a time, and the smell of roasting barley had begun to climb the hill. At the yard's far end stood the tower where Regent Pritam sat under guard, square and ugly, and lying to her.

She stopped at the north-west corner. Last winter she had paced the outside of that wall and logged it two spans out of true, then let it go as a drunk mason's sin. The inside had been a locked storeroom then. This winter Pritam was held in it, and the council had given her a pass. That afternoon, with a hill-council captain tapping his boot in the doorway, she had paced the long room behind the wall and taken off the thickness, which the window cut gave her to the finger. Now the figure closed from both sides. The room ran a full pace shorter than the outside allowed.

Half a span for sloppy plaster, she allowed. That left two. The same two.

"Kiran," she said. "Chain."

He sat on the mounting block with the brass chain across his knees, chewing a strip of dried apricot. He was twenty-five now, and since the new law went up he had stopped looking like a man waiting to be arrested for a rope. "I chained it twice, Ila-ji. It said what you said."

"Then do it a third time. I like the answer better when someone else has done the work."

He got up, grumbling, and walked the chain along the foot of the wall. The links rang cold and bright. At the corner she read the last link against her mark.

Twenty-two paces, outside. She opened her field book and wrote under it, in the cramped hand she kept for things that mattered: *North wall, two spans out of true, void?*

Question marks were not her habit. She left this one anyway. A question mark was honest. A guess dressed as a measurement was not.

"Void," said Kiran, reading over her arm. "That's where he keeps his conscience. Tight little room, nobody visits." The grin was quick and slightly too bright, the way it always was when he had decided in advance that nothing was serious.

Ila snorted. Then the joke snagged on something. Last autumn she had written to the Taravan archive for the Shilak Cairn file, and the clerk had answered, very pleasantly, that the crown kept it in a drawer she had no key to. She tucked the pencil behind her ear, and the thing she had carried for a month came out before she had decided to say it.

"Rohan wrote that the stones at the cairn spoke at the thaw. Everyone there felt it in their hands."

"Stones talk," said Kiran. "Mine mostly say 'move your foot.'"

It fell flat, and he heard it fall. His hand rose to his left ear, to the torn rim, and rubbed it once with two fingers. He did that when the weather turned, and when the talk did.

Ila watched the fingers. "They said the Old Bargain is unpaid. The pass closes for good after the fourth winter unless somebody pays. And the crown wants every way mapped, while the archive won't show me what it knows about the cairn. I'd like to know whether a map of a pass that is going to close is a map or an obituary."

"Ila."

"Yes?"

"It aches when it's about to snow." He bent and began winding the chain onto its frame, link over link, very carefully. "Come on. The light's going."

She let it be. Silence was not an answer, but it was a reading, and she kept it. His sister had tied a rope on Ila's brother last winter, and Kiran was about to become some sort of brother to her too. She had no intention of prying him open in a yard.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They went out by the main gate, where the notice was.

It was nailed to the left post, the new law in eleven lines on good rag paper, with two seals under it: the Shilak post's brown, the hill council's blue. Ila knew the lines by heart. *Licences carry two seals. Medicine always passes. No person is hanged or held to blame for a death on a bound crossing.* Somebody had scratched a small, careful goat into the margin beside the third line. The clerks had not scraped it off.

The road below was busier than a winter road should have been. Two pairs of crossers came down off the Neshar road, each tied wrist to wrist with a short grey rope, each with an oilcloth roll strapped flat to the back. A clerk in a blue sleeve stopped each pair and compared their seals with deep suspicion. Redbark. She could smell it, dry and bitter, like the inside of a medicine chest. A year ago it had crossed in the dark, and a crosser could hang for carrying it.

"They're allowed to smile," she told the second pair, who watched the clerk as if he were a hanging judge.

The man did, uncertainly. They went on.

"Hill-council men on the grain," Kiran said, nodding along the lane. Four stood in the lee of the long barn, in undyed wool with blue cords on their shoulders, leaning on spears they clearly did not enjoy. The barn had a new bar and a new lock. "Pritam's grain. They count it every morning and chalk it on a slate. Chetu's boy says it's gone up by a sack since the last count."

"Up?"

"Somebody gave it back. Or somebody's lying about the slate."

"Slates don't lie. People who write on them do." She meant it as a joke and it came out as a rule. She frowned.

Kiran kicked at a frozen rut. "I watched Varun go out of this gate in summer. Hands tied, a guard each side. Rohan's old lieutenant, the one who sold the regent every road he guarded. Did Rohan say what happened when he got to Taravan?"

"That Varun cried at the gate," said Ila. "Rohan's never said what he felt about it, but he mentioned the crying twice. Once in each letter. He's not subtle."

Kiran laughed, and it was real this time, and they walked the rest of the way in a better temper.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The guest-house kitchen had been given to her in the first week of snow, with the cook's blessing and the cook's own stool. Ila lit two lamps and spread the sheets across the table.

Twelve sheets, fixed at the corners with river stones. The summer passes lay across them in her finest ink: every col, every scree-slope, every saddle a laden goat could manage, the contours so close the paper looked grey in places. The commission had come with the summer post, sealed in the queen's wax. *Map every winter way.* Not a word on how.

She had drawn what she could reach without being killed. Of the winter ways she had nothing. A bound pair could cross a closed pass, and the stones would light the line for them, and nobody had ever drawn it, because a map was made in daylight and the winter line was seen only by the bound.

Her thumb went down the nearest sheet. There on the third ridge was the little hand-lettered name she should not have lettered: *Tamar's Shoulder*, for the cook who had fed her at seven when she was sulking. Farther east, a long ugly knob of rock was *Kiran's Elbow*. The crown's ledger called them Point 4392 and Point 4410, and the queen's clerks would, she supposed, cross out every name of hers.

A map was true or it was false. She believed that the way she believed her legs. And she knew, in the part of her honest about small things, that naming a peak after someone she liked was not a measurement at all.

She turned the lamp up. Without the winter ways, her map would sit in the Taravan archive as a very good summer map. They would thank her. And the chief surveyor's chair would go to Pell Orlan, who had drawn three hills from a window and never been cold in his life.

Her winter licence went before the hill council tomorrow, at the Fair. With it, the council would name a guide for her, bind her to him at the cairn, and she could cross the Saddle. Without it she was a woman of thirty-one with a lovely sheet of paper, and Orlan would have the chair by spring.

She stared a long time. Then she picked up her chain, her field book and a lantern, because she had remembered something, and the remembering was like a stone in a boot.

The stair. She had counted it from the outside, twice. She had never counted it from the north landing, where the room began.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The tower stair was a spiral of grey stone with an iron rope for a rail, cold enough to burn through her glove. The guard at the foot looked at her pass, then her chain, then the sky, and waved her up like a man who would rather not be asked later what he'd seen.

She climbed with the lantern low and counted under her breath.

On the north landing she stopped. The stair widened to a flat of old boards, and the north wall, rough-dressed and sweating a little, ran a few paces away to the dark of the long room's door. She put her palm on it. It was colder than the rest. Not by much. She would not have noticed if she had not been looking.

"The crown sent you to measure a prison."

The voice came from below, pleasant and level. She did not jump. She set the lantern on the step and turned.

Prince Kunal of Neshar stood three steps down with one hand loose on the rail. He was thirty-three, tall as mountain men were tall, in a plain dark coat with the hill council's blue cord at the shoulder. His long face looked made for laughing and not much used for it lately. He had a small ink stain on his right thumb. She had seen him twice across a drafting table in the guest-house, arguing over a rope.

"Highness," she said.

"Kunal, on a stair." He came up one step. "I'd like to know why the crown wants a prison measured."

"You'd have to ask the crown."

"Would the crown tell me?"

"It told me. The commission says every way. A tower with a prince's uncle in it is, in the narrow sense, a way."

"A way to where?" said Kunal.

She had stood at that drafting table beside her brother and found it funny, a man who answered every question with another. It was less funny on a cold stair with her lantern at his feet.

"How many steps from the yard door to here, Highness?"

"Does it matter?"

"Humour me."

"Thirty-nine," he said at once, easily, a number counted a thousand times with a hand on the rail. "That's what everyone gets."

"Forty."

"Is it?"

"There's a half-height step under the north landing, just there." She pointed with her chin. It was set so low into the boards that her boot would have missed it if she had not been counting. "Everyone steps over it. It's still a step."

Kunal looked at it. For a moment, only a moment, he looked at the wall beside it instead, the cold wet length of it, and then back at her face.

"Children's count," he said. "My cousins and I learned thirty-nine. We were small, and the step was for ghosts."

"You can't leave out a step because it's embarrassing."

"Can't I? I'm told it's done in every court in the world."

She nearly laughed. She held up the field book instead.

"The north wall. Inside, the long room is two spans shorter than the outside allows. I walked the outside last winter and found the same two. I walked the inside today, and my chain-man checked it twice. There's a void."

She said it flat out. A number was most of what she had, and she watched him, because she was also a person who watched.

Kunal stood still. Then he stepped past her onto the landing and laid his hand on the cold stone, in the place she had, as if it might say something.

"Do you know how often my family has been told our house is crooked?"

"Often, I'd guess."

"Garh Neshar was built by a mason named Madan, who was drunk for most of the work. He's in the family songs, mostly for that. The kitchen leans, the hall floor is a stormy lake, and a ball set at the door of the east wing will roll out the window." He turned and leaned back against the wall, arms folded. "A drunk man built a lopsided tower. Add it to your demands at council tomorrow. I'm sure you have a list."

"I have no list. I have a licence application, four lines, asking the council to name a guide and bind me to him at the cairn. You read it." Her voice had gone brisk. "You argued for ten spans of rope, and when I said eight, exactly, you asked whose arm."

"I did ask whose arm."

"It was the guide's own."

"I remember." His thumb moved across the ink stain, rubbing at it, though it had never come off. "It was a good answer."

The drum on the meadow boomed once, faint through the stone.

Kunal pushed himself upright. He was not smiling now. He looked almost kind, which she distrusted more than the smile.

"I'll say it straight, Surveyor, since you've been straight with me. Tomorrow I will speak against your licence."

She waited. He added nothing.

The field book lay against her ribs with its one question mark. Tomorrow the council would ask what she had found in the yard, and a void was a number she could give them. Give it, and she was the crown's woman prying at a prince's wall, handing him the best reason to vote her down. Hold it back, and her licence might survive his speech. Orlan's chair against her own creed. She had never drawn a line she knew was false. Leaving one off was not quite that, and she turned the difference over like a loose tooth. Keep it out of council until she knew what it was, she decided, and hated the decision at once.

"Against," said Ila.

"Against."

"All of it? Or only the crossing?"

"Does it matter?" He opened his mouth again, and she saw the next question coming, ready and polished, the third in a row.

Something in her came loose.

She laughed. It burst out of her, loud and sudden, off the stone and down the stair, a bray that had been getting her sent from dinner tables since she was eleven. Below, the guard must have flinched. She put the back of her hand to her mouth and could not stop.

"Against," she managed. "You climbed a spiral stair in the dark to say it to my face. One plain sentence. Highness, I thought you were built entirely of questions."

Kunal stared at her. It was the first time she had seen him with nothing to say. He looked startled, like a man who has put his shoulder to a door and found it open all along.

"I did say it straight out," he said at last, and for once it was not a question.

"You did." She wiped her eye with a knuckle and took up her lantern. "Thank you. It's the most honest thing anyone in this house has said to me since the snow." She stepped carefully over the half-height step, into the light. "Good night, Highness. It's forty."

She went down the stair counting, and did not look back, and on the thirty-first step she began to wonder what he had been standing so close to the north wall to hide.

# Chapter 2: The Council's Price

The runner would not speak until he had counted the silver.

Kunal let him. He stood in the lee of the long barn with the petition tube under his arm and the Fair waking below him, and he counted along in silence as four bits of Neshar silver went into the boy's grubby palm. Thin squares, each with one corner clipped off clean. The boy tilted the last one to the light and checked the clip, the way Dhanu had taught him.

"Well?" said Kunal.

"Dhanu says you're to hear it in council. Not in the yard." The boy shut his fist. "He says you'll want to be sitting."

"Does Dhanu often tell me when to sit?"

"He said you'd ask that." The boy was already backing away. "He said don't answer it."

Then he was gone between the barley roasters, small and quick, and Kunal was left with the drums.

They were up all along the river meadow, a dozen skins beating out of step beneath a white sky that had not yet decided whether to snow. Smoke from the roasting pans lay low on the frost. The Snow Fair smelled as it had when he was six, of scorched grain and wet wool, and for the length of a breath he let himself like it.

Then he shifted the tube under his arm. Inside, on four sheets of the best rag paper, lay the hill council's petition. It asked for self-rule for Neshar under the crown, sealed by every valley, to be set before Queen Sarala at the Shilak Cairn once Shilak's meltwater had run. A year of dinners had gone into those sheets. A year of saying one sentence in forty kitchens. *Nothing in Neshar should be hidden from Neshar.*

He had said it so often that it no longer felt like something he had decided. It felt like weather.

He climbed to the hall.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The council sat under the brass bell, nine of them at the long table. Madan's drunken floor tipped Old Bhim's chair a little toward the fire. Bhim spoke for the lower terraces, and he sat as though the whole slope sat behind him. Someone had rung the bell to call them in, and its hum was still in Kunal's back teeth.

Ila sat at the foot with her licence flat before her and the crown's packet beside it. She had ink on one cuff and her hair was escaping its pin. She gave him a short nod, as one surveyor to another. He returned it and wondered, not for the first time that morning, whether he had been laughed at on a stair.

"The crown's cover sheet," said Bhim, and slid a loose paper down the table. "Read it, Highness. My eyes are for goats."

Kunal took it. Good paper, the queen's hand at the foot. He read it to the table in the voice he used for petitions, clear and a little warm.

"'To the council of the hill valleys of Neshar, from Sarala, queen of Hemkot. We commission our surveyor Ila, of the Shilak post's family, to draw every way by which a person may cross between Neshar and Hemkot in winter, so that the crown may hold every way.'" His eyes were a phrase ahead of his tongue, and the next words were wrong. He read them anyway. "'And the way beneath the Shilak Cairn.'"

His voice went on through the seal line and the date, and under it the cold went all the way down.

The princes of Neshar had a way. It had no name outside his family's mouths and no line in any book, because his family did not write down what it meant to keep. Now it had a place in a crown commission, in a clause of seven words.

"That clause isn't in my covering note," said Ila.

She said it to the paper, not to him. Her note said *every way*, she added, and nothing after. He watched her face. Either she had not known, or she was the best liar he had ever sat across from. He had spent a year learning that the crown did not write clauses it did not mean, and he did not believe in luck.

"Refuse it," said Bhim.

"Bhim—"

"Refuse the licence. The crown has held these passes sixty-one winters with a rope and a peg. Now it wants them held with ink. Every way, it says. So it can hold every way." The old man's hands lay on the table like two roots. "Let her draw the summer roads. Everyone knows the summer roads."

"Or sell it," said Chetu.

Chetu of Upper Kuhl was round and pink, and he sweated in any weather. "Forgive me, but we're all friends here. Upper Kuhl has forty sacks left and every one wears Pritam's bell. The queen wants a map. Let the queen pay in grain, and let the surveyor name a ridge or two after whoever pays."

"I have named no ridge after anyone who—" Ila stopped. "That's not the question."

"It's my question," said Chetu mildly.

Bhim turned his whole head toward Kunal. "You said it at midsummer, Highness. At this table. A hidden road is a rope round a neck."

He had. He had said it with his palm flat on the wood, and the table had liked it, and it had been a good sentence. Now it had a hook in it, and the hook was in his own mouth.

He saw his position clearly, which was the worst part. To argue for refusal was to argue that a way ought to stay hidden, and he could not say that aloud in this room. Not after a year. And a council that turned away the queen's own surveyor, a few weeks before the queen heard its petition, would arrive at the cairn as a council with something to bury. Anyone who wanted self-rule refused would only have to point.

"I'll speak," said Ila.

She stood. She was shorter than he had remembered and she planted her feet like a woman at a theodolite.

"A map is true or it isn't. If it's true, it serves whoever reads it. The crown will have a copy. I will give the council one, and every valley can read where its own roads run. What anyone does with a true line is not in the line." She looked along the table, level and unhurried. "I can't draw what isn't there, and I won't leave out what is."

It was a clean argument. It was also the sort of thing said by someone who had never had a true line used against her.

*Whoever knows the way owns the people,* thought Kunal, and said nothing, and thought it harder.

At the back wall, Dhanu's runner cleared his throat. He had stood there through all of it, and now at last he spoke.

"Highness. Dhanu says to say it to the table." He drew a breath and said it fast. "Captain Raghu, with the red moustache, who ran from last winter's Fair with a dozen of the regent's men. He's in the high hamlets. A shepherd saw him at Sarn fold. He's going door to door asking for the princes' way."

Nobody spoke. The brass hum had finally died out of the room.

"What princes' way?" said Chetu.

"A story," said Bhim slowly. His eyes were on Kunal. "The old people's."

"Is there a valley in Neshar," said Kunal, "without a story about its lords' secret road?"

It got a thin laugh. It was also not an answer, and he saw Ila write something in her field book. Two courts, then, one bearing a seal and one bearing a red moustache, and both had the same words in their mouths. Somebody had told both. He did not know who, and there was a tower in this yard that held one possibility.

He put the petition tube on the table and made himself speak slowly.

"The council seals the licence," he said, "on one condition. A member of this council is bound to the surveyor for the whole survey. On her rope, at the cairn, every step of the way."

"Who?" said Chetu, already pleased.

"Me."

For a moment he had the satisfaction of watching nine faces fail to find an answer. Then Bhim leaned forward, and the chair tilted him on toward the fire.

"So you can choose what she sees."

"Would you rather Chetu chose?"

"I'd choose a price," Chetu admitted, and two of the council laughed in spite of themselves. Kunal had already done the sum. A bound pair could not lie to each other without paying for it in their hands, but silence was free. He could hold his tongue for a winter. He had held it for a year.

"We vote when the bell rings again," said Bhim heavily. "After the meat. Let the surveyor think about her rope."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The kitchen passage was the warmest place in Garh Neshar and the only one where nobody wanted anything from him.

It smelled of onions and barley bread. Chuni sat on an upturned crate with her sleeves pushed past her elbows and a pot in her lap, scraping at it with a shell. She was nine. She was the scullery girl, and she had a way of looking at a visiting prince as if he were a large cat that might or might not bite.

Kunal sat down on the flour bin.

"Count with me," he said. "You're slow after ten."

"I'm not slow."

"You're thorough. Hands off the pot." He tapped the stone floor with two fingers, a steady beat. "One for the goat who knows the gate."

"One for the goat who knows the gate," said Chuni, who had a perfect ear.

"Two for the stone that will not wait. Three for the bend where the wind turns back."

"Four for the fold with the broken latch?"

"Four for the fold with the broken latch." He was almost smiling. "Go on. Five."

Nobody who heard it would hear a road. It had no place names in it, only goats and latches and wind. A child could carry it in her head all her life and never know what she carried. Route-keepers died. Songs outlived them, and Kunal had never found a safer way to hand on a road he would not hand to anyone.

He had built it that way, and it had cost him a year of not thinking about what he asked of everyone else.

"Nine and thirty," he sang, low, tapping, "and the ghosts go by—"

A shadow crossed the passage door.

Ila stood there with a roll of paper under one arm and a pencil behind her ear. She had stopped on the threshold the way a person stops when someone else's counting touches their own. She looked at the girl. She looked at the pot. Then she looked at him.

*Nine and thirty. Children's count, for ghosts.* He had given her those very words on the north landing, with his hand beside a cold wall, and now he had sung them in her hearing, to a child, in time to a road. She had walked in on the tune.

His fingers stayed on the stone. They wanted the next beat, and he held them there and let the line die in his mouth.

Neither of them spoke. Ila's eyes dropped, very briefly, to his tapping fingers. Then she gave Chuni a nod, one counter to another, and walked on down the passage. Her footsteps went away at an even pace, two and a half spans each.

"You stopped," said Chuni. "Nine and thirty and what?"

"And nothing," said Kunal. "It's a silly song. Finish your pot."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The bell rang again, and the hall filled, and the vote was quick.

It was six to three. Bhim voted no and Chetu voted yes, and the other seven sorted themselves between them without much heat. Kunal, who had named the condition and stood inside it, sat out the count. The clerk wrote it down. Then Bhim, with the sourness of a man who had lost a small battle and expected to lose a larger one, turned his chair a hand's breadth toward the foot of the table.

"The council seals your licence, Surveyor, if you take the condition. A member bound to you, at the cairn, for the whole of the survey. His Highness has named himself." His mouth stayed flat. "Will you take him?"

She had been sitting through it with her field book shut and both hands on the cover.

Now she stood. She did not look at Bhim. She looked at Kunal, down the whole length of the table, and he saw nothing in her face that resembled surprise.

"Then you'll walk where I measure, Highness," said Ila.

It was said lightly, and it landed like a weight on a scale. Some of the council laughed. Chetu clapped once. The clerk bent to write.

Kunal sat very still, with the petition tube under his hand. Her licence lay open at her place, and he could read it from here. On the line left for the guide's name, the pencil had already been there. *Kunal of Neshar*, in her cramped small hand, rubbed soft by a day of her sleeve. It had been written before the bell. Before Bhim asked. Before he opened his mouth.

She had wanted him on the rope. He had walked up and put himself there, in front of nine witnesses and the queen's seal, within eight spans of her, where every lie would show in his fingers.

*Whoever knows the way owns the people.* The thought came back with a different face.

# Chapter 3: Names to the Stones

The council clerk found Ila in the guest-house kitchen at dusk and would not sit down.

"Surveyor." He held a flat packet in both hands, the way a man holds something that might spill. "The crown's commission came in two parts. The cover sheet was for the council. This part is yours. We were told not to open it, and we haven't."

She took it. Oilcloth outside, then a fold of good paper, then the wax. She turned it to the lamp.

The royal archive's seal, pressed deep. A thread of red ran through the wax, thin as a vein, curling once at the edge. She had seen that same thread in the wafer Rohan pressed on the Fair meadow a year ago, blood the queen had mixed in Taravan with her own hand. Her brother had stood white-faced beside it. It was a bad thing to remember at a kitchen table.

Across the fold, in a hand she had seen on her own commission, was a single line.

*Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes. Not before. S.*

"Where are the Glass Lakes?" said Ila.

The clerk looked relieved to have a question he could answer. "I don't know, Surveyor."

"Neither do I."

That was the part she did not like. She knew every named water in Hemkot. She had drawn tarns the size of a threshing floor, and given three of them numbers. She went through them now, valley by valley, and none was called Glass.

She turned the packet over. A man who wanted to know what was inside could slide a hot knife under that wax and nobody would see. She knew exactly how it was done. She laid the packet on the table and eyed it the way she eyed a bench mark someone had told her not to move.

The queen had put a clause in the council's sheet that Ila had never seen. Now she had put a place in Ila's hand that Ila had never heard of. Someone was drawing a map of her, and she was the last to see it.

She wrapped the packet in its oilcloth and sewed it into the lining of her coat, over the ribs. It was a poor hiding place. It was also hers.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kiran came to the kitchen door after the lamps were lit. He had his pack on, and his cap in his hands.

"I've a place," he said. "Two of the post's licensed guides, going over to Shilak at first light with seed barley. Bindu's cousins. One ties to me, the other to the barley-man." He turned the cap. "I've not been home since before last winter."

"Then go home." She said it before she heard how it sounded. "I mean it kindly. I can't take you. It's pairs only. You know the rule. A third on the rope and the mountain takes all three."

"I know." He tried the grin. It came a little crooked. "I only wanted to ask."

"You asked, and it's no. Go on, then. You've got the look of a man with a second thing in his pocket."

He looked at her. She had meant it for a joke. He didn't take it as one.

"If you find it," he said. "Where the Bargain is paid. Wherever it is. Send word to me first. Not to Didi. Not to your brother." His thumb came up and found the torn rim of his ear. "To me."

"Why you first?"

"Because I'd like to hear it before the rest of them." He gave a short breath of a laugh. "I'm the nervous sort. Ask anyone."

She pictured him walking the Saddle line alone in his head, all winter, wondering whether the mountain would close behind him.

She went and put her hand on his arm. It was a stiff gesture, the kind she gave a chain-man who had dropped a pole and was sorry.

"I'll send word," she said. "Eat before you go. They'll feed you on sattu and talk."

"Thank you, Ila-ji."

He left. She stood a while with her hand still half out, and told herself he was worried for her, and that it was kind of him, and that the kindness was the whole of it.

She wrote *Kiran: ear, again* in her field book, then drew a line through it, because it was not a measurement.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The Meadow Cairn stood on the shoulder above the Fair meadow, and they climbed to it in the dark.

It was the nearest jogini cairn, and the only one a licensed pair could reach without crossing a pass alone. Kunal went ahead, with a lantern he kept low. Ila came behind with her pack and her field book and a coil of rope over one shoulder. Frost cracked under her boots like a thin plate.

She had sent to Taravan for the rope in the autumn, undyed wool from Lata's house, spun to a hand's thickness. She had measured it against her own arm, span by span, on the guest-house floor, because the law said the guide's own arm and she was the guide. Eight spans. The wool smelled of lanolin and cold.

"There's a clock on this, you know," said Kunal over his shoulder.

"Is there?"

"A bond ends when meltwater first runs at the cairn where it was tied. Not at the cairn you name. This one." He stopped to let her draw level. "The Meadow Cairn faces south and holds the sun. Its water runs about a month before Shilak's does. And when it runs, the rope is just rope, wherever we're standing."

"Wherever?"

"If we're at the top of a ridge, we're at the top of a ridge. If we're on the wrong side of it, well." He lifted a shoulder. "The mountain is very exact. It's one of the things I like about it."

Her book was open before he'd finished. Eleven winters of thaw dates, each in her own hand. She ran the Meadow column against Shilak's by the light of his lantern. In a hard year the gap closed to three weeks. In a soft year it opened.

"Thirty-five days," she said. "In a soft year. That's what I lose."

"Lose?"

"The crown thinks I have the whole winter. I have the winter minus thirty-five days." She shut the book. "Did you plan that?"

"Did I plan a thaw?" said Kunal. But he was almost smiling. "You'd have found it in your own tables by morning. I only saved you a night's sleep."

It was true, and she disliked that it was true. She walked on.

The cairn rose out of the dark as a ring of flat stones, chest-high, pale with rime. Ila put her mitten on the top course and felt the stones hum under it, a low note, steady as a struck bowl. The east was going grey. On the inner face, where the rime had not reached, someone had cut two hands into the rock, joined by a line.

"Wrists," said Kunal.

They tied it the way the law said, the same knot on each other. She used a warden's double hitch, the one her brother tied on everything, and was irritated to find her fingers shaking. His hands were cold. He tied hers a little loose, then drew it snug and met her eye as if to ask whether that was right.

"Snug," she said.

"Good."

The rope lay between them in a long slack curve, eight spans, and it was heavier than it looked.

They stood either side of the stones. Ila spoke first, because she had decided to.

"Ila, of the Shilak post's family, surveyor. Bound for the Shilak Cairn."

The hum did not change. She had expected something and got nothing, which left her feeling foolish, then relieved.

"Kunal of Neshar," said Kunal. "Bound for the Shilak Cairn."

He said the last words a breath late. It was nothing. A man collecting himself. The hum held steady, low and level. But she had spent her life spotting a hair of difference in a line, and she spotted it, and put it away where she kept the stair count and the two spans of wall.

A warmth went round her wrist and stopped there, like a hand laid on it. Across the stones, Kunal looked down at his own wrist, and then at hers.

"Well," he said. "That's done."

"Is that all you do? Say well?"

"I'm told I say it at weddings."

She nearly laughed. Instead she did what she had come up the hill meaning to do.

"Highness. Watch my fingers."

His eyes went to them. She held them out, palms down, ten ordinary fingers in the grey light.

"I like you," said Ila.

She said it flat, and she meant him to believe it, because the rule would only count it if she did. The stones lifted half a note, thin and cold, before anything touched her. Then the cold came, a tight pinch in every fingertip, as if she had pressed them to a frozen bucket. She watched them go white, nail beds first, to the first joint.

"Ah," said Kunal.

He had believed her. She saw it, for the length of one breath, a small start of surprise at the plain words. Then his eyes found her fingers and the surprise went out of him, and what replaced it was so quick and open that she did not at first know what it was.

He laughed.

It was not the laugh she knew, the polished one for councils. It came out of him in a rush and bent him half over the stones, one hand on the cold rim to hold himself up. He laughed until the rope shivered between them. The first light caught the long face and found in it, for a moment, the man it had been made for.

"An hour," he said, when he could. "Is that an hour? For something so small?"

"An hour. Small lie." She flexed the white fingers and winced. "Don't gloat. You're wondering whether I meant it. Go on. Say you don't care what I meant."

"I don't," said Kunal, and then stopped, and looked at his own hands as if they had done something without leave. They stayed pink. He seemed more startled by that than by anything the stones had done. "That was true. It was a great deal easier than I expected."

"It's useful," said Ila. "Now we both know the rope works and that neither of us can say a thing for free."

"Except to anyone else."

"Except to anyone else."

"Or in silence."

"Or in silence." She tucked her chilled hands into her armpits. "You'll be very good at silence."

"I've had practice," he said. He was still half smiling, and it did not reach the part of his face that had gone careful again.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They walked west all day, up the Neshar road, because it was the only road a bound pair could take toward the Saddle. The rope paid out between them, eight spans, and she learned the length of it with her body. Seven and a half was the edge of comfort. At eight a thin chill began under the ribs and told her to come back.

They spoke little. He answered two of her questions with questions, and she let him. She wrote down the road: a culvert, a cattle-shed, the bridge where the road crossed a stream that made no noise because it was frozen to the bed. By the first culvert the white had gone from her fingertips, and the pink came back with a sting.

By dusk the road had climbed into thin air and open slope. The wind had a bite. Behind them the Fair meadow was a smear of smoke far below, and ahead lay the long white rise of the pass, going violet.

"Stop," said Kunal.

It was the first thing he had said in an hour. She stopped. He was looking not at her but past her, at a cairn on the bank beside the road, a waist-high stack of grey stones.

A light was coming up in it.

It came as pale as breath on a cold glass, blue where the stones touched, then brighter, a thread and then a rope of blue. It lifted from the cairn and ran along the road ahead of them, stone to stone, cairn to cairn, up the slope and away toward the Long Saddle. A path, laid out in the dusk by someone who knew the mountain. She had been told about it all her life. She had never seen it, and her throat had closed.

"There it is," she said, and her voice came out small. "The winter line."

She got out her book with clumsy fingers, because the only way to look at a thing like that was to measure it. Bearing. Distance between lights, a hundred and forty paces, then a hundred and sixty. Her pencil was moving before she thought.

Then she looked east.

There was another thread.

It was fainter. At first she took it for the afterimage of the first, a bruise in the eye. But it did not move when she blinked. It ran off to the east over a shoulder of ground she had walked in summer, and it was broken, a light and a gap and a light, as though something kept going out in it. It dropped toward a valley that lay in shadow. She knew the shoulder. She had named a ridge near it. She did not know the valley. It was not on her sheets. No stream ran from it, no path led to it, no shepherd had ever told her of it.

She had been through every fold of Neshar within two days' walk of the Fair, in boots and in ink. A valley was not a thing a person missed.

"Kunal," she said. "What's that?"

He said nothing.

She turned. He stood with the rope slack in his hand and his face toward the faint line, and the whole of him had gone still. Men who were thinking fidgeted; she had watched plenty. This was the stillness of a goat on a ledge when a shadow crosses.

His lips were moving.

She had to hold her breath to hear it. "Three," he was saying, "for the bend where the wind turns back." The east line gave a light, and another. "Four. Four for the fold—"

The fourth light did not fade. It went, like a lamp pinched in a fist, and the line beyond it stayed dark. Kunal stopped as if the count had been struck from his mouth.

She knew the words. She had stood in a kitchen passage and heard them tapped out for a scullery girl, with the last line cut off. It was no nonsense rhyme. She thought of a runner at the council's back wall: *Captain Raghu, with the red moustache. Sarn fold. Going door to door asking for the princes' way.*

She knew, too, what the hill posts taught every chain-girl about cairn-light. A light that dimmed slowly was a road nobody had walked in a generation, and the jogini let it go. A light pinched out all at once meant a cairn pulled down, a stone lifted from its place. Someone had been at that fold, or someone was there now. It could be Raghu's men. It could be anyone who knew a rhyme.

His hands hung at his sides, pink, quite pink, in the blue light. No lie there. No speech either.

The wind picked up and made the cairn hum.

Two lines lay in front of her, one strong and one failing. She was a woman with a commission to draw every winter way, a sealed letter against her ribs, and a rope of Lata's wool binding her to the one man in Neshar who clearly knew the answer.

"Which way?" said Ila.

Kunal turned his head slowly. His eyes, dark in the blue, held hers. When he spoke it was gentle, and she heard in it the old courtly sharpness, very faint, like a knife wrapped in cloth.

"Which does your queen want?"

She opened her mouth. Nothing came, because there was nothing she could say that was free.

He moved before she could try again. He looked at the dark gap in the east, where the fourth light had been. He looked at the rope on his wrist, and at the open book in her hand with the west line half written in it. She could only guess at what it cost him. If he had held that road in his head for a year, a surveyor's pencil a hand's breadth from it was a hard thing to stand beside. And the light had died at a fold where a man with a red moustache was knocking on doors. If he had kept the way for the people behind those doors, he might want it lit more than hidden. Or he was his uncle's nephew, and wanted her pencil off it. She could not tell which. His face gave her nothing to read.

Then he stepped off the road and onto the frozen slope, east, toward the broken light, and the rope drew taut between them.

Ila stayed on the west road. She planted her boots in the thin snow, and the rope tugged at her wrist. Seven spans. Seven and a half. At eight the chill began under the ribs, just where the sealed letter lay, and she let it come. It crept to her elbows. It reached her teeth. Ahead of her on the slope, Kunal had stopped with his back to her, neither calling her on nor hauling.

Every way, her own note said. Every way, the queen's sheet said, and a clause besides.

The cold took her hands. Her pencil would not hold.

She closed the book on the page with the west bearing. The east one was not on it. Whether her fingers had refused or she had, she could not have said, and the rope was already drawing her down the slope.

She went into the dark after him, and behind her the western line burned on, bright and empty.

# Chapter 4: The Walnut Man

The law, when she finally saw it work, looked like a man dropping a sack.

Ila stopped at the head of Ranu's lane with the rope slack between her wrist and Kunal's. Below her, Roop swung a sack of redbark off a mule and onto Nimmi's stoop. Chand took it from him, a big, shy man who held it as if it might take offence. It was noon. The lane was watching. Nobody hid anything and nobody ran.

She had spent six weeks in a Garh Neshar guest-house while eleven lines were argued into being. She had measured the drafting table twice. She had never once pictured a sack, a stoop and nobody afraid.

Over Nimmi's door, chalk tallies marched across the lintel in fives, one row for every dose poured. Beside them, faded to the grey of old frost, sat the print of a hand the width of a plate. She had heard that mark described. The description had not been big enough.

"Roop," said Kunal.

Roop looked up, looked at the rope on Ila's wrist, and gave Kunal a nod with no surprise in it. He said nothing at all. She was collecting a great many things she had not been told.

At dawn, where the east track met the Garh road, she had watched Kunal choose.

The broken line had not come back. All night the fourth light stayed dead, and the dark beyond it with it. Ila had stood with her book shut on a page that had no east bearing.

"Raghu's men are at that fold," she said. "Or they've been. You walked us toward it all night."

"I did." He was looking east at the grey hills, rubbing the ink stain on his thumb. "Two bound pairs, at the pace we'd keep, reach Sarn fold tomorrow night. Ranu's runners can be there by dusk today. They go by the low lanes, where no rope's needed, and they know every door."

"So you'll send them."

"I'll ask." He said the word as if it had a taste. "It should be my feet. A man who sends others to a door he ought to knock on himself is my uncle's kind of man. I know exactly what it costs." He looked east a moment longer, and the courtly face slipped, and what lay under it was only tired. Then he turned south. "Ranu is a day down the Garh road. Part of the way I mean to show you starts there. Part of it you can draw without me."

That was a half answer, and she had written it down as one.

A shout came up the lane. It came from low down, from a small boy with a runny nose and both fists in the air.

"Walnut man! Walnut man!"

He ran straight at Kunal, with no caution at all, and Ila watched the prince of Neshar go down on one knee in the frozen mud as if he had been waiting all morning to do it. The courtly face was gone. A younger one had replaced it.

"You've grown," said Kunal.

"I'm *seven*."

"Seven. Past all reason." He dug in his coat and brought out a walnut, brown and wrinkled, with a twist of red thread knotted round its middle. "Mind you don't eat it in one go. Your grandmother will have my head."

Pinku took it in both hands like an egg. Kunal straightened the boy's cap, and his thumb lingered on the child's cold ear, very gently, before he let go.

Ila stood quite still.

It came together the way a traverse closes, one angle after another. Meera at the drafting table, her left hand lying in its glove like something she was minding for someone else. Meera telling it in her dry way, as a joke on herself: the masked buyer. The cord on Nimmi's latch with three hard knots and a long tail ending in a loop. The silver, thin squares of Neshar silver, each with one corner clipped off clean. And a child sent home from the grove with a walnut tied in red thread.

She looked at Kunal's coat pocket. She thought of him at that table, twice, silent, across from the woman telling it.

"Ila-ji," said Roop, with the tact of a man who had seen her face. "Nimmi's inside. She'll want you to see the slope."

"In a moment," said Ila. "Thank you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The walnut terraces rose behind the village in steps, each wall built of the same grey stone. The trees were bare. She walked up to the second tier and stopped where the rope ran out its seven and a half spans and the chill began, and she turned.

Kunal had followed. He stood with his hands in his sleeves, looking at the ground, and she thought: he knows what I'm about to say. He's known since the boy ran at him.

"The masked buyer," said Ila.

"Yes."

"Two winters of medicine for Ranu. Paid for without haggling, in silver with a corner cut off."

"Yes."

"My brother's betrothed sat in that guest-house and told me the story as a mystery. And you came to the table twice and sat across from her and said nothing."

"I was not asked," said Kunal, "and silence is free." Then, before she could answer: "That's too neat. I'm sorry. I was asked, in effect, by everything. I chose not to."

She waited. If you waited, the rope did half the work.

"The silver was my uncle's," said Kunal. "From Garh Neshar's stores, the ones he holds in the crown's name. I took it a bag at a time and ran it down the back road through the lower lanes. Two winters. The regent was selling grain to this village at four times the autumn price, and I paid for medicine with the profit." He looked at her. "It was the only honest use I could find for it."

Her hands were warm in their mittens. She could feel that they were warm.

"Would you have mapped the back road?" said Kunal. "The road Nimmi's runners used. If you'd known, when you took the commission, that a road existed where a man was keeping children alive. Would you have drawn it?"

It was the question she had been dreading, because she knew the answer. She could tell the truth, or she could say something kind, and the rope would show which.

"Yes," said Ila.

She said it clearly. She kept her eyes off her fingers, though she wanted to look. Beside her the terrace wall hummed faintly in the wind and stayed low and level.

"I'd have drawn it," she said. "It's a true road. I'd have given a copy to the queen and to the council, and I'd have believed that was neutral. I still don't know it wasn't." She took her mitten off, and her fingers lay pink in the cold. "I don't like that you've seen my hands."

"I like it very much," said Kunal quietly, "and I find I mind that I do."

He said it without a smile, and she was glad. She put the mitten back on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Nimmi met them at her own gate, a broad woman with a face like a fist and a voice that listed things.

"Surveyor. Cough, you can hear. No fever. The Dev boy has gained four pounds. Come with me. You've a brother who stood on this slope and I want you to see the rest of it."

She never asked what the rope was, or why a prince stopped at eight spans below the last terrace, respectfully, with his back turned.

The graves lay in the lowest terrace cut, in rows, with the frozen earth heaped over each. Ila began to count because there was nothing else she could do with them. The small mounds were child-length. Some carried a pine bough. One carried a wooden goat with one leg missing. One carried a spinning top, set upright in the dirt as though the child had just let go.

"Thirty-two," said Nimmi, when she had finished. "Thirty-one at your brother's first count. The thirty-second was a man who'd nursed all of them. Your brother held the rope while we dug."

Ila's throat did what it had done at the winter line. "He told the Fair thirty-two."

"He said it straight. I've heard worse said in better coats." Nimmi stood with her arms folded. "We were to burn that night. Every marked house. You've heard it."

"I heard there were torches."

"There were torches. There was an officer on a tall grey horse in a slate cloak, with a grey beard, who rode up to the captain with the red moustache and said lower them. And the captain lowered them." She said it flatly. "I stood on that step and looked at his face the whole time. So I could say it afterwards."

"Who was he?" said Ila. "My brother never learned his name. The assembly's paper says the regent's general was exiled. It doesn't say why."

Nimmi's eyes went to the slope, to the graves, and came back. "I know who he was."

"Then—"

"That's not mine to spend," said Nimmi.

It was said without heat. Ila had met a hundred witnesses who would not speak, and most were afraid. Nimmi was not afraid. She was holding something that did not belong to her, and had decided to hold it shut.

"Ranu will say it, when it's time," said Nimmi. "Not before. Ask me what I'll tell you."

Ila thought. "Is he alive?"

"He's alive."

It was a small answer, and it opened something larger beneath it. A man who gave an order that saved thirty-odd houses, and had been sent away for it, and had a healer who would not speak his name. Ila wrote nothing down. She kept her hand on the field book and left it there, and for the first time in eleven winters she understood that not writing was also a measurement.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"The walnut," she said, when they were climbing again.

Kunal had said nothing for several minutes. He walked a pace ahead, scanning the slope, one hand idle on the rope. "Ask."

"Why a walnut? Why red thread? You gave it to that boy like it was his allowance."

"It's the oldest thing I own that isn't a name." He glanced back. "Route-keepers send true word with a walnut tied in red thread. Not a letter, not a seal. If a runner comes to a hamlet with news and no walnut, the news is a lie, or it's from someone who isn't mine. We've kept it ten generations. The regent's men never found it, because it looks like a gift for children."

"So when a boy brings a walnut, the message is true."

"When a boy brings a walnut, I'd stake a village on it." He seemed to hear what he had said. "I'm about to. One goes to Sarn fold before dark. Nimmi's eldest runner is saddling now."

She walked on. The terraces gave out above them into scrub, and the path became a stony track. She turned her own mind over, honestly.

She had arrived in Neshar with a picture of him, and the picture was tidy. A prince of his uncle's house, a man who spoke of openness and kept every road in his head. A man playing at rebellion for a throne, with a good voice for it. She had even named him, privately, after a peak she did not like.

That picture did not account for a kneeling man in mud, or two winters of stolen silver, or a walnut, or a man who hated sending others to his own door. It was not wrong in every line. He did keep every road in his head. He had told her so himself, by omission, at every stair. But she had taken his measure from too far off. He was not his uncle's kind of prince. She did not yet know what kind he was.

Where the track forked, a line of old worn stones led off to the right, up behind the terraces and over a bare spur. She had not seen it from the village. It did not appear on her sheets.

"That one," said Kunal.

"What is it?"

"The salt road." He shrugged. "Ranu's runners use it to go round Garh Neshar's gates. It runs up behind the terraces, over two spurs, and comes down on the dry side of the Garh. No warden has walked it in fifty winters."

She had her book out before he had finished. She sat on a flat rock with her cold fingers and drew it: the first spur, the angle of the terrace walls below it, the long slope of scree that carried the road up through the broken ground. She paced it. Two and a half spans a pace. She took a bearing off the spur and another off the corner of Nimmi's roof, and the road came up out of the paper line by line.

"Where is it unlawful?" she said.

"Nine places. I'm fairly sure the smugglers have counted them."

"Then count them for me."

He did, slowly, and she put a small neat cross at each. A ford on the closed side of the border stones. A gap knocked through a boundary wall. A goat-bridge over a toll gully. Six more. Under the lee of the second spur there was a stone-ringed hollow with a soot-black hearth, where Nimmi's runners halted to sleep out of the wind, and she drew that too, a little ring with a flame in it.

He stood at the end of the rope and watched her, and the watching lay on her neck like a hand. She kept her pencil level.

It was a true road. It was also a lifeline. When the page was done she held it up to the light, and the nine crosses looked back at her like nine charges.

"A warden could hang men from this page," said Ila. "Here, and here, and the place where they sleep. You know that."

"I do."

"I drew it anyway." She turned it so he could see. "That's what a map is, Highness. I told you at the table. If I leave out a lifeline to be kind, it's not a kinder map. It's a false one."

When she looked up, his face was the one she had seen at the cairn, the open one, startled by itself.

"I was waiting," said Kunal slowly, "for you to say you wouldn't."

"Were you disappointed?"

"I'm trying to find out."

They went on up the track. The wind had risen, and the cold was a hard clean one. Above the last terrace, where the scrub ended, a little cairn stood against the sky, a knee-high stack of grey stones with a flat cap. It was so small that she would have walked past it. Down the slope behind it, faintly, she thought she saw a pale thread of blue.

Kunal stopped beside it and put a hand on the top stone, as he had put his hand on the cold north wall of the tower.

"This is the salt road," he said. "It's the last way I'll show you that every smuggler in Neshar already knows. Anyone with a mule has walked it. You can draw it for the queen and you'll take nothing from anyone."

She looked at the page in her hand, and then at him. Every smuggler in Neshar. Nobody on that sheet was in more danger than the day before.

"You chose it for that," said Ila. "The one road where drawing costs nobody anything. You wanted to see whether I'd draw it, and what I'd say when I did. I was the weight on a scale."

"Yes," said Kunal. He did not apologise.

"And after this?"

"After this, you earn them."

The sentence sat in her ears like a stone dropped down a well, and she listened for the bottom of it. Behind her lay the village and the thirty-two graves. Ahead lay the road to a valley that did not exist on her sheets, and a man who had decided, and told her, that the next lines would cost her something.

She did not know what the price was. She did not know what he would count as earning. She knew only that a man who named a price before she asked had been thinking about it for a long time.

Ila shut the field book on the salt road, with her pencil in it.

"Price first," she said.

# Chapter 5: The Burned Hut

The smell reached Kunal before the hut did.

It was not fresh smoke. It was the wet, sour smell of a fire that had burned itself out days ago and been rained on by snow, and he knew it from a long way down the salt road. He knew it the way a man knows his own name said wrong.

"Stop," he said.

Ila stopped. The rope between their wrists went slack, eight spans of Lata's wool lying across the scree like a dropped thread. She had been counting under her breath since the second spur. Her lips were still moving.

"Four hundred and ten," she said. "You've interrupted me."

"Forgive me. There's a hut ahead that shouldn't smell like that."

She looked where he looked. The road had carried them over two bare spurs and down into a hollow under a wall of grey rock. Above the wall, a white staircase of ice and snow climbed out of sight into low cloud. The Shepherd's Stair. He had walked it as a boy with his hand in a goat's wool. Beneath it, where the road gave out, there should have been a low stone hut with a turf roof and a thread of smoke from Hari's chimney.

There was a black square in the snow.

He went down faster than the rope liked. She followed without comment, which he noted, and was grateful for, and had no room to be grateful about.

The roof had fallen in. The walls stood shoulder high, blackened, and the doorframe was a charred hoop around nothing. Snow had drifted into the room, melted on warm ash, and frozen into a grey crust. Hari's bench was there. Hari's iron pot lay on its side. Hari's goat-hair blanket was a rag stuck to the floor.

No body. He made himself look twice and saw none, and his knees nearly let go with relief.

"He's not here," he said, and heard how thin it sounded. "Hari has kept this stair thirty winters. His father kept it. He would not leave it for anything short of—" He stopped. "He's not here."

"Then he got out," said Ila, "or was taken."

"Yes."

She said it as a fact, neither kind nor unkind, and it steadied him more than comfort would have.

There were prints in the ash. Many, overlapping, pressed deep through the crust into the soft black beneath. Hobnailed boots, the sort that cost a month's grain. And half trodden into the char by the doorframe lay a small brass bell, the size of a walnut, with a loop for stitching.

He picked it up. The tongue was gone. It was a patrol bell, off a regent's grey wool shoulder. His uncle's men wore them so the whole valley would hear them coming and know whose they were.

*Pritam's still,* he thought. *He took the habit with him, and the bells.*

He closed his fist round the brass until it bit.

Hari had been burned out for a stair. Kunal knew how it would go from here. Raghu would climb the Shepherd's Stair and learn what lay at the top. After that the high hamlets would have a man at their gates, asking for what they could not give, and then asking again with a torch. The Stair was not a road. It was a door, and the people beyond it had kept it shut for three hundred years because the hill had no other wall. If a captain's pairs went through and the crown learned the way after them, Neshar would stop being a valley. It would be a road with a toll.

And he had a surveyor on his wrist. He looked at her.

Ila was not looking at the bell. She crouched at the edge of the ash with her mitten off, her bare hand flat above the prints without touching them, her lips moving again.

"Two roped pairs," she said.

He let it pass. She did not seem to need him to answer.

"The prints come in two files. Each file has a gap of seven or eight spans between the same two men. That's a rope, tied and kept slack." She pointed. "Two bound pairs. Four men. And look at the soles. The same ring of nine round nail-heads and a bar across the heel, in all four. Issue boots, cut in one store. Four men from one company, not four strangers who happened to climb together."

"Ila—"

"They're loaded." She tapped the print nearest the door. "That heel went in a thumb past the crust, and the toe is nearly flat. A man with a pack that heavy walks flat-footed. Salt weight, or iron, or rope. They stood here a while. They ate, there's grease on the stone. Then they went up." She looked at the white staircase. "The toes dig in, the stride shortens."

"How long ago?"

"The ash has crusted but not drifted. Snow's lain on the roof beams a day, not two. A day ahead, perhaps a little over."

He stood holding the bell.

She was right. He could see she was right, the way he could see a rockfall after someone had pointed to it. He had come down here braced for the shape of a disaster. He had read the ground as a wound. She had read it as a column of figures, and the figures were better.

In thirty-three winters of knowing every stone of this hill, it had not occurred to him to count the men who had burned it.

"Four," he said slowly. "Two pairs."

"Heavily loaded, a day ahead, going up." She wiped her hand on her coat. "Did you not see it?"

"I saw Hari's pot."

It was the most honest thing he had said to her all morning, and he watched her take it in. She did not use it against him. She nodded once, like a woman logging a bench mark. "Then it's as well one of us was looking at the ground."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They ate standing, because the hut had nothing to sit on. Sattu and cold salt tea from her flask. The wind came down the Stair and rattled the burned beams.

"May I ask you something?" said Ila.

"You may ask me anything. It's the answering that costs you."

"I'm aware." She wiped her mouth. "Is Captain Raghu going up to get Pritam out?"

It was the question he would have asked. His uncle sat under guard in his own tower. A man who loved him, or owed him, might well want a road to carry him over the mountain before spring.

"I don't know," said Kunal. "Freeing him may be part of it. I can't tell you it's the whole."

Her eyes dropped to his hands. They were pink. He saw her see it.

"That's true," she said. "You don't know."

"I know a good deal less than I'd like." He turned the cup in his fingers. "Raghu wants a way over the mountain for something. That much I'd stake the walnut on. What the something is..." He stopped.

He had a sentence ready, and he could feel it heavy in his mouth, and the sentence had the word *fear* in it. It was not a lie. It was a thing he was afraid of, particular and specific, and it had lived in him since the fourth light went out. Said aloud to a crown surveyor, it would be the first line of a map he had spent his life hiding. A true thing, once spoken, could not be unsaid. He had watched his uncle build a coup out of true things said at the wrong table.

"I won't say," said Kunal.

"What won't you say?"

"What I think he wants it for. Or what I'm afraid of, if you'd rather." He made himself meet her eyes. "I'm not telling you it's nothing. I'm telling you I won't answer."

The rope stayed quiet. Silence cost nothing. He had known it would, and he had still half expected the cold to come for him.

Ila took out her field book and wrote. He could see the page upside down, her small square hand, and made out two words before she turned it.

*Refused. Free.*

"You're writing it down," he said.

"I'm writing everything down. Every true answer becomes a line on my sheet. I don't see why a refusal shouldn't be a line too. A blank is data, Highness. A good surveyor learns more from the fog than from the clear."

"And what does the fog tell you?"

"That you're afraid." She closed the book. "Not of me, I think. For whoever lives above this stair. Not for a chair in the Garh."

He had braced for a guess at his ambition. He had not braced for this. It went in under the ribs, and his face gave it away before he could stop it. She saw that too. She did not write it down.

"I'd like to say I was sorry I saw it," said Ila.

"Are you?"

"I'm trying to find out," she said, which was his own sentence, and he nearly laughed, and nearly didn't.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The Stair began in a gully so steep that the first rung was a ledge of rock at his chin.

Before they started she untied his wrist and retied the rope round his chest in a loop under the arms, snug, so a fall would not wrench a hand. Then she stood beside him with the pencil behind her ear and her eyes on the ice, working out the same thing he was: how long a thing could stay frozen when a regent's men had trodden it for a day.

Shepherds had cut the lower steps over a hundred years into broad shallow treads, each the length of a boot. In summer they were wet rock. In winter they were glass with a skin of snow, and the skin had cracked.

He put his weight on the first one and felt it give like rotten bread.

"No," he said, and took it back. "Rotten. Heavy men, heavy packs, and the meltwater's gone through."

"Odds?" said Ila.

He glanced at her. She had her chin up and her rope-hand steady. It was the first time anyone had asked him for odds in the voice of someone who meant to use them.

"The first dozen are poor. Perhaps one in three that a tread shears under me. After that the ice is thicker, in the shade of the gully." He tried a second tread. It held, with a noise like a knuckle. "I'd rather it was me than you."

"Then you lead and I belay." She was already uncoiling slack. She put her back to the rock and took a turn of rope round her hip with a speed that told him she had done it on worse. "Don't pull unless you mean it."

"Yes, Surveyor."

"Don't say it like that. As if it's a joke." She said it very quietly. "I've held three men on a rope in my life and I didn't let go of any of them."

He had no reply. He turned to the ice.

The first tread shed a plate the size of a tray, which went skating down the gully behind him and out into the thin afternoon light. The second held. The third gave and held, and he took the next on his knees, spreading his weight. The rope ran easy behind him. He did not look back. He did not need to.

At the sixth tread his glove found iron. A shepherd's peg, black with rust, driven into a crack in the rock a hundred years ago by someone who had also not trusted this ice. He tapped it. It rang. He passed the rope behind it and went on, and the rope ran back through the old iron toward her hands.

At the ninth tread the ice simply let go.

There was no crack, only a lurch, and the gully wall went past his face. He fell the length of two treads. Then the rope snapped taut round the peg and came up hard under his arms and stopped him with a jerk that emptied his lungs. He hung with his boots scrabbling over nothing.

Below him, no sound. Then a scrape, a boot skidding on ice, and a grunt through her teeth.

He looked down. Ila had gone to one knee, her back against the rock and the rope bitten into her hip. The peg had caught most of him, and she had taken the rest. Her face was white with the effort. Her boots had slid a hand's width and stopped.

One breath. Two. The rope did not give an inch.

"Up," said Ila, low and level. "Find a hold. I can't do this all day."

He found one. His fingers closed on a ledge of rock, and the weight came off her slowly, and the rope went slack. When he glanced down she was flexing her hip with a look of outrage.

"That's four," she said.

"Four?"

"Men I've held." She wiped her face with the back of her mitten. "Don't make a habit of it."

He climbed on. At each good ledge he called "Holding," though he was holding perfectly well, and each time her voice came back up the gully, short and steady. "Holding." He told himself he was checking the rope. He called it again at the next ledge anyway.

By old family habit he counted as he went, because the Stair's lower treads had always been forty to the men of his house. "Thirty-eight. Thirty-nine." He hauled himself over the lip of the next ledge. Above it a short wall of dark rock stood between them and the long upper slope. "Forty. That's the foot of the Stair done. Bring the rope up."

"Forty-one," said Ila below him.

"I'm sorry?"

"There's a half-step under the first overhang, a low shelf, about a hand high. You stepped over it without weighting it. I counted it because my foot was on it." Her voice rose to him, calm as a ruled line. "Forty-one, not forty."

He looked down. She was a small figure against the grey ice with the rope bright between them, her head tilted back, wearing the expression of a woman with no intention of being wrong.

Kunal thought of the tower stair at Garh Neshar, and the half-step on its north landing that no one counted, so that his family said thirty-nine and taught the children it was the ghosts' count. *A hand high. Stepped over.*

"It's an old shelf," he said. "Nobody counts it. My grandfather said a man who counts the half-step is a man who plans to fall."

"Your grandfather wasn't belaying," said Ila. "Forty-one."

"Forty-one," said Kunal, and it came out of him like a surrender. He had spent a life letting the hill's numbers stay a little wrong, because wrong numbers were a kind of lock. He looked up at the wall, thirty feet of rock with a crack up the middle and a line of holds he had always taken on his left.

"The next pitch is yours."

Her head came up. "Mine?"

"You've the better count. And I've just shown you what I do to ice." He braced his boots and took up her rope in both hands. "Go on, Surveyor. I'll hold."

He watched her think about it, and decide it was not a trap, and that if it was the rope would tell her. Then she crossed the ledge, put her fingers into the crack, and climbed his stair.

Her rope slid through his hands like thread, a hand's width at a time, never a coil too much. Halfway up she looked down. "You can give me slack. I won't fall."

"I know," said Kunal, and paid out an inch.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The head of the Stair was a wide shelf of rock under the lip of the cloud, bare in the wind, with the ice below blue as a vein. The upper snowfield ran away white into the murk.

At the edge of the shelf stood a flat grey stone, waist-high, set upright in a socket of smaller ones. It was a route-keeper's marker, the kind that stood at the head of every hill stair. He had walked past it a hundred times.

It had writing on it.

The chalk was fresh. The wind had not yet scoured the strokes. Someone had drawn them carefully in the old hill script, slow letters with hooked tails, the hand of a man taught to write by his father with a stick in the dirt. Kunal knew the hand. He knew the pot that had held that chalk.

Ila came up beside him, breathing hard and pleased with herself. She saw his face, and stopped being pleased.

"It's in hill script," she said. "I can read the numerals. Not the rest." She held out her bare hand, and for a moment he thought she meant to take his. She pointed at the stone instead. "Read it to me."

He opened his mouth to say it was nothing. A route-keeper's warning, weather, a note to the next shepherd. He had a dozen smooth answers. His eyes went to the letters first, and his mouth followed them before he had chosen anything.

"*They ask for the princes' way,*" said Kunal.

The words went out over the shelf and across the ice, and he could not take one of them back.

The wind dropped. In the stillness the stones under their boots gave a low hum that sank half a note, like a bowl's song when a wet finger leaves the rim, and Ila's hand had gone quite still in the air beside his sleeve.

"Hari wrote that," he said. "Before they burned him out. Or while they did."

"Who are *the princes*?" said Ila.

Kunal said nothing. For a moment he could not have said whether it was refusal or whether he simply had no voice. There was a chalk line under the sentence, a short flat stroke, and beneath it a second line begun and abandoned, a single hooked letter, and a smear where a hand had dragged through it.

# Chapter 6: Whiteout on the Stair

The cloud dropped while Ila was still reading the last cairn.

One moment it was a grey heap of stones on the white, three hundred paces off. The next it was a smudge. Then it was gone, and the plateau had no edges, and the only straight line left in the world was the rope on her wrist.

"Stop," she said.

Kunal stopped. He stood two spans ahead, a dark shape with his collar up. Past him the white went on in every direction, the same white, with no slope in it and no sky.

"How far to the next cairn?"

"Four hundred paces. Perhaps five."

"Which way?"

He turned his head left, then right. Her mouth went dry.

"Straight on," he said. "I think."

"You think."

"I've walked this a hundred times, in summer, behind ewes." He wiped snow from his lashes. "The cairns are old, Ila. The jogini light them for the bound, and only a little, and they're tired. They'll show at dusk if they show at all."

It was close to dusk. The white had started to thicken to grey at its edges.

She took the compass from inside her coat. Brass, palm-wide, the lid dented where she had dropped it on a Taravan stair at nineteen. The needle swung and settled and swung again. There was iron in the rock under the snow. She held the case flat against her ribs until it quit.

"North-east by north," she said. "I've paced nine hundred and twelve since the head of the Stair. Your straight on bends a hand to the left of my needle."

"It's a song, not an instrument."

"There's a lip on the west side of this plateau. I saw it from the Stair." She put the compass away. "If we drift toward it, how far off are we?"

"Far."

"How far is far?"

He didn't answer, and she let it lie. Free, she thought. Not kind.

"Odds," said Kunal. It was her own trick handed back in her own level voice. "Yours first."

"One in three we miss the shelter and spend the night in the open. One in ten we find the lip." She flexed her cold fingers. "I don't like either number."

"Nor I," said Kunal. "Walk behind me. Count loudly."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She counted. He sang.

It was barely a song. It was a thread of sound under his breath, four lines that went round and round, and it clashed with her numbers the way two clocks clash in a small room.

*Keep the Crow's Wing on your cheek,*
*keep the cold hollow underfoot.*
*Past the stone that split in two,*
*three hundred to where the ewe lay down.*

The fourth time round, she stopped counting.

"Three hundred what?"

His voice broke off. "Sorry?"

"The last line. Three hundred paces, I assume. Yours or mine? Yours are longer by two fingers, I've measured."

He turned. In the grey she could not read his face, but his shoulders went very still. "You weren't meant to catch that."

"You sing it like a lullaby. I count." She pushed on, because standing felt like falling. "The Crow's Wing is a ridge. It's on the left, if it's on your cheek, and I'd wager it's the one we saw from the Stair before the cloud. The cold hollow is a dip the wind has scoured. The split stone is a boulder. And where the ewe lay down, that isn't a place, is it? It's an instruction."

"It's a place," said Kunal. "A half-ring of wall, waist high, where ewes go to lamb out of the wind. It's three hundred paces past the boulder. You'd find it in the dark if you had the song."

"So every name in it is a direction."

He was quiet for a moment. Snow ticked on his shoulders.

"In Neshar," he said, "all of them are."

The size of it settled on her. Two winters of crown surveyors had ridden these valleys asking shepherds what each hill was called. They had been given good, honest answers, and had written them down as names. No one had told them the names were the road. A hundred years of children had learned the whole high country as nonsense, and there was not a sheet of paper in it to steal.

And she had walked in with a pencil.

"That's why you won't say them," she said.

"That's some of why."

It was not much of an answer, but she wrote it down in her head as a true one. The rope had stayed quiet.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cloud tore open like a slit sack.

Black rock stood out of the white, a long crag on the left, no more than half a mile off, with four rounded humps along its top like a fist laid on a table. The light lay on it for a count of maybe ten. Ila had the compass out before she knew she had moved. Ninety-four degrees. She said the number aloud and the distance after it, and Kunal repeated them back to her without being asked.

"Knuckle," she said. "It's got a face. A blunt, bossy, entirely unimpressed face." She shut the compass. "Nimmi's Knuckle."

Kunal made a sound that was nearly a laugh. "It has a name."

"Then tell me it."

"No."

She waited for the rest. It did not come. "That's a refusal," she said.

"It's a refusal." He kept his eyes on the crag as the white closed over it. "A name is a way. If I say it and you write it, anyone who can read your sheet can find that crag. And anyone who knows the song can find where you were standing when you looked at it."

"You think I'd hand it to Raghu?"

"I think paper goes where paper goes." He said it without heat. "I'd rather you called it something rude and kept the true one out of ink."

"It isn't rude. It's a compliment. Nimmi would take it as one."

"Nimmi would pretend she hadn't heard it, and then be insufferable for a week."

She laughed then, loud and sudden, and the cloud swallowed the sound. He flinched and turned, and his mouth twitched before he put it right.

"I named a ridge Tamar's Shoulder once," said Ila, still grinning. "After a cook who fed me when I was seven. It's on my summer sheets."

"Of course it is," said Kunal, and walked on with his shoulders a little looser. She filed that where she kept the stair counts and the wall.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They found the place where the split boulder should have been, and there was no boulder.

Kunal stopped at the edge of a long smooth hump in the snow and stood looking at it for a count of twenty. She studied the back of his neck. She had learned to read it as she read ground: tight, then tighter.

"It stood here," he said. "I've sat on it. The whole of the cut, both halves." He put his boot on the hump and it held, solid as a bank. "The drift's filled it. A winter's snow, off the Crow's Wing, in one night."

"So you've nothing to count from."

"Nothing." He said it quite steadily. "Three hundred past a thing I can't see. If we walk the wrong line from the wrong place, we come out on the lip."
