"Thirty-one," said Ila. "Don't make it rhyme with anything. Thirty-two."

"It's a route-song. It has to rhyme or nobody remembers."

"Thirty-three."

The ice leaned. She leaned with it, and the song went round again, and she counted over it. At forty she put her left boot on a glaze she did not trust, and it held, and she let a long breath go through her teeth. At fifty-one the rope hummed against her wrist with the strain of him holding. At fifty-seven her boot struck flat stone and the world stopped sloping.

"Fifty-seven on the pitch," she called. "Ledge. Come down."

She had just time to stand with her back to the rock and her heart going before the line slackened and he began to descend. He came down much faster than she had. He had done it before, she thought, as a boy, with soldiers or without them.

The song was still in her head. *The widow was carried down.*

She turned the word over.

"Carried," said Ila, when he reached the ledge.

"Mm?"

"In the song. What does it mean?"

"What it says." He shook the rope loose from his hip. "She had a bad hip and a sister who walked her. She was carried down the way a child is. By the hand, over the bad places. My grandmother sang it that way. In the hamlets, to carry is to see someone across."

"In the chronicles it means something else."

He looked up.

"I've read every chronicle of the passes the survey office holds." She said it flatly, because that was how it went. "*Carried* is a word for the dead. A man is *carried down at the thaw.* It means they found him and brought him in. It's the only way the clerks use it."

"Same word," said Kunal slowly.

"Same word, two valleys."

"One of us has the wrong end of it."

"Perhaps neither." She was looking at the ice, at the clean green edge of it where the sun came through. Her grandfather had been carried down. His entry said so, in the Shilak chronicle's copperplate. She had read it years ago, over her father's shoulder, and thought it only a word.

She put it away, carefully, with the number sixty. The pile was growing. It was not a thing she could set a bearing on.

"Last pitch," she said. "Keep singing."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They reached the foot at dusk, with the cloud tearing off the slope in strips and the last light lying copper on the far rim of the Hollow Moon. There was a niche behind a boulder, lined with flat stones, where some family had sheltered often enough to build a hearth. Under the lowest slab lay a cache of dung cakes, dry as bread. The mountain's one custom, thought Ila, and the only one anyone ever kept.

They made a small fire. They ate, and said little, and he went out to look at the sky.

She sat with the field sheet on her knee.

It was the fair sheet, the one she would carry home. Not the notebook, which was her own, but the paper she had drawn on since Garh Neshar, and which she had sworn at nineteen would show the crown every way. She drew the day on it with the ruling pen. The lip of the Hollow Moon. The Two Aunts, in his words. The left fork, the cairn where she'd sat, the bearing she had taken of a long grey slope she had been steered away from. The rim above the fall.

She drew the rim, and the shape of the ice, and the line of rock. She stopped with the pen over the slot.

It would be a short line. A body wide. She had the count, a hundred and sixty steps all told, fifty-seven of them on the hard pitch, and she knew the slant and the three ledges, or four. She could ink the whole of it in the time it took the fire to settle. It was true. It was a thing she knew.

*If I drew less than I found I'd be lying on paper.*

She had said it herself, in a cave, and meant it.

She lifted the pen. The ink dried on the nib. She drew the rim of the fall as it looked from above, grey, unbroken, with nothing behind it, and went on to the next feature.

The east bearing had been different. That gap had come while she was following him in the dark with cold hands, and she had let it happen. This one she had chosen with the pen above the paper, and she meant to say so aloud.

A sheet that was true and incomplete. She knew the phrase. She'd have called it a dodge in anyone else.

Kunal came back in, ducking under the stone. The cold came in with him. He sat across the fire from her and held out his hands to it, and said nothing, and did not look at the sheet.

"I didn't draw it," said Ila.

He went still.

"The Ladder. The slot. The steps. I didn't draw it." She held out her right hand, palm up, over the fire, so the light fell on it. "I wanted you to hear it from me and not find it out by a gap."

He looked at her hand for a long time. It was pink and ordinary. The nails were clean. The fire lay along the lines of the palm.

"Warm," said Kunal.

"Warm."

"You told me in the cave you would."

"I told you in the cave I would draw a way if I found one. I found one." She put the hand down. "I've left it off. I don't know yet if that's a lie, but it isn't one that costs anything. I've checked."

"Why?"

She had thought about that on the hard pitch, with fifty-seven steps going by. The reason would not sound as good aloud as it had in her head.

"Because I sat at Gauri's fire at Ewe's Rest," said Ila. "Because Mini paced her wall for me. Because a girl of six has a place to go when soldiers come, and I would be the one who put it in the crown's hand." She stopped. "It isn't a principle. It's just that I know their names."

"That's quite a lot," said Kunal.

"I didn't say it was small."

He smiled, a small, crooked thing, gone as soon as it came. Then he was quiet. "You know what it costs you. The oath. The sheet is audited at Taravan every spring. If an auditor finds a way you walked and didn't draw—"

"Then I'm a surveyor who forgot a way."

"You, forgot."

"I'd have to say it was an error," said Ila, "and it wouldn't be. That's the part I haven't solved. I'll have to find the sentence that's true, and I haven't." Her voice went thin. "Chief Surveyor is a name on a sheet. I've worked eleven winters. I could lose it over a staircase."

"Then take it back." He was turning the loose end of the rope in his fingers, slowly, as he spoke. "Draw it. I'll not ask you to carry my people's ways at the cost of your own."

His thumb worked the wool until the strands creaked. His eyes stayed on the fire and never once came to the sheet.

"I won't," said Ila.

"Ila."

"I've decided." It came out sharper than she'd meant. She made herself breathe. "I've said I'd tell you before I drew a way, and I'm telling you. I didn't draw it. That's all. Let me have the whole of it before you say anything kind."

Kunal let the rope go. He laid it down between them in the dust and sat with his hands open to the fire. When he spoke it was low, and the courtesy was gone from it.

"Promise me you never will."

She looked at the rope.

It was a fair request, the only one he could have made. His bare hands lay open and steady on his knees, as if he had wanted very much not to make it.

"I can't," said Ila.

His face stayed as it was, but the fire in his eyes stopped moving.

"A promise made on this rope, if I broke it, strikes me as a great lie," she said. "You know that. I'd lose the hand, or worse. And I'd be promising against my own oath. The crown commissioned me to draw every way I find, and I swore it. If the crown asks for the sheet, the oath says draw. I won't promise to break that before I know what I'd do."

"You've just not drawn it."

"That's one staircase, on one evening. A promise is for all of them." She heard how reasonable it sounded. She hated it. "I won't make you a promise I might not keep. It's the one thing I can give you that isn't free to lie about."

He took that in.

"And besides," said Ila, "it isn't properly measured yet."

The words lay in the firelight. The rope did not hum. It stayed quiet and level as it always did for a true thing. She had taken the bearing of the head of the Ladder only by eye, and the whole slant wanted a second reading, and she did not yet know the length of it to within a span.

And she had said it so that he would hear a way out. A *not yet.* An *it may be.*

He heard both. She watched him do it. His eyes went to her hands, and they were warm, and his mouth moved a little at the corner, the way it did when a sum came out and he did not like the answer.

"That's true," said Kunal.

"Yes."

"And it's a dodge."

"Yes."

"You might have said so."

"You'd have heard it anyway."

"I did." He let out a breath that fogged and thinned. "A map can be true and still leave a thing out. You'll tell me that."

"I'll tell you a blank isn't false."

"No," said Kunal. "A blank isn't false. It's only a decision."

She opened her mouth to answer and found there was nothing in it. It was the sentence she'd have used on any man who tried it on her, and he had used it gently, as one hands back a dropped pencil.

The ink went stiff on the pen. She let his sentence stand.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He rose a little later and went out to the edge of the shelf. After a time she put the sheet aside and followed, with the rope paying out between them, and stopped at his shoulder.

The cloud had gone. Stars lay thick and hard in a black sky, and the cold came up off the ground as it does after a storm, with a clean smell like iron and snow.

"The fall has a name," said Kunal, without turning. "The families don't give it to people from below. They call it the Long Braid."

She stood very still.

Every night since Ewe's Rest he had set a question beside a name. Tonight he set none. It was a name, held out, with nothing on the other side of the scale.

"That's your name for tonight," said Ila.

"It's just a name." He was still looking out. "I'd have given it you anyway."

"It'll go on a line."

"I know what happens to a true answer on this rope." His voice was almost gentle. "I've watched you. You write what's true, even when it's me." He looked at her then. "You may write it."

She took out the notebook, the one that was her own. She wrote it by starlight, exactly. *Long Braid.* The pencil scratched. Her hand was steady, and it was warm.

When she looked up, he had turned back to the edge.

"There," said Kunal.

Below the shelf the ground fell away into a vast pale floor, ringed by black mountains. Three long shapes lay on it, one after another, lighter than the snow round them, as if someone had set down three mirrors and walked off. Starlight lay in each, flat and perfect. Far out, from somewhere in the middle one, came a sound like a great drum struck once, deep and slow. It rolled along the basin and came back off the cliffs.

"The Glass Lakes," said Kunal.

He didn't give her a bearing. He stood with his hands bare in the cold and let her look.

Her left hand went to her coat, to the lining over her ribs, and lay flat on the paper sewn there.

*Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes.*

She was not standing at them yet. They were a night's walk off and a long way down. But she could see them, and she was looking along their length to the far head of the basin when a light came on there.

It was a pinprick, orange, low on a shoulder of black rock above the last lake. It steadied, and did not flicker like a hearth seen through a door. It burned like a fire set by men who did not mind being seen from a long way off.

"Kunal."

He had already found it. His bare hand closed on the rope.

"Nobody keeps a fire there," he said. "There's no hut. No fold. There's nothing up there but the view."

Ila thought of two loaded pairs on the north-east fork, a day ahead of them, and of a staircase she had just taken off her map. It was the only way down that no one in Taravan could name, and it came out at the foot of that shoulder.

The ice boomed again in the dark, and she did not take her hand from the letter.

# Chapter 13: The Ringing Stone

They came down onto the basin floor at first light, after a night of walking by blue cairn-light with the rope between them. Kunal knew the storm would reach the water before they did.

He saw it as the last scree gave out underfoot. A long grey wall rolled down the far peaks like a lid sliding shut. Under it the three lakes lay pale and flat and shining. They would not shine long.

"Three hours," said Ila.

"Four. Perhaps five."

"Three." She had her watch open on her palm and her eyes on a spur across the basin. "Its shadow crossed that spur between my first count and my second. That's not a cloud drifting. It's a cloud arriving." She snapped the watch shut. "Walk."

They walked.

The floor was scree and old drift, hard under the crust. The rope ran eight spans of pale wool, taut and then slack and then taut again as their strides found each other. The wind was at their backs, which he disliked. A wind that pushes you is a wind that wants you somewhere.

Ila never glanced at the sky again. She worked. He had watched her for six days now and it had not once grown ordinary. She took a bearing off a crag without breaking stride, her elbow tucked, her lips moving on the count. When her pencil lead snapped she held out her left hand, palm up, and kept her head forward. He put a fresh one in it, warm from inside his coat. She wrote. She held out the hand again.

"The shore cairn," he said, to keep his voice steady. "It's close. A mile along, no more. We'll shelter there if the front comes early."

"A mile."

"About."

"Kunal." He heard the delight start in her voice before she turned. "I paced a baseline at the foot of the Ladder and took two bearings on that cairn off the Raven Spur. I've carried the figure down the basin all night. From where we stand, right now, it is nine hundred and ten paces, give or take a dozen. A third of a mile, with a following wind."

"It looks a mile."

"It looks a mile because you hill people measure distance by how tired you'll be." She was grinning. "I have never once been wrong about a pace."

"You were wrong about the Ladder's third ledge."

"I was told there were three. Possibly four. I was misled."

"Fair."

Then she laughed. She did it the way she always did, with no warning, her head going back and the sound going out across the basin so loud that two ravens broke off the scree and flapped away. It rang on the cliffs. It came back thin.

He had been set down in front of that laugh perhaps six times now, and each time he had thought he was ready. He was not. His boot went wrong on a loose slab and he caught himself on the rope, his count lost entirely. He had spent years teaching himself not to want a thing where others could see him wanting it. Now he stood on a frozen shore, caught wanting this, openly, to be proved wrong by her again.

"Walk," said Ila, still smiling, and he did.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cairn stood where she had said it would, within a dozen paces of her figure.

It was a jogini cairn, old, chest-high, built of flat grey slabs the lake wind had polished. It stood on a shelf above the shore where the scree gave out. The storm had pulled the morning down to dusk, and the blue light in the stones showed even by day. When they came up under it the wind went through the gaps and made its low note, level as a bowl rim before a finger touches it.

The Mirror lay below them. It was the first lake and the largest, a flat white plate a mile across with no mark on it. Beyond it the second and third were smaller shapes, sunk in shadow. Kunal had never come this way in full light. He had come at night, by the ridge, twice, and had not looked down.

Ila stood on the lip of the shelf to take her bearing of the far shore. Her left hand went flat against her coat, over the lining at her ribs, and stayed there. It stayed through the sighting. It stayed while her right hand wrote, and she held the book against her hip and wrote one-handed, rather than take it away. She gave no reason. He had seen her do it once the night before, at the edge of the shelf above the lakes, and had not asked then either. Whatever lay under that wool, she kept her palm on it the way a person guards a sleeping child.

Behind her, the storm stood nearer than she had put it. The grey wall had crossed the second spur since the scree, and its lit edge had gone the colour of old pewter. Frost had caught in the loose hair at her temple, and she had not noticed.

"It's gained," she said. "Faster than I put it. Twenty minutes to be on the ice before it. After that, none."

"Then we have twenty minutes."

"That's what I said."

"I know." He was standing too close, and he knew it, and he did not step back. "You make a deadline sound like a dare."

She turned.

She had not meant to, he thought. She had turned to say something sharp, and then the sharp thing was not there. They stood a pace apart under the stones with the wind going through them. Her face was bare and wind-scoured, her lashes white. Her eyes moved, once, from his eyes to his mouth. He saw her see him see it.

Neither of them stepped away.

"You've been watching me since the scree," said Ila. Her voice was lower than he had heard it.

"I've been watching you work."

"Is there a difference?"

*Is there?* The words were already in his mouth. It was his oldest habit, a question handed back like a coin. But she had spent six days learning his habits. She would count it a refusal, and she would be right. He opened his mouth to say *yes*, which was true.

She spoke first. She glanced down at the field book in her fist and up again. "You've watched me draw all winter. Does it matter to you what goes on it?"

It was the question the whole rope had been walking toward. Thirty years of dinners answered for him. The smooth, warm reply that soothes a guest and costs the host nothing came out before he had weighed a word of it.

"It doesn't matter to me what you d—"

The stones rang.

It was not loud. The low note in the cairn lifted, the way a bowl's song climbs when a wet finger presses harder on the rim. It went up past the wind. The rope gave a hard cold pull at his wrist, and his fingertips went numb in his glove.

He stopped. His mouth shut on its own.

Ila had heard it. She went very still, her head a little to one side, the way she listened to ice.

"Finish it," she said.

"No."

"Finish the sentence, Kunal."

"I won't."

"Why not?" Her voice was perfectly even. "If it's true, it costs nothing. If it isn't, you've lost a few fingertips and I've lost an afternoon. Say it."

He looked at her, and at the stones behind her. He thought of his uncle, who had taught him the autumn he was nine, over supper, in the pleasant voice he used for terrible things. *Whoever knows the way owns the people, Kunal. Remember it. Everything else is manners.* It had sat in his mouth ever since, a stone he could neither swallow nor spit. And what had just left his mouth was manners, the cheapest kind, pressed on the one person who had asked him a simple thing straight. If he said what was true now, she would hold it in her hand, written down, with his name beside it.

But she had refused him a promise by the fire at the Ladder's foot because she would not give one she might break. She had still left the staircase off her sheet and told him why. She had paid for his people's ways with her own good name. She was owed something.

"It matters more than anything," said Kunal.

The stones dropped. The note slid down through the wind and settled, low and level, as it had been when they arrived.

"Your hand," said Ila.

He pulled the glove. Five nails the colour of candle wax, the white climbing a knuckle's depth. The stones had gone quiet, but the cost had not. A lie once begun did not unbegin because a true word came after it.

"An hour," he said. "It's small."

"I can see how small." She did not look away from the hand. "More than anything?"

"More than my seat on the council. More than my uncle's head on a plate. More than Neshar, on some mornings, and I'm not proud of that." It came out of him in a rush, rough. "The first thing was manners. It was out before I knew it, the way I've answered every question since I was nine. The mountain weighed it for me. So you'll have to have the truth."

"I can have it?"

"You've had it all winter. You'll do what you'll do." His throat hurt. "You'd write it down if I asked you not to."

"Yes."

"Then write it."

She did not reach for the notebook. She lifted her bare hand, slowly, as if there were a long way to bring it, and laid two fingers against the front of his coat. They rested over his heart for the length of one breath. They were warm.

"I'm not writing that one," said Ila.

It was not a kiss, and it left him weaker than any kiss would have.

The wind changed on the stones. It went from a hum to a groan.

"Kunal," said Ila.

"I know."

"The front."

He looked past her.

The grey wall had stopped being a wall. It had become the whole north, and it came on faster than any storm he had seen. Its edge was a white line eating the far shore, and under the line the lakes were going dark.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They ran the last of the shore with the snow coming in sideways. It was not yet a storm. It was a warning of one, hard small pellets that stung the face and hissed on the crust. By the time they came down to the lip of the Mirror, he could not see the third lake at all.

The cairn-light stood on the ice. He had expected it. Faint blue shapes ran out from the shore in a line, lamp to lamp, dead straight across the plate toward the far side. The winter line. Two bound people could walk it to the other bank.

Ila stopped at the edge and went down on one knee.

"Wait," she said. "I want to sound it."

"There's no time."

"Ten minutes. I'll tap the ice with the haft every ten paces out and listen." She had the axe off her pack already. "Clear lake ice rings. Rotten ice goes dull. If there's a pressure line, a spring, a seam, I'll hear it. I won't walk onto a plate I haven't read."

"The line is lit."

"The line is lit for people. It isn't lit for the weather."

"Ila." He took her arm, his bare hand on her sleeve, the nails still white. She looked at the hand, and then up at him, snow packed on her eyebrows, the axe haft in her fist and the hunger to be exact all over her face. He wanted to give her the ten minutes. He wanted to stand on that shore and let her read the whole lake like a page. "Look behind you."

She looked.

The north had gone out. There was no far shore any more, no cliffs, only a brown-white wall a few hundred paces off, and the sound that came out of it was long and flat and full of grit. Three minutes, he thought. Perhaps two.

"If we're on the shore when that hits," said Kunal, "we'll be in the open with no cave and no wall and the whole sky coming down on us. If we're on the line, we'll be moving. We'll be across in a quarter hour."

"On ice I haven't sounded."

"On the one road the mountain lit for us."

He watched her weigh it. It took her about as long as a breath. She had been misled about a Ladder and could not stand to be wrong about a lake, and he saw her hate the answer and take it.

"Then I tap as we go," said Ila. "Every step. You don't talk."

"I won't."

"You walk where I walk. You walk on my left foot's track and nowhere else."

"Yes."

"And if I say stop—"

"I stop."

She nodded once and stood. She stepped onto the Mirror with the axe haft low in her right hand, and the wood went tick on the ice, tick, and the sound ran out ahead of her, bright and clean.

He pulled the glove back on over the white nails and went after her.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind took them at the third lamp. It came on all at once, a hand between the shoulders and a roar in both ears, and the world went to white and rope. He could see her back, a dark shape, three paces off. He could see the faint blue gleam on the ice at his feet. He could see nothing else.

Tick, went the axe, and tick. He put his boots where hers had been. His fingertips lay cold and dead inside the wool, and would for the best part of the hour. He thought of her two fingers laid against his coat, and the warmth of them. He had never been so glad of a wind in his life, because it let him pretend the shake in his breath was the cold.

*It matters more than anything.*

He had said it aloud, to a surveyor, on open rock, and the stones had sung it low and true. It could not be called back. Anyone who could read could take a bearing off that sentence and find where he was soft. A gift, or a handle? He did not know. He had set a truth in her hand hilt first, so he counted lamps instead, blue and dim, one and the next, and kept his boots in her track.

By his count they had gone halfway, perhaps a little more. By hers, he was sure, it would be exact.

The wind dropped for a breath. In the lull he heard the ice.

It was a low groan first, far down, as if the lake were turning over in its sleep. It rose through the soles of his feet and the bones of his legs. Then the whole white plate under them split with a crack like green timber under the axe, a seam running away into the snow on both sides at once, and the sound rolled out to every shore and came back doubled.

Ila stopped dead.

She did not turn. She did not look at him. She stood with the axe haft lifted off the ice, her head tipped, her whole body gone to listening, from her boots to the snow on her hood.

"Don't move," she said.

# Chapter 14: Orders

Ila stood still. Behind her the rope went slack and stayed slack, which told her Kunal had stopped dead on her left foot's track, as promised.

The boom rolled away to the shores and came back doubled. Then she heard the other sound under it.

It was a thin, rising crackle, like a long strip of cloth being torn very slowly. It came from ahead and a little left, and it ran. She turned her head to follow it. It slid across the plate in front of them from left to right, and it did not fade the way an echo fades. It crossed the winter line a dozen paces on.

"Pressure line," said Ila.

"What?"

"The ice is pushing against itself. The cold has locked the plate and the wind is working it, so it cracks along a seam." She was whispering. "The boom is the lake letting go. That's what we heard. The crackle is where."

"The lamps run straight through it."

"I know."

The lit line was a good road. It had been laid for the mountain's purposes, which were to keep two bound people off the cliffs and out of the whiteout. The mountain had never promised them dry feet. She tapped the haft against the ice, and it rang clear. She took two steps forward and tapped again, and the note went flat and dull, like a spoon on a sack.

"Stay here," she said, and went on to the end of her track with the rope paying out behind her. Tick. Dull. Tick. Dull. At four paces the dullness lifted, and the note went bright again, and she was a long way past what she'd have called safe. Slush and old fracture ran under there. She could hear it in the wood.

She worked it as she would have worked a river ford. The seam was a ridge of broken ice that had heaved and frozen again. It was thin at its crown, where the lamps crossed it. The plate was thicker to either side, because the heave had stacked the old floes there. A thick edge, then. Somewhere along it the sound would turn clean.

She went left. Forty paces, tapping. The wind tore at her hood. Behind her the rope hummed along the ice as Kunal walked her track, and she never looked round, because she did not need to. He put his boots exactly where she had said. It was a very odd thing to be trusted so entirely.

"Here," said Ila.

The note rang. She tapped again a pace to the right, and a pace further, and each answer came back bright and long, the sound of ice you could build a house on. She laid the haft down flat across the line of the crack, and it spanned the seam with a hand's breadth to spare on either side.

"Narrow," said Kunal.

"A step and a half, and a ridge in the middle. Put your feet where mine go. Don't look down. There's nothing to see, only a bit of grey."

"I'm not looking down."

"You're looking at me."

"Yes."

She went over. The ridge under her boots groaned and settled, and the far side took her weight with a sound like a door closing. She turned, and he was a dark shape in the white, with his hand out for the rope. He crossed in one long stride, his boots exactly in her marks. On the other side he stood a moment with his chest heaving.

"The lamps are over there," he said. "Forty paces."

"We'll go back to them past the seam. We've no use for them where they cross it."

"You tapped the whole way."

"I told you I would."

He laughed. It was short and surprised, and the wind took it. Then the storm came down in earnest, and there was nothing to do but walk.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They crossed the rest of the Mirror in a white roar, with the cairn-lights guttering blue at their feet. When the far shore came up, it was a darker grey in the grey, a slope of scree with a black bulk above it. Kunal said something she could not hear. He took her elbow and turned her left, and a few hundred paces on a wall stood in the snow, low, square, and roofed with slabs.

"Dhanu's," he shouted.

It was a stone hut with a door of split deodar and a smoke hole in the roof. Inside, it was black and bitterly still, and the silence rang after the wind. Kunal found a flint at the back of a shelf and struck it. The first spark showed her a chulha built of river stones, its mouth scraped clean, and beside it a pile of stunted juniper and dung cakes laid up to the rafters. There was one sleeping shelf, a plank wide enough for two if they were careful, with a folded blanket on it. A line of jars stood above the hearth. She read them by the lamp as it caught: barley meal, salt, dried apricots, a wrapped brick of tea.

"Dhanu stocked it," said Kunal. "He came the short way, with Gauri and the child. He'd want the pot used."

"He's thought of everything."

"He's thought of what he'd want, which is the same." He was already kneeling at the hearth, laying kindling with those scraped, careful hands. He flexed his fingers. The wax was gone from the nails. It was an hour and more since the stones had rung for him.

Ila stood in the door and let the storm go on without her.

The lakes were out there. She could not see them. She could hear the third one, far off, booming through the snow, a deep note that shook the doorframe in her hand. Three frozen tarns. She had measured the first by pacing and two bearings off the Raven Spur, and now she stood in the middle of them, exactly where the letter said.

*Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes. Not before. S.*

Her hand had gone to her ribs without asking. The paper was a flat dry square against her side, its edges plain through the wool. She had carried it for a week. She knew every thread of the stitching that held it.

It was a queen's letter, and the queen had named this place. She had not named it to Kunal, or to the council, or in the commission Ila had signed. She had named it only to her, in a fold of paper, with a thread of blood in the wax.

Open it. She was standing at the Glass Lakes. The condition had been met.

And she knew, with a clean cold certainty that she did not like, that the first thing the letter would do was tell her what she had been sent to find. Then she could not un-know it. Then it would be a line on a sheet before her pencil moved.

Kunal had walked her down the Stair and the Ladder and the Hollow Moon, one way at a time. He had given her names and bearings and a staircase of ice she had not drawn. He had said *it matters more than anything* with his fingertips going white. Where did his road go next? She wanted to see it. She wanted to walk it with her own eyes before the queen's hand came between them.

It was not a good reason, and she knew it. She shut the door on the wind.

"Soup," said Kunal. "If you'll hold the pot."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They ate in the red light of the chulha, sitting on the floor with their backs against the shelf. The barley was thin and salty, and she thought it the best thing she had ever put in her mouth. Kunal had spread the wet wool to steam. His hair was dark with snow-melt, and he had a smear of ash on his cheekbone that she did not tell him about.

He gave her the night's name first. That was the order of it. He gave it with his eyes on the fire.

"The seam you stopped for. The families call it the Sleeper's Spine. When it cracks, they say the sleeper's turning over. They don't cross it with a laden mule." He glanced at her. "You'd have known it for what it was. Nobody told me you read ice."

"Nobody told me you'd walk on it with your eyes shut."

"I had them open."

"You said you weren't looking down."

"I wasn't." His mouth moved. "I'd have told you if I was frightened. I'm not so good at lying. I've learned that this week."

She wrote it down. *Sleeper's Spine.* The pencil lead was one of his, still warm from his coat, and the line came out clear and black. Her hand was steady and pink in the firelight, and she looked at it for a moment, and then at the page.

"Your question," said Ila.

It was the rule. One name from him, one answer from her, for anything she knew, or a refusal that cost nothing. She had never once refused a night question. Nothing he had asked had been worth the price of the silence.

He turned the bowl in his hands, eyes on the fire. She saw him decide.

"Do your orders name these lakes?"

It came very evenly, and she heard everything under it.

She had known he would ask, had known it since the Ladder, if she was honest, and in the cave before that. It was the question his whole winter had been walking toward, and it was a fair one. He had given her the Glass Lakes freely. She had done nothing to earn it except leave a staircase off her sheet. He wanted to know what he had bought.

The letter named them. *Open when you stand at the Glass Lakes.* The words were in a woman's hand, folded against her ribs. She had only to say so.

And he would stop.

She could see it clearly, as she might have seen a road in a survey. She would say *yes*, and his face would close. He would be courteous. He would be so courteous. He had said he'd not ask her to carry his people's ways at the cost of her own, and he meant it, and that was why he would turn them round. He would take her back across the Mirror to Ewe's Rest in the morning, and the lakes would be a sound she'd heard once through a storm. And the letter would stay sealed in her coat, a thing she carried and could not read.

She tried the narrow truth first, because it was hers to try. "My commission names every—"

"Not your commission." He said it gently, and looked up. "Anything you carry."

So he had read her. Two weeks of her careful sums had taught him where she kept the small exact true things, and he had shut that door before she reached it. That left a refusal, and a refusal from her on this was an answer. He would read it, because he was Kunal.

That left one word. Her hands would show it before he did, she knew that. She weighed it, quick and cold. Pattu sleeves, a bowl to hold, the fire at her back. She could keep them hidden for an hour, and an hour was all the lie would need.

"No," said Ila.

It came out level. It was a small word. She had said it a hundred times in her life, to clerks and cooks and men who wanted her pencil.

For a moment nothing happened at all.

Kunal's shoulders came down. She watched them do it. He let out a long breath through his teeth and shut his eyes, and he laughed, softly, as at a joke on himself.

"Good," he said. "I'd hoped."

"You'd hoped?"

"All winter. I'd stopped believing it." He opened his eyes, bright and a little wet in the fire. "I thought, however I led you here, I'd be leading you with a knife at my back. Your orders were the first thing I asked you. They're the last thing I've needed to hear. And you said no." He shook his head. "It's very small to be so large."

She said, "Yes."

That was true. It was the only true thing she had said in a minute, and she held to it.

He reached over and took the empty bowl from her lap, gently, as he'd have taken a dropped pencil, and set it by the hearth. His fingers brushed her wrist. They were warm. She wanted to say something, and her mouth would not make the shape.

Then the cold came.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The cold began in her fingertips, as it always began. She had read about it. In the hall at Garh Neshar, Meera, who had lost two fingers to one lie, had told it flatly: the hands first, then the feet and ears. Ila had thought she understood. She had measured it as a quantity, a size of lie against a size of loss.

Under the nails it came like a needle drawn through, and then like a hundred. Her right hand rested on her knee, and it looked the same. She flexed it, and it did not feel like hers.

This was not the cold of the hut. The chulha was roaring now, and the stones were heating, and her cheeks stung with warmth. This cold sat inside her fingers like a thing set there. It ate its way up from the tips. Her ears began to burn, and then to prickle, and then, worse, to go quiet, and she put a hand up and found the rim of one numb as wax.

*Middling*, she thought. It was a clerk's word. A middling lie. Frostnip, and a night of it. She had seen her own face in the rule and had not liked the mirror.

She shifted closer to the fire. It did nothing. She held both hands out to the coals until her knuckles ached with the heat, and the cold stayed, bright and exact, under the skin. A shudder took her at the shoulders. She clenched her teeth to hold it, and it went down her back instead.

"Ila."

"I'm all right."

"You're shaking."

"It's the storm. I've been wet since the scree." Her teeth clicked on the words. "I'm cold, Kunal. That's all. The fire hasn't got into the wool yet."

The cold answered before he did. It slid up her fingers like water up a wick, past the first knuckle, and stopped there with a hard small ache. She had paid for that, and she knew exactly what coin. She had bought nothing with it. She had only told another lie on top of the first and made it larger.

*A map is true or false*, she had told her father and the clerks at nineteen and at thirty, and once, across a fire, him. Here was a line she had put on a sheet. It was not a mistake. She had done it with her eyes open, and she could not rub it out.

She pulled the pattu round her shoulders and held it shut with both fists. Her knuckles had gone from pink to a dead cream. She tucked the fists in against her ribs, where the letter lay, and bent over them.

Kunal had gone very quiet.

He sat with the iron pot in his hands, still half full, the last of the soup he'd put by for the morning. He had been about to set it on the hearth. Now he did not move. She heard the fire ticking in the dung cakes, and the third lake booming far away, and her own breath, ragged and loud.

"Ila," he said again.

"It's nothing."

The white climbed again, a slow cold step, to the second knuckle. She bit down on a sound and did not let it out.

"Show me your hands."

"I said—"

"You said you were cold." He said it gently. "Then it'll cost you nothing to show me."

She could have refused. It was free. She knew what the refusal would say, and she was too tired and too shaken to build the sentence that would make it say something else. Slowly she uncurled her fists and held them out in the firelight, palms down, as she had held them out above the fire at the foot of the Ladder.

Her hands were white. The white went past the tips and past the first knuckle, up to the second, a clean hard line like the edge of a map, and below the line her skin was livid. The fire lay along it and did nothing at all. She watched his face as he looked. It did not change, and that was the worst of it. He looked the way a man looks at an old, true thing he has been reading for some time and has only now seen the date on.

He set down the pot.

# Chapter 15: Taking the Cold

Kunal took her hands.

He did it before she could close them. He had set the pot down so he would have both hands free, and he spent no breath on whether he should. He folded her fists open and wrapped them in his, palm to palm.

"Don't," said Ila. "Kunal, don't. You'll know."

"I know where to find the answer. Hold still."

He had learned the loophole at six, the way every hill child did, from a grandmother who told it as a warning and a lullaby together. *The one who was lied to can take the cold. Skin to skin. And then they know.* Children played at it in the lanes with snow and a ring of stones. Nobody had told him what it would be to do it with a woman who had just said *no* in a level voice and gone white to the knuckle.

For the last minute he had been holding a small, shabby doubt. She had said *the storm*. She had said *I've been wet since the scree*. A frostnipped hand looks much like a lie-cold hand, if a man wants it to. He had wanted it to. He had sat there with her hands on display and let the fire's failure to warm them mean nothing at all.

Touching her would end that. He knew it, and he held on anyway.

The cold came up his arms like the Mirror in spring, when you go through to the elbow.

It was not hers any more. It was his. It went into his fingertips first and hit the bone, and then it climbed. With it came the knowing, clear as a name read off a stone. A lie. Not small. Middling, the weight that costs a night. She had said several untrue things about the cold tonight, but a lie this size could only have come with the one answer that mattered.

*Do your orders name these lakes?*

*No.*

The fire cracked in the dung cakes. Kunal sat very still with her hands going warm between his, pink coming back under the nails, and felt his own go dead.

He learned no truth. That was the cruelty of it, and the mercy. He knew only the shape of what she had hidden, like the hollow in snow where something has lain. He could not tell what her orders said, or why she had put a lie over them. He knew he had been lied to, and how much it had mattered, and that was all.

"You've gone grey," said Ila. Her voice cracked on it. She stared at their hands. Hers were pink and shaking and her own. His were the colour of tallow. "Let go. You can't take it all. It's too much, you'll—"

"It's a night's shaking. Nimmi would call it a nuisance."

"That's not what Meera said about—"

"Meera told you about a great lie." He held on. "This isn't one."

"It's still mine."

"It was yours until I asked." He heard how hoarse he'd gone. "I could have kept calling it the storm. Your hands would have let me. I could have gone to sleep tonight on a doubt I'd never had to look at." He found half a smile. "I seem to like my ignorance less than I thought."

"Kunal."

The first shudder took him at the shoulders and ran down his spine. His teeth went. He tried to say something dry about it, and his tongue would not shape the words.

Ila said something. He lost the words. Then there was wool around him, the grey pattu, and her arms, and he was pulled in against her side with his head on her shoulder. She was so warm. It was absurd. It was the ordinary warmth of a body, the heat that had left her an hour before and come back, and it was lent to him now with both arms and no argument.

"Don't talk," said Ila into his hair.

"I want to say—"

"Not now."

"I'd rather say it—"

"Morning." Her arms tightened. "If I tell you now it'll be to stop you shaking. I won't do that to the truth. Tell me you can wait till it's light."

He lay in the circle of her arms, and the cold went through him in long waves. In the gaps between them he understood what he had done. He had taken the lie into his own flesh and, with it, the knowledge of it. His bound partner had lied about her orders, and he had chosen to know. Nobody had made him. A smaller man would have stood at the edge of the doubt and warmed his hands at it. His uncle would have.

*Whoever knows the way owns the people.* And what did knowing her cost? He could not say. He had never bought anything so dear on purpose.

"I can wait," said Kunal, and the shaking took him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He barely slept. The shudders came in rounds, each worse than the last, and between them the world went small and bright: the red mouth of the chulha, the smoke finding its hole, a seam of cold air under the door. Ila fed the fire without letting go of him. She had found a way to stretch and reach the dung cakes with one arm while the other kept him. Twice she wedged more of Dhanu's blanket round his feet. Once he woke with his face in her neck and her bare hand flat against his chest.

"Your heart's going like a goat's," she said. She sounded wrung dry.

"Yours?"

"Steady. I counted it."

"Of course you did."

She laughed, once, a cracked sound with no joy in it. Her breath shook. After that she said nothing for a long time, and he lay in her arms with his teeth going like dice and knew, quite clearly, that she was crying, and that she did not want him to say so.

He did not.

By the time the hut's seams went grey, the shaking had thinned to a tremor. His hands were stiff and prickling, flesh again to the last knuckle, though the tips stayed pale. The wind had gone. In its place was a vast listening quiet, and under it, once, far off, the long boom of the third lake letting go of the night.

Ila sat up slowly. She wrapped the pattu round them both and sat cross-legged in front of him, hands on her knees, palms up, in the dim.

"Check the work," she said. It was what she said to clerks who doubted her sums. "Hands first."

He looked. They lay open and pink in the first light, the nails clean, the knuckles easy.

"The letter names the Glass Lakes," said Ila.

There were no stones here to sing. Her hands did not change.

"My commission says only 'every way.' That's all of it. But there is a letter, in the queen's own hand, sealed. It's sewn in my coat, here." Her hand went flat to her ribs. "The fold says to open it when I stand at the Glass Lakes. Not before. I took the letter for part of my orders, so when you asked, I said no, and it wasn't true. And it wasn't the storm, either. I let you think it was. I don't know why she wants the lakes. I've carried it a week and haven't opened it."

Kunal looked at her hands. He looked a long while.

They were warm.

"Why," he said. It came out as a breath. "Why did you say no?"

"Because I was afraid you'd turn back." She did not look away. "You said yourself you would. If my orders named your lakes, you'd have walked me to Ewe's Rest at dawn and shut the Glass Lakes like a door. And I wanted to see where your way goes." Her mouth shook. "I wanted to walk it. Before the queen's hand came between us and told me what it was for. That's all. It's no better a reason than it sounds."

*I wanted to see where your way goes.* It was a surveyor's reason. It was also, he thought, the plainest thing anyone had said to him in thirty years.

"You could have refused," said Kunal. "It's free."

"You'd have read it. A refusal on that question was an answer."

"Yes." He thought about it. "I would have."

"I know." She let out a breath. "I did the sum, and I did it wrong. I thought it was the lesser cost."

"It was." He turned his own hands over in his lap. The tips were still waxy, and he was glad to see them. "It cost you a night of this, and me one. And I've the truth now, which I'd choose over the quiet."

"Do you want the letter?" She said it very low. "I'll hand it to you sealed. I don't know what's in it. It may be a warrant. It may be a map. It may say that my commission is to hold your people's ways, and then I'll have to choose in front of you."

He might have said *give it to me*. Pritam would have said it, softly, with his hand already out.

"It's yours," said Kunal. "The queen wrote it to you."

"And if you're in it?"

"Then I'd like to be told in daylight." A crooked look crossed his face and stayed. "With my teeth in."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The storm had spent itself in the night. When they came out, the sky over the lakes was scoured and high, the cold so clean it rang in the nose. Snow lay in long ribbed drifts along the shore, and the ice beyond was blown bare, a dark floor with the wind-curl still frozen into it.

They stood on the scree with their breath smoking. Ila had her field book out and was not writing in it.

"The second lake," she said. "What do people call it?"

He knew what she was doing. She was handing him the night's name early, on credit, because she could not yet stand to open anything else. He took it.

"The Drum."

"Because it booms."

"Because it's the loudest. My father said it was the heartbeat of the valley." He heard how it sounded. "My father said many foolish things at the Fair. That was one of the better."

She wrote it down in her small hard hand. *The Drum.* Then she shut the book and slung her pack.

"The winter line crosses it," she said. "I'd like to know what it crosses before we trust it. That's not a delay. It's what I'd do on any day of my life."

"Then do it."

The Drum lay a long half hour beyond the Mirror's far shore, over a lip of rock and down a shelf of drift. It was smaller than the first lake, a dark oval bounded by black cliff on one side, and its ice was not smooth. Seams cut across it in great pale lines, shore to shore, where the plate had heaved in the night.

"Boom lines," said Ila, kneeling at the edge. "Every one is where the lake let go. It lets go where it's weakest." She slid an iron spike out of her pack, as long as her forearm, with a ring welded in the head, the kind a surveyor drives to hold a chain. "Give me your knife."

He gave it. She laid the spike against the ice and set the point of his knife in the ring like a lever, and began to chip. It was slow work, the spike turning and the blade biting, and chips of white flew up and stung his cheek. In a quarter hour she had a hole as wide as her fist, and black water welled to the lip.

She dropped a length of cord with a knot in it down the hole. It sank, and caught under the ice, and she drew it up until the knot snagged. Then she held the spike beside it and counted along its length, her lips moving.

"Eleven fingers."

"Is that good?"

"It's a number." She wrote it. "A pair with packs wants eight. Three wants twelve. That's what I've always used on rivers, and it's never lied to me yet." She sat back on her heels. "Eleven holds the two of us with packs. It won't hold a third."

"We're only two."

"Raghu's men are two pairs. If they cross close, they're four, and whatever is behind them." She had her pencil out already. "I'd like to know where they'd go through."

He wanted to say that nobody was following them. He swallowed it. There had been a spyglass on the Hollow Moon. He knelt beside her and took the spike when she held out her hand for it, and he drove it where she pointed, at the next seam, forty paces along, while she worked the knife.

It went like that all morning. She never hurried. At each boom line they cut a hole, and she dropped the cord, and she counted and wrote. *Nine. Ten. Fourteen on the thick side, under the cliff. Seven, here, where the black water showed through, and a cross marked over it.* He watched the numbers go down the page in her handwriting, small and square and sure. After the first hour her shoulders came down from her ears. After the second, she was humming under her breath, off key, a counting tune.

Whatever was in her coat, she had left it there. She had a lake to read, and the lake was honest.

*A pair but not three.* He found himself marking the places with her, a little behind, in the margin of his own head. A pair could cross where nine fingers lay. A pair could not cross where seven did. Where fourteen lay, you could march a column. His uncle's men would have given a year of their lives to buy such knowledge.

And she was writing it down in front of him, with her hand open on the page, and he had not once asked her to stop.

Perhaps that was what the night had bought him. He had taken her cold and paid it, and he had not turned into a man who guarded what he'd paid for. He turned the thought over carefully, as a man handles a bone he thinks has healed. It held.

At noon the sun came over the cliff and lit the Drum from end to end, a great dark oval stitched with white seams and Ila's small black holes. She stood at its centre with the spike in her hand and the last number in her book, steady. He could see it in the line of her back.

Then she turned, and the steadiness went. Her hand rose, as if of itself, to her coat.

"I've sounded what I can sound," said Ila. "I haven't another reason."

She said it to the ice. He walked across the plate to her, on her tracks, and stopped with the length of the rope between them slack.

"Open it," said Kunal.

She drew a long breath. She unpicked the stitches at her ribs with the point of his knife and drew out the folded square, thin and cream and stiff with wax. The seal was red, and when the light struck it a thread of darker red ran through it like a vein.

She looked at him once. Then she put her thumb under the wax and broke the seal.

# Chapter 16: The Grave Map

She did not unfold it.

The seal lay in two halves in her palm, red wax with its thread of darker red. The paper sat in her other hand, still folded in three. Ila looked down the length of the Drum at her own small black holes. Eleven fingers. Nine. Seven, with a cross. A lake was a place where numbers held. She did not think a letter would.

"Not out here," she said.

"No," said Kunal.

They walked back along her tracks, hole to hole, and she tapped the haft at every step although she had sounded it all morning. The rope hung slack between them. It was eight of her arm-spans, and he kept to the near end of it, close enough that she could hear him breathe. At the lip of rock above the shore she found a flat shelf, dry and out of the wind. She sat with her back to the cliff and the letter on her knee.

He stood two paces off with his hands hanging, as a man stands in a doorway when he has not been told to come in. His fingertips were still grey at the ends. They had been grey since dawn, because of her.

Ila was thirty-one years old. She had measured four mountains and two rivers, and she had never once been at a loss for what to do next. She was at a loss now. So she did the thing in front of her.

"Give me your hands."

He gave them. She wrapped them in her own, and they were cold as river stones. She had thought she knew that cold. She had stood half a morning on eleven fingers of ice and trusted it more than this. This was only what was left of a night.

"I'm going to kiss you," said Ila. "I'm saying it first so it's on the record. It's thanks. Some of it isn't thanks."

His brows went up and his mouth pulled in at one corner, the look of a man who has been handed a riddle and means to enjoy it. "Which part is which?"

"I'll let you work it out."

She kissed him. It was not graceful. She had to rise on her knees, and the shelf was narrow, and his hands were still folded in hers, so for a moment they were two people holding a bundle between them. His mouth was cold and then it was not. He made a small sound, low in the chest, surprised out of him. When she let go of his hands to take his collar, they came up and held her face, so carefully that she wanted to laugh. The laugh came out against his mouth and he felt it, and she felt him smile.

She drew back an inch. His eyes were open. He had snow-melt in his lashes.

"I promise nothing," said Ila. It was a rule of the place, and she said it like one. "Not one thing. If that paper says what I'm afraid it says, I may stand up and draw a line you'd hate. I want you to know it before this is the last thing you remember."

"I wouldn't take a promise today. The rope would hold you to it."

"I know."

"Then why say it?"

"Because I'd rather you were angry at me honestly."

He was quiet. His thumbs moved once on her cheekbones. He was not looking at her hands or his own. Ila marked that. A week ago he would have glanced down at his fingertips for the colour.

"I don't know what you'll do with any of it," said Kunal. "The lakes. The numbers. Me. And I'd show you anyway."

It was not a speech. It came out the way her laugh did, before anyone had checked it. She looked at his hands against her jaw. The nails were wax-pale from last night. They had not gone one shade whiter.

"That's true," said Ila.

"I know. I could feel it."

"You could not."

"I could feel you decide not to argue." His thumb moved again. "It's a different thing from being agreed with. I'm learning the difference."

She sat back on her heels, and he let her go. His hands stayed in the air a moment, shaped to her face, before he made them rest on his knees. She picked up the paper.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


*Ila,*

*If you are reading this you are at the Glass Lakes, and you have walked there beside him. I hoped it. I will not pretend I was sure.*

*Sixty-one winters ago the pass began to close. The archive holds the reason in one line, and I will give it to you plain, because nobody gave it to me plain. The price is a life, paid beneath the Shilak Cairn.*

*It was paid. Warden Harish of the Shilak post went down toward the Cairn in the dead of that winter. Prince Udai of Neshar guided him, by the way that begins at these lakes. Prince Udai came home. Warden Harish did not come home.*

*He was your grandfather.*

*The Closing Law was written the next spring, so that no one would walk that way in winter and ask what it cost. I have kept that drawer since my crowning. I will not be the last to keep it unless you help me.*

*Draw the way and read the price before the fourth winter. Tell no one Neshar's part until it is found.*

*Sarala*

She read it once. Then she read it again, slowly, with her finger under the lines, as she read a bearing she meant to trust her life to.

The words did not go in at first. The paper was good, and the queen's hand was small and upright and steady. *Harish* was written with a long first stroke, the way a clerk lengthens a name he has written ten thousand times in other men's ledgers. Ila noted these things the way you note a stone in your boot on the way to a cliff.

*He was your grandfather.*

She had known the name as she knew the name of the first king of Hemkot. It lay in her father's chronicle, in her father's careful clerk's hand, with a date. *Carried down at the thaw.* She had read it over his shoulder as a girl, and she had thought, what a tidy word.

*Carried.* In the hamlets it meant seen across by the hand. In the post's chronicle it meant dead.

Then she remembered the gloves.

Her father had told it once, late, with a cup in him, and never again. Her grandfather had been found at the thaw, sitting whole against a flat stone on the slope above the third bend. His gloves lay on top of it, palms together, set square to the edge, the fingers pointing the way he had been walking. *Like a pair set out for the morning,* her father had said, and shaken his head, and let it go.

She had always thought it a sad picture. A neat man making a neat death. She had even liked it, a little.

It was not neat. It was a receipt.

He had been told where he was going, and what it cost, and he had gone anyway. He had taken off his gloves because he would not need them, and laid them out where someone would find them. Where her father would one day tell it, drunk, and never work out why.

Out on the Drum the ice let go of something with a long low note. She felt it in the rock under her.

She hunted for the right words and found only measurements. Sixty-one winters. The Closing Law, written the next spring. Every crosser a warden had hanged since. Meera's mother, dead of the cough behind the closed pass. Ranu's graves in their rows on the terraced slope. The whole law had been a lid, and she had spent her life drawing lines on the ground beneath it and calling that neutral.

*A map is true or false.* She had said it at nineteen and she had said it at thirty. And here was the truest map she had ever been handed, and it was a grave map. Her own family's. She could not draw one line of it as a thing with no owner.

She took out her field book and shut it over the Drum soundings, the eleven and the nine and the seven with its cross, and put her palm flat on the cover, as if the numbers might get out.

Then the other half struck her, and she looked up.

*Prince Udai of Neshar guided him.*

Kunal was watching her. He had not moved. His face wore a careful, courteous quiet. Behind him the Drum lay dark and white and open, and she heard him again at the toppled door cairn on the Hollow Moon, as lightly as he might say the hour. *Sixty winters ago, in the old women's reckoning. My grandfather walked the princes' way in winter with a guest.* An unnamed guest. The year before the Closing Law.

*I'm counting,* she had told him. He had taken it for arithmetic.

It had been arithmetic. It had come out at a man's name.

She went through it as she would have gone through a field sheet, point by point, and every point turned over under her hand and showed its other face. His family's secret ways, never written, never said to anyone below the passes. *Whoever knows the way owns the people.* The route-keepers, burned out of their huts. Raghu's men digging nine holes in the snow round a toppled cairn. Kunal's own voice, with his fingertips going white at the Mirror: *it matters more than anything.*

She had thought he was guarding his people's road. He was, but the road also guarded something. Perhaps he did not know it himself. The secrecy that kept his people free had also kept a grave unmapped.

Her neutral map was a family grave map.

And neither of them knew it, except the one holding the letter.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She folded it along its creases. It took three tries. Her hands were pink and steady, and she looked at them, because that was a thing she did now.

She could tell him. *Your grandfather walked mine to the Cairn, and mine did not come home.* She could put the letter in his hands and watch his face. But the archive oath sat on her like a hand. She had sworn it, on a long gold-edged page in a hall in Taravan, to take her commission, and it bound her silence about whatever the crown's papers held. Taking it had not cost her a night's sleep. The queen had laid a line of her own across it: *Tell no one Neshar's part.* Ila did not owe that line her obedience. She owed it a hearing.

And she did not know what Udai had done. A man could walk another man down a mountain and come home, and it might be a crime or a mercy. The letter had no word for which. To say it now was to name a prince's grandfather a murderer on a queen's say-so, to a man who, a quarter hour ago, had held her face in both hands.

So she would say nothing. She made herself look at that squarely, since it was a choice and not a thing that merely happened to her. Silence was free. The rope would not make her pay for it. That was exactly what she hated. Any other cost she could have carried to him and laid down.

"Ila," said Kunal, very quietly. "What does it say?"

He had settled on his heels at the rim of the shelf, and the rope lay slack across the snow between them.

She said, "It says I'm to find where your way goes, and read what's written there. The rest I can't tell you yet." She watched her own hands. They stayed pink. It was true. It went through the cold air and the stones above the shore did not sing. She kept her eyes on his face, because she owed him that.

He looked at her hands. He did it openly, and it cost him to do it openly, and she saw the cost go across him in one long breath. He had taken her cold last night. He knew exactly how a lie looked on her skin. He knew now how a true refusal looked, and it looked like nothing at all. It looked like a closed door.

*Can't tell you yet.* He turned the words over the way she turned a bearing. She could almost watch him count the ways it might go. A warrant. A garrison. A line drawn across land his grandmothers had never named. Pritam would have asked again, softly, and kept on asking. Kunal's hand moved to his knee.

"You said 'yet,'" he said.

"I did."

"That's a thing you'd say if you meant it."

"I mean it."

"Yes." He let out a breath. "Your hands say so."

She had thought he might argue, or turn courteous and cold. He did neither. He sat on his heels with the Drum behind him and the whole white morning on his shoulders, and she watched his hand leave his knee and turn palm-up on the snow between them, open, empty, there to be seen. The snow took the print of it, four fingers and a palm, the tips still grey.

And she was still sitting on the answer.

"Kunal," she said. "Whatever's there, I'll tell you the first moment I can. I'm not making that a promise. The rope would hold me to it, and I don't know that I can keep it."

"I know."

"I'd rather you were angry at me honestly."

"You said that." The corner of his mouth moved. "Do you think I'll be angry?"

"I think you'll be right to be."

He got up slowly, and offered her the same hand, and she took it, and he drew her to her feet on the narrow shelf. They stood very close. The wind had dropped, and the cliff stood over them with its black seams. She looked at his face and saw that it was perfectly steady. His eyes were bright, and not with anger.

"The way begins at the head of these lakes," said Kunal. "It's mine to show. It was my grandfather's, and he'd have said it wasn't his to show either." His hand tightened once on hers and let go. "I won't make you swear what you'll do with it. I haven't the right, and you've said you'd not give it. But you came here to find where it goes. So."

He turned east, where the third lake lay hidden behind its spur, booming softly, a deep note that came up through the rock into her boots.

"Then I'll take you."

She could not say anything that was both kind and true, and she had learned this week what the second kind cost. She said nothing. He took the silence for what it was and did not press it.

She knew what his family had done, and he did not. And he had just offered, with an open hand and a steady face, to walk her down the road where his grandfather had walked hers.

She folded the letter into her coat, buttoned it inside against her ribs with a shaking hand, and went after him.

# Chapter 17: The Shape of a Silence

Ila had not looked him in the eye since the shelf above the Drum.

She looked at everything else. She looked at the roof poles of Dhanu's upper hut, the smoke hole, the juniper stack, the grain of the split-deodar door with the wind leaning on it. When Kunal fed the chulha, she looked at his hands. When he passed her the tea, she took the cup by its rim, so their fingers never met, and watched his hands go away again.

Her own she kept in plain view, flat on the blanket or spread on the sleeping shelf, as a suspect shows a clerk she carries nothing.

They had been an hour along the shore past the Drum when the storm came over the spurs, a white wall, faster than her pocket watch allowed. The Eye lay half a night's ice ahead with no roof on it. The fold of shore under the Drum, where the lower huts stood, was already drifting shut behind them, the wind packing it to the eaves. So they went on west with the rope hauled taut between them, over the shoulder and up to Dhanu's hut above the Mirror, the only shelter they could still reach. That was a day and a half ago. The wind had not stopped since, and the stones outside sang in it, a low note with no meaning.

Kunal had learned her silence the way he learned a new road. It had edges. It began exactly where the kiss ended. She talked well enough about the weather, the ice, the thickness of the hut walls, which she had paced twice and found the north wall a hand shorter than the south. She answered whatever he asked. But when he asked anything that came near the shelf, her gaze dropped and stayed low.

By the second morning he had a theory, and he disliked it.

She was ashamed of the kiss. She had been cold and grateful and wrung out after a night in which he had taken her lie onto his skin, and she had paid him the only coin she had. Now the coin sat between them, and she was too honest to take it back and too kind to say so.

He turned it over while he did the one useful thing the storm allowed, which was the petition.

It was four sheets, folded in his coat, for the thaw council. He wrote it on his knee with one of her pencil leads, warmed against his chest and held in his fist like a coal. The hill council wanted a seat at Taravan, and the keeping of the high ways under the new law, and no crown garrison in any hill village. Every clause was a thing he had said aloud at a long table. None had cost him anything to write until this week.

He wrote *the high ways* and stopped. He had been looking at ten days of Ila's field book, and for the first time in his life he understood that "ways" was a word you could hand someone, and that they could draw it.

He finished the line. Then he put the lead down and said, to the roof poles, "If it was the cold talking—"

"It wasn't."

She said it before he reached the verb. She was crouched at the door, chipping ice into the water pot with the back of her axe, and her head did not turn. But her hands had stopped.

He looked, because he could not help it. They were pink. The knuckles were easy, and they were hers.

It was true, then. And it left him with less than he had come in with, because the theory had been tidy and the truth was not. If she did not regret it, and was not cold, and was not ashamed, then there was a wall, and he could see it clearly. He could not name a single stone.

"Good," said Kunal. He found a smile and put it on. "I'd hate to be the sort of man who was kissed by weather."

She laughed. It was the short, loud one, cut off at once, and it startled the water pot. Then the quiet came back down.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind dropped a little after dark, enough that they could hear the lakes through it. Far off the Drum boomed, one slow note, and then another answered it from somewhere under the cliff.

They had the sleeping shelf to share and one blanket's worth of room on it. The first night, they had lain like two planks. Tonight Kunal banked the fire and came back along the rope, and Ila was sitting up against the wall with her knees drawn in.

"Your grandfather," she said. "Prince Udai." She said it carefully, as if setting a foot on rotten ice. "You said he walked the way in winter. What was he like?"

It was the first thing she had asked him in a day that was not about a measurement. He sat down at the shelf's end, a hand's breadth from her boots.

"I don't know. He died when I was four." He thought about it. "I remember a beard that smelled of apricot smoke, and being lifted onto a wall to watch the Fair. That's all of him I own. The rest is my uncle's."

"What did your uncle say?"

"He said it at supper, a week after my father's funeral. I was nine." Kunal picked at a loose thread on the blanket. "Pritam likes a lesson to arrive with a meal. He said my grandfather had shown the way once, to one guest, in winter, and was not the same man after. Then he put down his knife. *The way kills those who share it,* he said. 'Remember that, boy.'" He heard how light his voice had gone. "That was the whole story. The lesson's on the end like a rope on a post."

"And the rule?"

"My grandfather's rule was only for winter. My uncle made this one that night, over the fish. No prince shows the way to wife or son or friend. It stays in the head of one man at a time." He tried the smile again. "It's a very good rule. The way's been kept close for sixty winters. My uncle only gave the keeping a rule."

Ila had gone still. It was a particular stillness, the kind that came over her at a bearing that would not close.

"You've gone quiet," said Kunal. "It's a tale for frightening boys. I've shown you three ways in ten days, and you're alive."

"Yes," said Ila. "I am."

It was not an answer. He put it with the others.

"The night's name," he said. "You measured the long seam at the cliff foot, where the plate rings. Hill people call it Where the Drum Answers."

She had her field book out already. "Because it echoes?"

"Because when it answers, you lie still till morning. You never cross on a night the Drum answers." He watched her write it, small and hard. "My nurse used to say it to me. *Where the Drum answers, lie still till morning.* It was how she got me to sleep."

Her pencil stopped on the last word. Then it finished, neatly, and she shut the book.

"Your question," said Kunal. "I've a name given, so I've a question owed. Or you may refuse."

"I know the terms."

"I've been choosing it all day."

She sat still. He thought of the other questions he could have asked. *What does the letter say. What did you mean by yet. Why can't you look at me.* Any of them was fair. But each, he had found, could be answered with a shrug and a word like *warrant*, and he would never know whether he had been told the truth or only not been lied to.

So he asked the narrowest thing he could find.

"Is my family in your letter?"

The fire ticked. In the stones outside the wind rose, and sank.

He watched her hands, as he had watched them for ten days, from the angle where he could see all ten nails. They stayed still.

"I'm not going to answer that," said Ila.

He had known she would say it. He had told her himself, on the Drum, that a refusal on a close question was an answer, and had thought himself clever. He waited for the door to close on it.

It did not close.

"But I'll give you something true instead," she said. Her voice had thinned. She made her head come up, and her eyes went to his collar and no higher, and he watched what it cost her to hold them there. "I didn't ask about your grandfather to pass the time. I have a reason. I can't tell you what it is. I'd rather you knew that than thought I was only being kind."

The cold in the hut did not change. Her fingertips stayed pink. The stones outside held their one low note.

So it was not an idle question. He had half hoped it was. He had sat all day hoping that somewhere in her silence there was a clerk's habit and nothing else, and she had taken that from him, with her own hands in the light, and paid for it in her voice.

He sat with it. The word *family* had Pritam's face on it, because everything did. But she had not asked about Pritam. She had asked about Udai, and a dead man smelling of apricot smoke could not have a warrant out on him. It could mean a hundred things. It could mean the way itself, and a line she meant to draw. It could mean something sixty winters old, in a ledger from before his birth.

"Then I'll take it as it stands," said Kunal. "Not as a yes."

Her head dipped, a little. He did not know if it was thanks.

"I can't help what I infer," he said. "I'm not a stone. But I'll put it down and not build on it. If I find out otherwise, I'll find out in daylight, from you." He heard the dryness in it and let it stay. "I'd like it noted that this is a considerable feat for a man raised by my uncle."

"Noted," said Ila. "Kunal, it's more than I've earned."

"Probably. But it's cheaper than the alternative, and I've lately found I'm thrifty."

She made a sound that was nearly a laugh, and nearly something else. She slid down along the wall and lay on her side with her back to him, and the length of the rope lay between them across the blanket. After a while, without turning, she put one hand out behind her, palm up, on the boards.

It asked for nothing. She was only leaving it where he could see it.

He lay down beside it and did not touch it. He looked at it in the red light until the fire went to coals. It was warm and steady, and it was hers, and it told him nothing he wanted to know.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The third day brought nothing but the count.

At first light he took the petition out again and ruled the margin with the edge of the pencil case, a line for each day. He had started it the night of the binding. Thirty-five days to the Meadow Cairn's meltwater, when the bond ended and the rope came off them for good. After that, three weeks on in a hard year, the Shilak thaw council would sit, and the crown's men and the hill's would read what had been written. He had thought of the thirty-five as a distance. Now he laid it out in strokes and counted them down.

Ten. Ten strokes, and twenty-five empty places, and at the end of them the rope came off.

He had gone into the winter wanting two things, and each had a number on it. The first was that council. The second was the unmapped hills, still his when the bond ended. He was beginning to understand that the two might not live in the same house.

When he looked up, Ila was at the door with her eye to the crack, one hand flat on the wood. She had been there some time.

"It's easing," she said. "The pitch has changed."

She opened it a hand's width. Light came in white and hard, and the cold with it, and the singing in the stones thinned to a thread and stopped. Beyond the scree the Mirror lay blown bare and shining. The sky had been wiped.

They stood in the door together with their breath going up. Neither spoke, and he found that he did not want to, because he knew what the next hour was for.

Within the hour they would walk east along the shore, past the Drum, to the head of the third lake. She would unfold her field sheet and ask him for a bearing, and he would have to say what he had never said aloud to anyone.

He had already said the easy part, on the shelf above the Drum, with her hand in his. *Then I'll take you.* Taking her was a gesture. The hard part was the plain telling: the name of the place, and how little he knew of it. *No prince shows the way to wife or son or friend.* He was not sure which she was. It was the rule he had just written a petition against. *The high ways, in the hill council's keeping.* Nine council members would share what one man had held for sixty years, and he was giving the first line of it to a crown surveyor before he had asked any of them. It was not a trade, and she had not asked. That was why.

"The way goes in at the head of the third lake," said Kunal.

She turned her head. Her eyes came to his face for the first time in two days and stopped at his chin.

"There's a cleft in the cliff where the ice meets the rock. Hill people call the lake the Eye. It's the last, and it's the deepest, and the cleft is the pupil." He kept his voice level. "That's where the princes' way enters the mountain. I've never been inside it past the first turn."

"Never?"

"My uncle took me to the first turn when I was twelve. He stood me against the rock and turned me round himself, and walked me out." He made himself say the rest. "He said the remainder was for a prince who had earned it. I took that to mean him. I've never been told what it meant."

The cold came in at the door. She said nothing. The pencil was in her fist, and her field book stayed shut.

"I'm telling you because we are going there, and I'd rather you walked in knowing how little I know than how much I'd like you to think I do." He pulled off his right glove, then the left, and held them in one fist. The tips of his fingers showed in the white light, grey and blistered, the skin gone tight and shiny with frostnip.
