He had not meant to show them. "That's what your last lie cost me, and I'd pay it again. I'm not charging you for it. I'm showing you that I've no more than you. Past the first turn I know nothing. It's all true. You can look."

She looked.

He saw her do it. Her eyes went to his bare fingers and then to the sky behind him, and then, at last, to his eyes, full on, and it was like a door opening onto a room with no floor. There was grief in it. He could see that much and no more. He could not tell whose it was, or what for.

"Thank you," said Ila. She said it very low.

The Eye stayed out of her field book. He noticed, and did not know what to make of it. She stood in the white doorway with her pencil shut in her hand, and her eyes went east along the shore to where the third lake waited behind its spur, and then, quickly, back to his hands.

# Chapter 18: The Eye

The ice on the Eye rang clear, and Ila trusted it about as far as she could throw it.

She tapped her axe haft along the shore for a hundred paces, then knelt and chipped a sounding hole with the survey spike and Kunal's knife. Fourteen fingers. Fifty paces on, sixteen. Near the middle, where the lake was deepest, the ice ran so clear that her hole went down like a blue throat.

"Well?" said Kunal.

"It'll carry us. It won't carry us kindly."

Frost smoked off the lake in the low sun, and the morning was so still that she could hear her own pulse. The third lake lay long and dark between steep walls. The Drum was behind them, and the Mirror behind that. At the head of it, where the ice met a black cliff, was the cleft.

It was a slot twice a man's height. A curtain of blue ice hung from its lintel, half drawn, like a lid over an eye. Somewhere deep inside, water ticked on stone, slow and patient, like a clock in another house.

She stopped fifty paces short and let the rope go slack.

Low in the dark, a long way in, there was light. It was faint and blue, and steady as a lamp left burning in a far room. She had seen it on the Stair and on the Hollow Moon, strung out along the winter line. Cairn-light. It never flickered. The mountain had put it there to be seen.

"You see it," she said.

"Yes." Kunal's voice came from behind her shoulder. He had stopped a pace short of where she stood. "I was twelve when he brought me here. There was no light. There was a hole in a cliff and my uncle's hand on the back of my neck."

"You weren't bound."

"No."

"So it shows itself to the bound." She said it flatly, to the ice. "The mountain marks it. It wants us to find it."

Kunal said nothing. She looked round at last. He stood with his arms away from his body and his hands open, the way he had stood in the doorway of the hut. His feet had not moved since the last sounding. She knew that stance. It was a boy's, when a hand had been laid on the nape of his neck.

He had walked her to the one place in the world he was afraid of, on a morning when he had every reason to think she would draw it and sell it.

Ila sat down on a flat tongue of rock at the shore and took out her field book.

She drew the lakes first, because they were the part she knew. The Mirror, the Drum and the Eye lay one below the other like three beads on a string, and every number she had paid for in holes went down in her small hard figures. Then she took her bearings on the cleft from two points along the shore. She paced the ice to the lip. Eighty-eight paces. She wrote *88* and underlined it.

Then she said, without turning, "I'm drawing the mouth."

The wind had no voice. Kunal's boots shifted on the snow.

"Where it is, how wide, how high, and the light inside. All of it, to the pace. I'm telling you because the book goes where my commission sends it, unless I decide otherwise. I haven't decided. I won't promise you that I will."

"I know."

"I can draw it less true." She made herself turn and look at him, full in the face. "Move it a hundred paces. Leave off the light. People do it. Maps lie by being kind." Her pencil pointed at the slot. "Somebody will come here in a storm with a sheet in his hand, and he'll believe it. A sheet that's wrong is a grave with a tidy lid."

He thought it through while she watched. When he spoke it was slowly, like a man reading a bill aloud.

"If you draw it true, the crown has it by spring. An engineer will lay your sheet on a table and put his thumb on the eighty-eight paces. Twenty men on that shelf. A fire on this tongue, where we're sitting. Sentries along the shore, so nothing goes in or out that the crown hasn't counted." His hands stayed open, though it cost him. "My uncle taught me that a line is an owner. I know exactly what you'd be handing them."

"Then say it. Tell me to move it."

Kunal looked at the cleft for a long moment. Then he looked at her.

"Draw it true," he said.

It came out rough. He cleared his throat and said it again, quieter. "Draw it true, Ila. I'd rather a hundred men knew where it is than one went in the wrong place."

She said no thanks, because it was not a gift. She turned back and wrote the bearing down, and her hand was steadier than her breath.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


To take the width of the mouth, she had to go in.

From the shore the slot looked shoulder-wide. It was not. The blue curtain had grown down over the rock for years, and the true walls were behind it, out of sight and out of reach of any sight bar. She could chain only what she could touch.

She sounded the first pace of the shelf under the curtain, and the haft rang clean. She sounded the second and it rang clean. She stood with the rope slack behind her and weighed it, as she weighed everything.

"One in eight," said Ila.

"One in eight what?"

"That it's hollow under there. I can hear meltwater, and meltwater undercuts." She tugged the rope twice. "Three paces. I take the width off the rock itself and come out."

"One in eight is a great deal of mountain."

"It was one in five on the Hollow Moon and you didn't say a word."

"I said a word. You didn't hear it." He moved up behind her and took the rope in both hands. The wrappings he had made, torn from his shirt, were dark where the blisters had broken. "If you go through, I'll hold you."

"Hold. Don't haul." She looked at him over her shoulder. "If I drop, don't pull until I say. A rope dragged over a lip cuts. Let me climb."

"You want me to stand there while you hang."

"I want you to stand there while I climb."

He looked at her a long moment. Then he set his heels in the snow. "Go."

Under the blue curtain, the cold closed round her like a wet cloth. The ticking was suddenly louder, and the cairn-light lay ahead, calm and low. One pace. Two. Her haft rang, then rang again. She laid her palm on the left wall, wet and glassy, and stretched the chain to its end.

On the third pace the haft gave a short dull note, like a spoon on a pillow. She knew what it meant, and her weight had already gone onto the foot.

The shelf went with a sound like a dropped plate. She dropped through to the armpits, and her chin cracked on the edge. The rope snapped taut across her ribs and wrenched the knot on her wrist. Under her boots there was nothing at all, only a cold breath coming up from far below, smelling of stone and water.

The survey spike slid out of her belt. She heard it fall. One, two, three. Then a small bright sound, very far away.

"Hold," said Ila.

"Holding."

His voice was ten paces behind her and perfectly level. The rope did not move. It might have been tied to the cliff.

She swung her axe with her right arm and sank the pick into the ice wall at the back of the mouth. It bit. She tried it with her weight, and it held. She kicked, found a crack with the toe of her boot, and pushed.

"Don't pull."

"I'm not."

She could hear what it cost him. His breath came harsh through his nose. Every muscle in him must have been screaming to haul her out like a sack. He did not. He stood in the snow and let her climb on her own axe, a hand's breadth at a time, as she had asked.

Then she was over the lip on her belly, lying with her cheek against the ice and her heart going like a mallet.

"Ila."

"I'm out." Her voice had a crack in it. "I'm out. Let go."

The rope went slack. She crawled the last three paces on her knees and her good hand, and sat down in the snow. Then she looked round.

He was on one knee, with the rope wound twice round his hip and still locked in both fists. The wrappings had come off. Red had run into the wool.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They sat on the stone tongue out of the wind, with the sun on their backs and the Eye shining below them. She had the chain in her lap and the field book safe in her coat. She had the left wall, from the lip to the centre, and nothing of the right. The spike was gone. Without it the chain had no anchor, and the figure would stay half a figure until she found another way to hold it.

"Give me your hands," said Ila.

The words went through her like a draught. She had said them on the shelf above the Drum.

He gave them. She laid them across her knees, palms up.

They were worse than she had thought. The fingertips were swollen and shining, with blisters at each pad. Four had burst on the rope and wept pink into the creases. She had known what the cold had cost him. She had not looked at it this closely, and she made herself look now.

*Because of me,* she thought. Then she put the thought down, because it did not help, and reached into her coat.

The jar was small and brown, with a cork bound on by a thong. He frowned at it.

"That's Dhanu's. From the hut."

"The second morning." She worked the cork out with her teeth. "It was on the shelf by the barley. I took it and said nothing."

"You wouldn't look at me for two days."

"I was looking at your hands." She warmed a thumbnail of the fat in her fist until it softened. "I was also not looking at you. Both are true."

He made a short sound. She kept her eyes down. She cleaned the broken blisters first, with snow-melt from the flask. He flinched, once, and held still. Then she smoothed the fat over each pad in turn, as lightly as she could, and wrapped the fingers one by one in strips torn from her own shirt hem. She was good with small, exact work.

"It's not cold you can warm," she said. "Fire doesn't touch it. But the skin heals."

"You've been carrying it for two days."

"I've been carrying a good many things for two days." That was true, and she heard it as she said it. She kept her eyes on the knot she was tying. "Does it hurt less?"

"It does." He turned his hand over in hers, very slightly. "That's the first time anyone has dressed my hands since I was nine."

She had no answer for that, so she talked, and reached for the safest ground she had.

"Rohan sends me numbers," said Ila.

"Numbers."

"Every summer. A packet from the Shilak post, tied with the post's grey cord. Snow depth at the door by the week. Thaw dates at the cairn. How many crossers turned back, how many sacks the wagons carried in the autumn." She pulled the last knot snug. "Eleven winters of columns. There's never a word of his own. Once, in a margin, he wrote *ice late*."

"That's all?"

"They're love letters." She heard how it sounded and let it stand. "That's what he can do. He sends me his sums, and I send him mine, and neither of us has to say a thing. I read them when I'm homesick."

Kunal began to laugh.

It came out of him by surprise, and it was not the courtier's laugh she had learned in the first weeks. It was lower and rougher, and it shook his shoulders, so that she had to hold his bandaged hand still. "The most feared captain in Hemkot," he said, "sends you his thaw dates."

"With the sacks."

"Tied in grey cord."

"He'll be insufferable once he's married. Meera counts things aloud." She was laughing too, the loud sudden one, and two ravens went up off the scree. The sound rang off the cliff and came back to her from the cleft, thin and strange, and she stopped.

For a long time she looked at the mouth. It was no longer a place she was afraid of. It was a place she had drawn, which was worse.

She opened the field book on her knee and laid her rule against the *88*. She meant only to finish the line, the way she finished every line, from the ice to the lip. Her pencil ran along the rule, out across the shore, and onto the flat tongue of rock where they sat.

It stopped there.

*Twenty men on the shelf,* he had said. *A fire on this tongue.* She could see it as clearly as the sheet. A clerk at Taravan with the bearings in a drawer. An engineer's thumb on the paper. A watch-fire where her own boots were, and a chain of sentries drawn along the shore as neat as stitching. Every figure would be accurate. Every line would be hers.

Ila lifted the pencil off the page and held it away from her, as if it might go off.

Where she would aim it, she did not know. Only that the choice had stopped being someone else's.

The book went deep into her coat, beside the letter and flat against her ribs. Kunal asked nothing, and she was grateful, and she hated that she was.

"I'll draw the right wall from the shore, and mark it as not measured," she said. "I won't put a number on what I haven't touched."

"I should hope not."

"You held."

"You asked me to." He lifted the wrapped hand a little and looked at it. "I find I'm bad at it. Not pulling."

"You were very good at it."

He smiled at her, plainly, without having first checked it, and she had to look away over the lake.

It was then, past the pale curve of the Eye, that she saw the smoke.

It stood up from the far end of the chain, a grey thread against the rock, where the lower huts hid in the fold of the shore. It rose straight and thin. Someone was burning dung and juniper, and had been for an hour.

Kunal had seen it too. She felt it in the rope.

"That's the lower hut," he said. "Nobody's lived there since autumn."

"Raghu's men?"

"Raghu's men burn pitch. They'd have no reason to hide the smell." His eyes narrowed, and the tension went out of his shoulders. "That's a chulha that draws well. That's Dhanu's. Dhanu and Gauri have come."

She stood up. The sun was bright on the ice.

Dhanu would not have climbed this far up in the snow for nothing. Whatever he carried, it was news. She turned toward the smoke and took the first step down the shore.

# Chapter 19: Knucklebones

"No news until you've eaten," said Gauri, and shut Ila's fingers over a fistful of dried apricots as if she were closing a gate.

Dhanu stood behind his elder sister in the door of the lower hut with his mouth open on whatever he had climbed here to say. He had blown the goat-horn whistle three times along the shore, two long and one short, *come on, it's clear*, and he had not stopped limping toward them since. Right foot planted, left swung out and round. He looked like a man who had run a long way with a full bucket and been told to put it down.

"Gauri—"

"Eat. Both of you." She had been cutting him off since he was small, and she did it now without looking round. She took Kunal's wrapped hands in her broad brown ones, turned them over and sniffed the goat fat. "Who tied these?"

"I did," said Ila.

"Flat knots." Gauri gave the hands back as though they had been inspected and passed. "Good."

Ila had been checked by clerks and engineers for twelve years. She had never once felt it go all the way down.

The hut was one room, warm and low, smelling of dung smoke and wet wool. On the sleeping shelf under a pony blanket lay a small shape with one sock off. Mini. Her fist was against her mouth and her breath whistled faintly.

Ila ate an apricot. It was leathery and sour and sweet, and her stomach turned over with gratitude. She ate a second. By the sixth she had started counting, so she ate a seventh to spite herself.

"Now," said Dhanu, "the bones."

"News," said Gauri.

"After the apricots, you said. The bones keep the hands busy while the mouth works." He was already digging in his coat. He came out with a leather twist, and from it spilled five knucklebones, sheep's, polished by years of thumbs. One was stained red. "A woman who can't throw knucklebones can't be trusted with a pass. Sit."

Ila sat on the hearth edge. Kunal folded himself against the wall with his bandages in his lap and the rope slack across the floor. He had the look of a man who meant to enjoy being useless.

The rules were simple, which made them cruel. You scattered four bones on the flat stone by the chulha. You tossed the fifth into the air, swept one bone off the stone with the same hand, and caught the fifth before it fell. Then two at a time. Then three and one. Then all four.

She lost the first game badly. The bones skittered where they liked. Her hand, which could hold a chain steady in a gale, made a fist of itself and dropped the red one in the ash.

"Ha," said Dhanu, with deep satisfaction.

"She's learning," said Kunal, from the wall.

"She's *losing*."

"She's gathering data."

Ila looked at him. He kept his face grave, but one corner of it had gone.

She gathered data. The hearthstone was a river slab laid by someone in a hurry. The side by the door sat a finger low. The third corner rocked, very slightly, under the heel of her hand. She tapped it twice and listened. When she threw the bones again, she did not throw them anywhere. She let them fall from a hand's height on the one spot where the slope walked them together, two and two, with the red one nosing neatly out on its own.

Then she swept, and caught, and did it again with three.

By the fourth round Dhanu had stopped saying *ha*.

"Do that once more," he said.

She did it once more. The bones went where she had meant them to go. They always did. He stared at the stone, then at her, then at the stone.

"Those aren't throws," said Dhanu. "Those are *sums*." His voice cracked upward. "You've surveyed my hearth!"

"Only the one slab."

"She's surveyed my hearth, Gauri!"

"It's been crooked since the day you laid it," said Gauri, without turning from the pot.

"A finger low on the door side," said Ila, "and the third corner rocks. You'd have a draught under that slab in winter."

"I do have a draught!" He slapped his knee and roared. The bundle on the shelf stirred and muttered, and he clapped a hand over his own mouth and shook with it, the tears running down his face. When he could speak he said, "Ten winters I've lost to my own hearth. Never a soul told me why."

Kunal was laughing against the wall, without sound, with his shoulders. It was the rough laugh from the lakeshore, and she did not look at it for long.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The soup came after. Gauri said "Now," in the tone of a woman shutting a book, and Dhanu put the bones away.

He told it plainly, with his hands on his knees. His boy had run in two nights ago from the upper folds, and what he had seen was this.

Raghu had eight pairs in the Hollow Moon country.

"Eight," said Kunal.

"Sixteen men, each pair bound at a cairn. They came by the Kuhl cut in twos and threes, so the watchers wouldn't count them." Dhanu rubbed his bad leg. "Six pairs are in the bowl. They've dug it like a field. Nine holes round the door cairn, and more along the east wall. The boy says they sit round the fire at night and don't talk."

"They took Hari's goats," said Gauri.

"Nine of them. Drove them up the Hollow Moon road in a string." His voice dropped. "Hari wasn't with them. Nobody's seen him since the hut burned."

Ila remembered the hut. She remembered the black ring of it, and the chalk on the marker stone with its second line smeared out. She put her spoon down.

"They haven't found the lakes," said Kunal. It was not quite a question.

"Not the basin. Not yet." Dhanu looked at him. "Two pairs went off along the rim shelf, and the boy followed their tracks as far as he dared. They've sat on the Kuhl shoulder, the one over the head of the lakes, two nights now. He saw their fire from the upper fold."

Nobody spoke. The fire settled in the chulha.

Ila did the sum. She could not stop it, and it came out the same each way she ran it. A steady orange fire on a shoulder of rock above the last lake, seen from the foot of the Ladder, where Kunal had said nobody kept a fire. She had tied it to Raghu's pairs then, and still she had walked in under it. Four bound men could see the blue light low in the cleft. That shoulder looked straight down on the Eye. And that morning she had stood on that ice in full sun, drawing the mouth, with the rope slack and a book in her hand.

She looked up. Kunal was already looking at her across the hearth. His face had gone still, and she saw him reach the same figure, in the same order. They might already be looking down on the cleft. They might have watched the two of them kneel to chip a hole in the ice.

Neither of them said it. Dhanu had spent himself climbing here, and Mini slept ten feet away with her fist at her mouth. A number said aloud in this room would sit on the child's blanket until morning. Kunal gave the smallest shake of his head, and Ila picked up her spoon again. She could taste nothing in it.

"Mini," said Dhanu, after a while, in a different voice. He was looking at the shelf. "She's asked after you. All the way up. 'Is the lady with the string still counting?'"

It went into Ila before she had thought. It slid under all the sums.

"The star I named for her," she said. "I wrote it down."

Dhanu's head came round.

"The small yellow one over Ewe's Rest, the night we came in. It had no name in any book I own, and it was bright enough to shout." She kept her eyes on the pot, because it was easier. "Kunal said it wouldn't go on the map. It won't. It's in the margin of my field book, in pencil, which isn't the map. But it's hers. It has her name against it, with the bearing."

Dhanu said nothing at all.

When she looked up, his eyes were full. The tears stood in them, bright, and did not fall. He was a big man, and he had laughed himself weak over a crooked hearth an hour ago, and he sat with his hands open on his knees and could not say a word.

Gauri put her palm flat between his shoulders and left it there, as she must have done when he was a boy with a scraped knee.

"She'll want it on the map," he said at last, thickly. "She'll make you swear it."

"I'd rather swear to that than most things," said Ila.

He scrubbed his face with the heel of his hand. Then he did what she had come to expect of him, and reached for a laugh to stand on.

"Look at him." He jerked a thumb at Kunal. "Sitting there like a lamb in a blanket. Do you know how long it took me to teach *him* the bones? Seven winters of evenings, and he sulked every time a goatherd's child beat him. Stubborn as a stone, our Kunal, and you'd never believe the half of what's behind it. Ask him what his uncle did on the ridge when he was—"

"Dhanu."

It was said flat, and it was not loud. It went through the room like a snuffer over a wick.

Dhanu stopped with his mouth still shaped for the next word. His hand came down off his knee. In the stillness Ila heard the lid rattle on the pot, and Mini's whistling breath, and the wind going over the roof.

She looked at Kunal.

He had not moved from the wall. His face was composed. His hands lay in his lap in their wrappings, and he studied them with great attention, as if he meant to count the knots. But the colour had gone out from under his skin. His mouth had the set of a door pulled to from the inside.

*And I came home,* he had said, in the snow cave on the Hollow Moon, and put the rest where she could not reach.

A question was ready. It had come up whole, the way they did, with its bearing already taken. *What did your uncle do?* It was fair. She had a right to ask it, with the rope round her wrist and the hill's half-spoken story lying open in the room. She knew what not asking would cost. Dhanu would tell her by morning, joke and all, for the price of one look over the washing-up. And if she let the question go, she would walk into the Eye tomorrow blind to whatever drove the man on the other end of her rope. Against that she set the letter flat against her ribs, which she was keeping from him, and the narrow question he had put to her on the shelf, and how he had taken her refusal and set it down without building on it. *I'll find out in daylight, from you.* She did not ask.

"My throw?" said Ila.

Dhanu let out a breath that went on a good while. "Your throw," he said, small and shamefaced, and he took the bones out again, and put them in her hand.

She threw without looking at Kunal. The bones fell exactly as she meant them to, and for once she took no pleasure in it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dhanu had put them in the second hut, the smaller one, where the chulha had been lit that morning. It held a plank shelf, a stack of dung cakes and one blanket. They had shared quarters nine times now, and neither of them mentioned it.

Kunal had said almost nothing since the bones. He had eaten, and thanked Gauri, and let Dhanu clasp his shoulder at the door and say, "I'm a fool, brother," and answered, "Yes," quite gently. Ila had taken her boots off and lain on the shelf with her face to the wall and the rope across the blanket. She listened to him not sleeping.

The Drum boomed, far off through the cliff. It was a slow single note, and then nothing.

"You didn't ask," said Kunal.

She was not asleep, and said so. "No."

"Why?"

She thought about it, because he deserved a true answer and because it cost nothing to give one. "You said *and I came home*, in the Moon, and then you put the rest somewhere I couldn't reach. I thought it was yours to bring out. Or not."

"Dhanu would have told you. If you'd asked him tomorrow, when I was out of the room." His voice was very even. "Half the hill knows a piece of it. Dhanu has the best piece. It's the one with the joke in it."

"I wasn't going to ask Dhanu."

"I know." The blanket shifted. "That's why I'm not going to let him."

She turned over. He was sitting up against the wall with the bandaged hands on his knees. In the red light of the coals his face had no courtesy on it at all.

"I'll tell you myself," said Kunal. "Not from Dhanu. From me." He stopped. She watched him make himself go on. "I don't know what it's worth. I don't know what you'll do with it. I've been trying to work it out since the bones, and I can't, and I think that's the point of it."

Ila sat up. Her heart was going hard, and she kept her voice level.

"All right."

"Not here." He looked at the door, and past it, to the dark and the lake and the long ringing ice. "There's a place on the shore where you can hear the Drum. Where it booms. I'd like to be standing when I say it."

"Where the Drum answers," said Ila, "you lie still till morning."

"I know what it says." One side of his mouth moved, not quite a smile. "I've never in my life done what it says."

"Then we do it with the figures in front of us." She said it now because the room held no one it could frighten. "The open shore is in plain view of the shoulder. But the Drum fold, the cut under the cliff at the far end, is behind the overhang. I drew it from the mouth of the Eye this morning. From that shoulder the cliff hides it. The fold itself is safe."

"And the way to it?"

"Three hundred paces of open shore, there and back. That they can see, if they look." She counted it off on her fingers, because it steadied her. "Four men up there, two awake. Each one is staring into his own fire, and a man who stares at a fire can't see across snow at night. We carry no lamp. We keep to the shadow of the bank. Against that, a glass, a cold clear sky, and two shapes on white ground. Two pairs of eyes is a worse sum than one. I put it at one in five that anyone looks at that stretch tonight, and I can't make it smaller."

"One in five."

"And they know about the huts already. Dhanu's whistle carried three times, and that smoke has stood up all afternoon. That much is spent." She made herself say the rest. "What isn't spent is a bound pair walking out of those huts after dark, toward the cliff. They'd know the two who stood on the Eye this morning are the two walking. Then Raghu has who drew it, and that's what we'd lose."

"One in five."

"I've walked worse for a smaller reason." She put her feet over the edge of the shelf and found her boots. "It's your choice and I'm weighing it with you. I'm not following it."

The rope went with her, and with it the whole length of him, so there was no question of going alone. She tied the laces by feel and did not hurry them.

When she stood, he held out one wrapped hand, palm up, the way she had once left hers on the boards. She laid her own in it. The bandage was rough, and the hand beneath it was warm.

He pulled the door open on the cold and the stars. Far down the shore the Drum spoke again, one slow note under the ice, and the whole dark answered it.

# Chapter 20: The Ridge

The shore was a shelf of black stone with the Drum under it, and the ice there was alive.

Kunal stood where it boomed. Out on the lake a long seam let go, and the note came up through his boots and into his teeth. The cold was clean and the sky was thick with stars. Behind him Ila had stopped at the end of the rope's slack, a pace and a half away, with her hands in her sleeves.

She had not said anything since the hut. In twelve days he had learned this about her: she waited like a woman holding a level, to see which way the bubble went.

He had rehearsed it on the walk down, in the courtier's order. *First the fact, then the frame, then what it means.* He had a version that began *You should understand that my uncle* and ended with Ila nodding. He knew what that version bought. It bought a woman who thought she had been told everything.

"I was nine," said Kunal.

His voice came out thin. He started again.

"My father died at the end of summer, of a lung fever. A month after the funeral, in autumn, my uncle came to the nursery and said he would take me to see what was mine." The ice boomed. He waited it out. "We went up the Kuhl ridge. It was a long day. He bought me honey cake at the last farm, and he carried my coat when I got hot."

He stopped, because the next part had a shape he had never said aloud, and his mouth would not make it.

"At the top he gave me a knife and a heel of bread. He said, 'Find your way down.' I said, 'Which way?' and he said, 'That is the whole lesson.' Then he walked off along the ridge, and I watched him get small."

"Highness," said Ila.

"Let me." He held up a bandaged hand. "I'll lose it if I stop."

She said nothing more.

"He said one more thing before he went. He didn't shout it. He said it like a man reading out a price. *Whoever knows the way owns the people.*" Kunal looked at the dark lake. "I had no route. It came on to snow at dusk, and I sat under a rock and held the knife, and I was not brave about it."

"How did you get down?"

It was a surveyor's question, practical and low. It steadied him as nothing else could have.

"Goats." He half laughed. "In the morning I heard a bell. A nanny and eleven others, going down to the winter fold, and she knew the way in her feet. I followed her for two days. On the first night a herd boy gave me a cheese rind and didn't ask my name. I walked into Garh Neshar at dusk on the second day. My uncle was at the table. He said, 'Well?' and I said, 'I came home.'"

The wind moved over the ice, and the seam boomed again, farther out.

"I kept every way after that. A map can be burned. A guide can be bought. A route-keeper can be hung. Whatever's in your head can't be taken. So I carry the Stair and the Moon and the Ladder, and the Eye, where he took me at twelve and turned me round, and I give them to nobody. It's why I'm alive." He breathed out. "It's why the hill council has followed me for eight winters, and why none of them has the whole of it. I tell them to be open, Ila. I stand up and tell them. And I've never once opened my own hand."

She said, very quietly, "You think you're him."

"I think the one thing I took from that day was his lesson, and I kept it too well." His voice cracked on it. He let it. "I'm afraid I'm his best pupil. I'm afraid that if I say a way aloud to you, I'll start counting what it buys. I counted at the Eye. I'm counting now, and I can't find the sum."

He had never said any of it. It lay on the stone between them, and nothing he could think of would pick it up again.

"I don't know what telling you gets me," said Kunal. "That's why I'm telling you."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


For a long moment she did not move.

Then she crossed the pace and a half. She stood at his shoulder, not touching him, and looked out where he looked, at the pale lake and the cliff beyond.

"You found the way," said Ila.

He turned his head.

"A boy of nine. No map, no guide, a knife and a heel of bread. You found it by listening for a bell, and you were home in two days." She said it in the flat, exact voice she used for distances. "He walked off along a ridge and left you the whole mountain to cross. He owned nothing. He owned a table and a chair, and a nine-year-old who got home without him."

He had no answer. His throat had closed.

"The goats owned more of that road than he did," she went on. "And the thing you're afraid of." She paused, choosing the words one at a time. "Garh store silver is punched with the store-bell in one corner. Clipping the corner takes off the crown-held stores' mark, so no coin could be traced to the Garh treasury, or to you. You paid nineteen silvers with the corners clipped, so the medicine in Ranu could not be traced to your hand. A man who does that in secret for two winters, with no one to thank him, is not Pritam. Pritam would have put his name on the sack."

The Drum boomed. Kunal was shaking, finely, in the shoulders, as a horse does after a fall.

"I didn't think anyone would work out why they were clipped," he said.

"I'm a surveyor. I check my closures." Her face in the starlight was grave and open. "Nobody has told you that, have they?"

"No."

"Then I'm the first, and it's measured."

She did not reach for him. She stood at his shoulder and let the shaking run. Slowly his shoulders quieted. Only when they had stopped did she turn.

"I've watched you for twelve days," said Ila. "You carry my pencil leads against your chest so they won't snap. I said it once, to the air, and you heard. You clip your coins so no one hangs for your medicine." She took her hands out of her sleeves. "I want you."

She held them up between them, bare, palms toward him, the fingers slightly curled. She was not holding them out to be taken. She was showing him.

They had been in the cold air for half an hour. In the starlight the tips were pink, the colour of blood close under skin.

He looked at her hands, as a bound man does. Warm. Not a shade paler. They stayed warm while he looked.

"I'd like you to know I said it with these showing," she said. Her voice was not entirely steady. "I'm not very good at this. I'd rather be checked."

"Ila."

"I'm thirty-one. I've known it since the lake. Possibly before, on the Stair, but I hadn't the numbers." One corner of her mouth went up. "Say something, please, because my hands are going to freeze."

He laughed. It cracked in the middle and became something else. He took her hands in his bandaged ones and held them, and they were warm, and he had never held a better piece of evidence.

"Kunal," she said.

Not *Highness*, the word she still kept for distance. His own name, and nothing brisk in it at all. He had to look out at the lake a moment before he could look back.

"Say it again," said Kunal.

"Kunal." She stepped closer. "I'd like to go to the hut, if you want that too. If you don't, I'll sit here with you till the Drum stops, and that's true as well."

"I want it," he said. "In twenty-four years of counting, I've never wanted anything so plainly."

The rope lay slack across the stone between their boots, eight spans of undyed wool, and for once neither of them was thinking of it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The herders' hut stood on the scree above the Drum, a steep climb from the shore. Dhanu's people used it in summer for the high grazing, and tonight it was cold.

Ila did not go in. She stood in the doorway and turned toward the black shoulder of the Kuhl, sighting along her thumb. Four men sat up there with a glass. Kunal waited, and did not ask.

"The spur," she said at last. "The scree spur is square across the line. They can't see the door." She looked at the smoke hole. "They could see a flame through that. So no flame."

She had him bank a single coal in the chulha and cover it with an upturned clay pot, a finger's gap at the rim for air. It gave no heat and no tea. The hut stayed as cold as the night outside it. That was the price, and she paid it without complaint.

Her coat came off and hung on the peg. She unpicked three stitches of the lining, drew out a folded square of cream paper, and slipped it into the hem of her pattu. Her needle went fast and hard. He read in it that she would not leave the paper anywhere it could be found unasked, even by him, and she did not look at him while she sewed. Then she untied the rope from both their wrists and laid it on the floor, because the knots were for the stone, and the stone was not what she was thinking of.

He sat back on his heels and found her watching him.

"I should say the practical thing," she said. "I want this, and you can say no at any point and I'll stop. I'd like the same." She blew out her breath. "I find it easier to put it as a rule."

"Then it's a rule." He stood. "No at any point. A word, and we stop, and I'll find a way to warm tea."

"With a covered coal." Ila was smiling. "You'd try, too."

She came the last step. He kissed her, and for a moment the cold went out of his hands. The dry warmth of her mouth, the cold edge of her nose, the way her fingers closed in the front of his coat and pulled: he took in all of it. When he lifted his head she followed him a little way, and said, "Again," and he did.

His bandaged hands were clumsy on the toggles of her coat. She laughed against his jaw and undid them herself, and then undid his, one by one. She unwound the strips from his palms and knuckles and left the wrappings on his fingertips, where the skin had split raw. Then she kissed each knuckle above the dressings, and laid the loose wrappings on the shelf in a neat row.

"Oh," said Kunal, to the roof.

"You're hurt."

"Not where it matters. That's the trouble."

Her pattu came off her shoulders. Beneath it she wore the thick grey wool of a woman who had crossed three lakes, and she smelled of snow and goat fat and old smoke. He found the laces at her collar. He looked up for leave, and she nodded once, and he undid them. She did the same for him, with her mouth set seriously, and put her palm flat over his heart and held it there.

"That's quick," said Ila.

"Yes."

"Good." She said it fiercely. "Mine is too."

They went down onto the shelf and the single blanket together. The one red slit under the pot lit her from the side: the long line of her shoulder, the freckles below her collarbone, the white scar along one rib where a chain had once whipped back. He kissed it. She said "Ow," and "Don't stop," in the same breath, and he didn't.

In a hundred cold rooms he had thought desire was a thing you managed. A debt, a trade. Not this. She slid her hands down his back and he arched, and she said, shaky and delighted, "There?" and he said "There."

When she drew him over her she held his face between her hands and stopped.

"Say it with me. Yes?"

"Yes," said Kunal. "You?"

"Yes." Her mouth shook. "Come here."

It was slow, because she wanted it slow, and he found that he did, too. The clay pot ticked as it cooled. She breathed his name into his neck and again against his mouth, and her hand found his and laced their fingers beside her head.

Somewhere in it she stopped counting.

He knew the moment. She had been breathing in sets, a habit he had seen on every climb, in threes and fours. Then the sets broke, and she gasped, and laughed in surprise at herself, and held on. She went over first with his name in her throat. He followed, with his face in her hair and her heart going under his.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The coal had sunk to a thin red line when she shivered.

She had gone to the end of the shelf for water and come back with her feet bare. She tucked them under the blanket, and they met his shin like two stones taken out of a river.

"Ila. You're frozen."

"They're always cold. It's nothing."

"Is that a small lie?" He looked at her hands. Pink. "No. It's true. Come here."

She let him pull her feet into his lap. He wrapped his hands round the left, then the right, and rubbed the arches slowly with his thumbs, the raw tips of his fingers held clear. They were long, narrow, slightly callused at the heel, and so cold they made his palms ache. She went rigid for a breath. Then she let her head fall back against the wall and gave a long, helpless sigh.

"No one has done that," said Ila, in a different voice.

"Never?"

"My mother, when I was small." She laughed, shakily, with her eyes shut. "I keep them under me in the field. I put them in a bag on the Stair. I don't show them. It's absurd. They're feet."

"They're feet," he agreed. "And it's you telling me where you're soft. I'll take it as given."

"Ila."

"Mm."

"I'll give you a name a night," said Kunal. "From now on. One of ours, one of the hill's, true, as I know it. We've traded so far. A name for a question. I'd like to stop that." He kept his eyes on her foot. "I'll ask nothing back. Not an answer, not a line. You can draw it or burn it, and I'll know I gave it freely."

As he said it his eyes went, unbidden, to the pattu lying by the pot, and the hem where he had watched her sew the paper in. *Not an answer.* It covered that too. He saw it plainly enough, and let it stand.

She sat up. Her feet stayed in his lap, but her face had changed. It was the one she wore over a closure that would not come out.

"Then hear the price first," she said. "Out loud, so neither of us can say we didn't. You say that across the rope and it's a promise. A promise made while bound and broken before the thaw strikes you like a great lie. Your hands first, then the rest." She held up a second finger. "And every name you give me, I have drawn for the crown for eleven years. I haven't decided what I'll do with them. Each one could be a line on the queen's map by spring, with your hill's name on it, and you'd have put it there."

"I know both," said Kunal.

"Say you do."

"I know both. I'd rather lose the fingers than keep counting." He heard how that sounded and did not take it back. "Tie it on, and I'll say it again."

She looked at him a long moment. Then she reached for the rope on the floor. She knotted the end round his left wrist, flat and snug, and he tied hers, and the wool warmed between them.

"I'll ask nothing back," said Kunal.

The rope hummed against his wrist, low and level, with no climbing in it.

Her hand came down and lay over his on her ankle. "Which is tonight's?"

He knew which. It was the one he had held back longest, and it surprised him how little it weighed.

"The black crag you named for the healer." She went still. "We call it the Widow's Knuckle. For the hand a widow laid on the rock while she waited for her husband. It's older than the Stair. Write it down however you like."

"Kunal," said Ila.

"It's yours."

She slid down against him and put her face in his neck, and her cold feet against his calves. He pulled the blanket up over both of them and held her, and for the first time in twenty-four years, he did not count.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He woke before dawn, with the hut dark and grey at the edges, and frost furring the rim of the clay pot.

She was awake. She lay on her side with her head on her arm, in the pattu, watching him. How long, he could not tell. Her eyes were very steady.

"You were speaking," said Ila.

"What did I say?"

"Names. In hill speech. I didn't write any of them."

He smiled and reached out. She let him touch her cheek, and it was warm. The pattu had slipped where its hem hung over the shelf's edge, and a corner of cream paper showed through a loosened stitch. Her hand lay over it, flat and still.

She saw him see it. She left her hand where it was.

Whatever it was, she had carried it since the Meadow Cairn. He remembered the shelf, and *I'll find out in daylight, from you.* He had set that down on purpose.

"Ila," said Kunal.

"Yes."

The question was already in his mouth. *What does it say?* He drew breath for it, and the cold arrived at once. It came into the raw tips of his fingers like the first bite of a long night on the ice, and the rope at his wrist went tight and still. He had said *nothing back*. He had said it with the knot on, and he had known what it covered.

He closed his hand on the blanket and swallowed the question whole.

"You're awake early," he said instead. The chill drew off his fingers slowly, as a tide goes out.

She had seen his fist close. She looked at it, and then at his face, and something crossed hers that he could not read.

"I was thinking." Her voice was calm. "About a number. It won't come out."

She lay watching him in the grey, and her hand did not leave her hem.

# Chapter 21: A Promise with a Date

Gauri knocked with the flat of her hand, twice, as if the door might be asleep.

Ila had been up for an hour. She sat on the floor of the herders' hut with a field sheet pinned under her knee and a pencil lead warming in her fist, and the rope lay slack across the boards to the shelf where Kunal slept with one arm flung over his eyes. She had spent the grey hours counting roof slates, and the count had come out the same every time.

"Open," said Gauri through the planks. "I've bread, and I've news, and I'm not carrying either back down."

Kunal was awake before the bolt was drawn. He sat up with the blanket at his waist and his face already shut, the courtier's face, and Ila watched him put it on like a coat.

Gauri came in smelling of cold. She set the bread on the shelf, looked once at the two of them and once at the rope, and said nothing about any of it. It was a kind of courtesy Ila was beginning to value above speeches.

"Dhanu's boy was out on the north shore at first light," she said. "Two pairs. Four men, past the Eye on the far side of the basin."

"Raghu's?" said Kunal.

"Grey wool, no blue cord. The boy says they walk like men who've been told to be quiet." She drew a line in the air. "Tracks along the west side of the Eye, close under the cliff. Fresh. They've been looking at the water."

Ila's pencil had stopped. "How far from the cleft?"

"A morning's walk. Less, if they stop looking at the water."

*Sixteen men bound,* Ila had thought last night, over the soup, and had not said it. Here was the first four, small as a pencil mark on a sheet.

"They'd have come down the Kuhl shoulder," she said. "It's the only way onto the west shore without crossing the ice." She heard herself, brisk and exact, and found it a comfort. "If they hold it, they see the whole basin. The Ladder's foot, the path to the lake huts, and the slope under this hut."

"Dhanu says the same," said Gauri, with a short nod, as if Ila had passed another inspection. "He wants you both told before the sun's up the cliff. After that he'll ask what you mean to do."

She went. The door banged. The bread steamed on the shelf, and neither of them touched it.

"Tonight," said Kunal. "The cleft is the one place four men with spyglasses can't follow." He rubbed his face with a bandaged hand. "Draw me what's behind us. Dhanu will need to know which ways to shut."

It was the sort of request she could do in her sleep. She had walked every one of those ways with a chain.

"I'll draw it," said Ila.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She drew the exits.

The Widow's Ladder came first, because it was the biggest, with its four pitches and its hundred and sixty steps. Then the shore path along the Mirror, with the lakeside cairn at nine hundred and ten paces from the scree edge. Then the foot trail from the lower huts. Then the scree slope where they sat now, steep and loose, with a sheep track going up it that no one had ever bothered to name. Last came the cleft at the head of the Eye, as far as she had seen it, which was as far as a dropped spike would fall in a count of three.

The sheet came out true. The line was what her feet had made it, and the numbers sat beside it in her small square hand, bearings and paces and the fall of the ground.

Halfway through the Kuhl shoulder she saw what she was making.

Every line on it was a door. Every bearing was a key. Raghu's four men would pay dearly for this sheet, and the crown might do anything with a copy. It was also excellent work. The closures were tight. There was not one wrong figure on it.

*A map is only true or false.* She had said it to herself for eleven years, like a woman checking a latch. She looked at the shore path, sharp as a hair on the paper, and for the first time the sentence did not close.

"You've stopped," said Kunal.

He had come to sit against the opposite wall, with his bandaged hands round a bowl of tea. He had eaten the bread. He watched her draw, as he always did, with the open, slightly stunned look of a man watching someone do a thing he can't.

"I'm checking a closure," said Ila.

"Does it close?"

"Yes." She turned the sheet toward him. "That's the trouble."

His eyes went along the Ladder and the shore and the cleft, and she saw him name each place in his head, in the old speech. He kept his hands round the bowl and said none of them aloud.

"It's very good," he said.

"It's the best sheet I've made in eleven years." She tried a laugh, and it came out short. "And if it goes the wrong way, it opens the whole basin like a purse."

"So don't let it go the wrong way."

"Which way is that?" said Ila.

He opened his mouth and shut it. She watched him decide not to say the neat thing.

She took her pattu from the peg and turned the hem out. A corner of cream paper still showed through the loosened stitch. She mended that first, small and flat. Then she unpicked the lining along the side seam, above the hem, laid the new sheet against the wool with the three older ones from the Stair and the Moon, and began to sew them in. The queen's letter slept in the hem below.

Kunal watched her stitches go in and said nothing. He had asked her for the exits, not the sheets. It would have been simpler to hand him the paper. She didn't.

"Ila," he said.

She knew from the weight in his voice what was coming. It had been in the room since dawn, with the hem and her hand over it.

"Raghu's four don't need my letter to find a cleft," she said, before he could go on. "They've seen its shadow from the shoulder. Whatever the letter says, it changes nothing about tonight's way. I'm telling you so you don't have to ask."

"I know," said Kunal.

"Then what are you going to ask?"

He set the bowl down. "I promised you I'd ask nothing back. I'm going to break it, and I want you to hear why first." He drew a breath. "You've carried that letter since Garh Neshar, and last night you sewed it into your hem. The crown put something in your hands about my country, and I gave you the Eye without knowing what. I can't stop counting what's in it. I'd rather pay than go on counting."

He held out both hands, fingers spread, in the light from the door. They were bandaged to the knuckle, and the tips were pink.

"Kunal. You promised."

"I know what I promised."

"Then you know what it does to your hands." Her voice had gone hard, and she let it. "That's you counting again. You've set a price on the table so I have to look at it. If I refuse, I watch you pay. If I answer, you've bought it with your fingers. It's the thing you told me you were afraid of, and you're doing it to me."

"I know." He met her eyes. "I'd still rather you were angry."

"What did the letter say?"

The cold came at once. She saw it arrive. The pink in his right fingertips went waxy, then grey, as if a hand had wiped the blood out of them. The rope at his wrist drew taut and still. His breath went out in a short, hard push, and under the bandages the skin lifted in blisters, fat and clear, and the grey beneath them did not change.

He did not drop his hands. He held them where she could see, shaking a little, and kept his eyes on her face.

"I'll pay it," said Kunal.

She was on her knees before she knew she had moved, her hand out to take his. He drew them back against his chest.

"Don't. It's paid." His voice was rough. "Take it onto yourself and you'll be climbing with them as well. I need one of us whole on stone tonight."

Her hand hung in the air between them. A broken promise struck like a great lie, and a great lie killed flesh. Those tips were gone, and nothing she did now would bring them back.

Slowly she drew her hand in and set it on her knee. It was the hardest thing she had done since the Ladder.

The question had been coming since the Mirror. The answer had waited since the Drum, folded small. *It says what the crown wants of this mountain, and what the crown owes it, and a name I know.* All of it was true. She could say all of it. Saying it would not mend his fingers. It would lead straight to the gloves on the stone, and to Neshar's part in what the stone meant.

"I'm not going to tell you," said Ila.

The hum did not rise. Her own fingertips stayed pink.

His face went still, and then it opened, a little. What showed under it was a boy of nine sitting under a rock with a knife, who had asked which way and been told that was the whole lesson.

"Right," he said. "That's yours to refuse."

"It's not about you."

"I know."

"It's not." She heard her voice go up. "It's mine. And an oath I swore before this crossing. I took a commission from the archive, and it binds my silence on what their papers hold. If I break it, I'm a clerk who sold the crown's drawer." She pressed the needle into the wool. "That's *one* reason."

"What's the other?"

"I don't know yet." It came out of her like something torn. "I don't know what it does to you."

He nodded slowly and laid his ruined hands on his knees, where she could still see them. He asked nothing more. That was the worst of it. He had swallowed the question once before, in the grey light, and now he had asked it and paid, and was swallowing the answer with nothing at all in his face.

She could not bear it.

He had held her weight at the Eye, and his fingertips had burst on the rope. She had said *I want you* on the Drum shore, with her hands out. She had not known you could lie warm in a man's arms all night and still keep a locked room in the same house.

"When we're through the mountain," said Ila.

He lifted his head.

"When we're through." She held the sheet against her ribs, and the words were out before she had checked them against anything. "Through the cleft, and the Eye, and whatever's under it. I'll tell you everything in it. The whole of it, and the oath be damned."

The rope hummed.

It ran through the bone of her wrist, low and level, steady as a held note. It did not climb. It was the sound of a thing meant, and she had made it, and it would not be unmade.

Kunal had gone white about the mouth. "Ila. A promise, with the bond on. You know what a miss would do."

"I know."

"You don't have to—"

"I'd like to," said Ila, and found it was true, which was the most alarming part.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She told him the rest because it was the same sum she did over any survey, and he deserved to see it written.

"There's a date on it," she said. "I haven't chosen it. The bond ends when meltwater runs at the cairn where we tied it, and that's the Meadow Cairn. South-facing shoulder. Soft weather." She tapped her field book. "I have eleven winters of its thaw dates. They spread wide. A hard year gives me weeks. A soft year gives me fewer, and nobody on this hill can say which one this is."

"And if it runs before we're through?"

"Then the blue light goes out of the cleft, and the path under the Eye closes to us like any lone crosser's. The promise lapses the same hour. Not kept. Only ended, with the words still in my mouth and the oath still on me." She made herself say the last of it. "You'd be standing in the dark at the head of the Eye, with five dead fingertips, holding a refusal and a debt I couldn't pay."

It was the first time in her life she had made a promise with a date on it. She sewed the last sheet in. Her hands were steady, and it was a very long way down.

Kunal was looking at his fingers, turning them in the light from the door. The grey had not crept anywhere. It had stayed, and the blisters had settled over it like glass. He closed his hand slowly, and it closed, but the last joints went down a beat behind the rest.

"The cleft is stone and ice," he said, to the hand. "I'll need those on it tonight."

"Then we'll rope the hard moves," said Ila. "I'll lead them. You'll hold with the palm."

"Yes." He flexed it again. Then he looked up, and the courtier was nowhere in his face. "Dhanu wanted till dusk to see what Raghu's four did. I'm not waiting for that."

"Kunal—"

"I was going tonight for Raghu. To get ahead of him." He shook his head. "It's your date now. Hard year or soft, I'd rather be through the mountain with a week to spare than be right about the weather. We go tonight, and we go for yours."

It was a choice, not a promise. She heard the difference, and so did the rope, which stayed quiet.

"You didn't have to give me a date," he said, lower.

"It has one whether I name it or not," said Ila.

He laughed once, without sound. Then he held out his right hand, palm up, the fingers curled away from her, and she put the pattu aside and took it by the palm. It was warm. The dead tips lay against her wrist like cold pebbles, and she held the living part of him and let them lie. The sun came up the cliff and laid a bar of cold gold across the floor.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dhanu came at midmorning, limping, with a whistle round his neck and a waxed cloth full of cheese.

He stopped at the door and looked at Kunal, who stood at the lip of the scree with Gauri, pointing at the far shore, and then at the rope. It ran from Kunal's wrist back across the stones to the hut, eight spans exactly. The whole length was the width of a courtyard.

"He's at the end of his string," said Dhanu.

"Most mornings," said Ila.

"Then he can't hear me, can he, if I speak low."

She understood. She stood, and walked the pattu to the doorframe, and let the rope go taut behind her in a long straight line. Kunal looked round once. She nodded. He turned back to the shore, and the wind took what was left.

Dhanu set the cheese on the sill. He did not sit. The limp had worn the heel of his right boot to a slant.

"My boy says the four on the west shore aren't looking at the lake any more," he said. "They're looking at the cliff." He rubbed his leg. "It'll be tonight, then, as the prince says."

"Yes."

"And when you're through, and you've seen what he'll show you, and you go back down with your lead and your string." He said it carefully, as a man sets down a full bucket. "What will you do with the map?"

Ila looked at the pattu on its peg. She could feel the sheets in it, like a second spine.

"Draw it true," she said.

It was the answer she had given the survey office, and the archive, and herself in the dark for eleven years. It came easily. It was solid as a surveyor's stone.

Dhanu waited a moment. He was not unkind about it.

"True for who?" he said.

The wind came over the scree. Out on the lake the ice spoke once, a long note, and the whole basin answered it.

She opened her mouth, and the word she wanted was not there. There was only the lined shore, and a small yellow star in a margin, and a woman at a table in Taravan with a key she had never been given.

Ila said nothing. She laid her hand flat on the hem of the pattu, over the stitches and the paper under them, and the ice sang again across the dark water.

# Chapter 22: Walnuts

Dhanu put a walnut in Kunal's palm and folded the dead fingers over it himself, one at a time, like a man closing a purse.

"Feel that?"

"No," said Kunal.

"Then look at it."

Kunal looked. The shell was dark and ordinary, with a red thread wound twice round the seam and knotted off. Dhanu laid a second one beside it. They sat just inside the herders' door, with the scree spur at their backs hiding it from the far shore. Between their boots a clay pot stood upside down over a single banked coal. It gave off no flame, no smoke, and a small orange line at the rim.

"My word without a walnut isn't from me," said Dhanu. "You know the rule."

"I made half the council swear to it."

"You taught it to them. My people made it, ten generations back. Different thing." He lowered himself onto the upturned stool with the stiff right foot first, the left swinging after. "A boy comes with a walnut, it's mine. A boy comes without, I never said it, I was asleep, I'm a lame man with a bad leg. Even if it's my own runner. Even if it's you at my door, doing my voice."

"Why two?"

"One for when you're stupid." Dhanu's face did not move. "I brought a second because I've known you thirty years."

Kunal laughed, and it hurt somewhere under the ribs. He shut the dead hand on the walnut. Behind him the hut was dark. Ila slept on the shelf in her boots, because they went into the Eye at moondown. The rope lay across the boards, untied, a long pale line from his place at the door to her wrist. Eight spans. He had paced it twice without meaning to.

"Send one, and I come," said Dhanu. "I don't ask why. Not till after."

"Thank you."

"Don't. It's a bad bargain. Half the time you'll be wrong." He held his hands over the pot's warmth. "Now say it."

"Say what?"

"What you've decided. You've been decided since noon. You sat on that scree with your jaw set and your mouth shut tight as a fist."

Kunal watched the orange line. He thought of the sheet Ila had drawn that morning, with the shore path on it, thin and certain as a goat track across new snow.

"I'm taking her through the princes' way," he said. "All of it. Every turn I know."

Dhanu was quiet for a time.

"No," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"I thought you'd say that," said Kunal.

"Then you thought right, and you can stop there." Dhanu never raised his voice, and he didn't now. He leaned in. "Listen, because I'll say it once and then again. She's the crown's surveyor. Her commission has the queen's own words in it. *The crown may hold every way.* She read it to you herself."

"She said she doesn't know what holding means."

"I know what it means. A garrison means it. A garrison is a house with soldiers in it, and the house goes where the way goes." He tapped the stool with one finger. "You give her the princes' way, and she draws it, and she's honest, so it's true. Then it sits in a drawer in Taravan, with a key you'll never be given. And one winter a captain opens that drawer and sends two hundred men through the mountain in a night. Can the villages shut a door against that?"

It was a fair argument. It was better than the one Kunal had made himself on the long walk from the lake, and he took his time answering it.

"The hills have kept that way sixty winters, and more," said Dhanu. "Your grandfather kept it. Your father kept it. They didn't keep it because they were mean, Kunal. It's the one door we have."

"And my uncle kept it for the same reason, he'd say."

"Your uncle kept it for a throne."

"He kept it exactly as you describe. In one man's head, with nobody to check him." Kunal turned the walnut over. "That's the part I can't get round. A door only one person can open isn't ours. It's his."

"That's a speech."

"Then here's a fact." He said it flat, the way Ila would. "The crown's cover sheet to the council names a way beneath the Shilak Cairn. I'd never heard of any such way. Raghu's men have dug nine holes round the door cairn on the Hollow Moon, looking for a carved marker that was never there. They're hunting the princes' way with picks. And the queen put a letter in Ila's hem that she won't show me, and she wouldn't have sewn it in so carefully if it didn't matter." He opened his hand. "It's already out, old friend. Somebody in Taravan knows it exists. Somebody in the hills is paid to dig. All that's left to argue about is whether the one person on this mountain who lied to my face once and owned it by dawn gets the true line, or whether Raghu draws a bad one and sells it."

"Secrecy's failed, you're saying."

"It failed before I was born. I only noticed."

The Drum boomed on the lake, a long note that rolled up the scree and through the planks of the hut. Neither of them looked round.

"It's a good case," said Dhanu at last. "I still say no."

"I know you do. I'm glad. If you didn't, I'd be doing this because it was easy."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Dhanu lifted the pot with one finger, checked the coal, and set it down again. When he spoke, he had changed the ground, which he did when a quarrel was done and the work was not.

"Tell me the petition."

So Kunal told it. He had rehearsed it on the scree, with the wind taking half of it, and it came out smoother than he liked. "When the thaw council sits at the Shilak Cairn, I stand up and ask two things of the crown. First, no garrisons in the hill villages. Not on the Stair, not on the Moon, not in any fold the council names. If a warden wants a hearing, he asks the council, and the council answers."

"And second?"

"A council seat at Taravan. One voice of ours in the queen's own hall, with a right to speak before any order touches the passes." He heard how it sounded in the open air, and it still sounded good. "Neshar a self-governing valley under the crown. Not outside it. I don't want a war, and she can't afford one. This gives her the passes safely open, and it gives us a door she can't close from the capital without our hearing."

"It's a fine petition."

"But."

"But they'll ask." Dhanu did not look away. "Old Bhim will stand up first, because he always does. And he won't ask whether it's fair. He'll ask what you gave the crown for it. A queen doesn't hand over a seat in her hall for a speech."

Kunal had known that question was waiting, as one knows the next stair in the dark. He had put off stepping onto it.

"I'll tell them the truth," he said. "That I showed a crown surveyor the way. That it was mine to show. That I gave it before I knew what she'd do, and the council can hold me to it."

"They'll hold you to it, all right." Dhanu said it without heat. "Some will say you sold the hills. Some will say you were a fool in love, and that'll be the kinder half. Let me tell you what none of them will say, because none of them have watched you as long as I have."

"Go on."

"You have never given anyone anything you couldn't take back."

It dropped into the quiet like a stone down a well.

"That's not true," said Kunal. "The silver—"

"Clipped at the corner so no one could trace it to you. Nineteen bits, and you never once said your own name to the woman who ate because of them. The names you give the council, you give in pieces, so none of them holds the whole." Dhanu lifted the sheep-knuckles from his belt and let them lie in his palm. "Seven winters I taught you the bones. You played me every game and never once let me win, nor lost on purpose. You played so it couldn't cost you."

"I broke a promise to her this morning. I paid for it."

"You broke it so you could stop counting, and you paid in flesh you'd already priced." Dhanu closed his hand. "This one's different. You put a way in her head, there's no clipping it back. She can't forget it. You can't have it back. If she's the crown's, it's the crown's, and the hills pay."

Kunal sat very still.

"I know it," he said.

"Do you?"

"I've known it since the Eye, when I was twelve and a man turned me round by the shoulders." He looked at his bandaged hands. "That's why I want to. If I can't take it back, then I've finally given it. Not lent it. Not parcelled it, or priced it. I want to find out what I am without the lock on."

Dhanu studied him a long time. The limp was in the way he sat, the right foot planted and the left held out, as though even at rest he were walking round something.

"The council will want your head."

"They can have a hearing."

"And if she draws it and takes it down the mountain and hands it over?"

"Then I was wrong, and the hills will say so, and I'll stand in the hall and hear it." He said it slowly. "I'd rather be wrong that way than right in the way I've been."

Dhanu grunted. It was the sound he made when a thing was settled and he had no intention of praising it. He heaved himself up, and the left foot swung out and round.

"Ask her what she'll do with it. Not before. After. I'll say that without a walnut, so you needn't take it from me."

"I'll take it anyway."

"You're stupid enough already." He went off down the scree with the whistle swinging on its thong, and the dark folded behind him.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Kunal stayed by the pot until the sound of him was gone.

There was a third walnut in his coat. He had carried it since the day the council fixed the thaw date, in the lining over his ribs, wrapped in a scrap of grey wool. It was plain and dry, with no thread on it. He took it out without asking himself why he had brought it.

Mini had asked for one. That was all. Six years old on the pony in front of Gauri, with her fist in the horse's mane, she had said, *Uncle Kunal, can I have a walnut with a string, like the runners? I want my own.* And he had said yes and, by the stones, had said *When I'm back.* It had been a light thing to say, the way men say it to children. He had not thought of it again until last night, when he lay awake with Ila's cold feet against his shin and did not count.

A hank of red thread lay in his pouch. It was Gauri's, a gift from the winter before. He pulled out a length with his teeth, because the fingers would not pinch it, and laid it round the seam.

The dead tips of his right hand sat on the shell like the grey ends of burned candles. He could see them move. He could not feel what they held. The thread slid, caught, and slid out again, and the first knot came out a lump the size of a seed.

He undid it with his teeth, and began again.

It was a simple knot. A runner's turn, a loop, a tuck. He had tied it ten thousand times, in the dark, in a gale, with half a hand frozen. The second try came out lopsided, the thread riding up off the seam and leaving the walnut naked on one side. He pulled it loose and sat a moment with the thread dangling.

Pinku came to mind, in the walnut grove at Ranu at moonrise, last winter, with a clay lamp too big for his fist. Kunal had tied that one in a hurry, by lantern light, with all ten fingers. It had been the best knot he knew.

The third try he did slowly. He held the walnut against his knee, trapped the thread under the heel of his hand, and made each turn with the whole arm instead of the fingers. The knot came out round and fat and slightly off true, with an ugly tail he could not cut straight. It held. He tugged it, and it did not slip.

It was the worst knot he had tied since he was eight.

He sat with it on his knee, smiling, stupidly, at a nut.

"Mini will like it," said Ila, from the shelf.

He had not heard her wake. She sat up with her hair flat on one side and her boots laced, and the rope slack across the boards. It had stayed within its eight spans the whole night. She looked at the walnut, and then at his hand, and said nothing about the fingers, which was her own kind of manners.

"It's bad," said Kunal.

"It's very bad. It's lopsided." She crossed and sat on the stool where Dhanu had been, close enough that the coal lit one side of her face. "She'll show it to everyone."

"That's the idea."

"Is it from you?"

"It's from me." He turned it so she could see the tail. "No one else could make it so ugly."

She laughed, short and sudden and loud, and it rang off the scree like a dropped pan. Kunal's head went round to the doorway before he could stop it. Beyond the spur the far shore lay dark and still. Out on the lake the ice answered her. Nothing else did.

Ila had already lowered her voice. "Sorry," she said, and held out her palm, and he put the walnut in it. She took the tail between two fingers, drew the pencil knife from her sleeve, and cut it off square at the knot, one stroke, without asking. She tucked the scrap of red in her sleeve and gave the walnut back. The tail was clean now, and the knot sat round and fat and honest under it.

He put it in his coat, over the heart, in its wool.

The coal had sunk to a dull orange. The moon was low behind the cliff, and above the Eye the stars stood thick and cold. Raghu's four were somewhere on the far shore, with their spyglasses and their patience. In an hour the line would show, blue, to the two of them and no one else.

Kunal looked at the woman beside him. She had a pencil lead in her sleeve and a letter in her hem and eleven years of true sheets in her head. He did not know what she meant to do with the way. He had not asked, and her one promise covered a letter, not this. He meant to give it before he heard her answer.

"Ila."

"Yes."

"I'm taking you into the cleft tonight." He set his hands open on his knees, and the words came out whole, with nothing kept back. "The whole way I know, every turn, in order. And then past it. Past the place where my uncle turned me round. I've never been there. We'll find out together what's on the far side of it."

She said nothing. The wind came down off the shoulder and slid round the spur. He watched her face go still, the way it did over a closure that would not come out. The pencil lead in her fingers had stopped mid-roll, and under her breath, so low he almost missed it, she began to count paces.

# Chapter 23: Seven Breaths

The cleft breathed on her before she had the lamp lit.

A slow, even draught, cold enough to ache in the teeth, came out of the dark at the head of the Eye as if the mountain had turned over in its sleep. Ila shuttered the lamp to a slit. Behind her the lake lay black and still. On the far shore, four men with spyglasses were watching a cliff.

"Blue," said Kunal.

She had been looking too high. She looked low. Along the foot of the left wall, where the ice met the stone, small stacked stones ran into the cleft, one every few paces, each with a pale blue glow. The glow was no brighter than breath on glass.

"I see it."

"I didn't. Last time." He stood with the rope slack between them and his bandaged hands held a little away from his coat. "I stood here at twelve and there was nothing. Only a man behind me saying it was dark and I'd been brave."

She did not say anything kind. He would have heard the effort in it.

"Walk," said Ila. "I'll count."

They went in.

The walls were ice over stone, and the lamp lit them from inside, so that they walked down a throat of blue glass. Water ticked somewhere ahead. Each drop came alone, like a clerk tapping a pen. She counted her paces under her breath, because counting was what her feet did when her head wanted somewhere to stand. Eighty. Eighty-one.

The wall on the left came in. The passage doubled back on itself like a hairpin, the stone slick and black at the bend, and Kunal stopped dead with the rope drawn out behind him.

"Here," he said.

He was not asking. She waited. His breath smoked in the lamp slit.

"He turned me by the shoulders. Like this." His hands stayed in his coat. "He said, 'The rest is for a prince who's earned it.' Then he walked me out. I counted his steps all the way to the shore, so I'd know them."

"Kunal."

"There's a song." He said it to the hairpin, not to her. "He taught it to me that day, so I'd have it when I'd earned it. I have sung it to myself for twenty-one winters and never once walked it." He drew a breath, let it go, and sang very low, in a child's flat tune.

*First the turn that turns you home,*
*then seven breaths and none to keep,*
*then the stone that weeps and weeps,*
*step on the wet where the water sleeps.*

"It ends there," he said. "Past that, nothing. He never gave me the rest."

Ila looked at the hairpin. Her eyes had walked into it already. "That's not an order," she said.

"What?"

"*The turn that turns you home.* You've heard it all your life as 'go home.'" She put her gloved hand on the bend. "It's a bearing. The passage turns you round, and you face the way you came. It doubles back, so you're facing home and walking away from it."

He stared at the stone. Then he laughed, very short and not happy. "All my life."

"Twenty-one years. You were twelve." She held out the lamp. "Go on. You're past your furthest point by a step, and I'd like you to be the one who takes the first."

He took it. The rope paid out behind him around the corner. She followed, and the blue stones went with them.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The slot began forty paces on, a crack in the wall the width of a man's shoulders sideways and no more. It ran away into a dark the lamp could not enter. Cold air came out of it. Kunal put his face to it and took it in like a man smelling bread.

"Seven breaths," he said. "That's what the song means. Long enough that you'd want seven, and you can't have them. The rock's too tight. Breathe in and your chest swells and you stick." He turned to her. "Let it all the way out first. Then don't take another until you're through."

"How long is it?"

"I don't know." He said it plainly, and she respected him for it.

She did the sum anyway. A man's slow walking breath, seven times. The slot was long. "Take the lamp through with you," said Ila. "Set it down on the far side, shuttered. I'd rather cross toward a light than back into one more dark." She tied her pencil case flat inside her coat, where her ribs would shield it. "If you stick, don't fight. Breathe out more, and I'll pull." She went on, because he was not saying it. "If I stick, you do the same."

"I'd have done it anyway."

"I know. I wanted it on the record."

He went in first, the lamp held out before him in one bandaged hand. The rope slid after him in short, hard jerks. She counted them. At seven spans it went slack and then taut again, and one long pull came down it. *Come.*

She breathed out until her ribs met each other. Then she turned her shoulder to the stone and went in.

It was worse than the Ladder. The rock took her front and back, cold through her pattu, and the white scar along her rib dragged over a knob of stone and woke. *One.* Far ahead, a thread of lamplight, no wider than a hair. *Two. Three.* Her lungs began to talk. *Four.* The rope at her wrist pulled steadily, and she let it. *Five.* A black pressure came behind her eyes. *Six.* The stone ground at her breastbone. *Seven.*

Then the stone let go of her chest, and she fell out of the crack onto her hands and knees and breathed in. The air tasted of iron and snow. She had never tasted better.

Kunal had her by the collar, one hand in her pattu and the heel of the other under her arm. She saw that the dead fingers of his right hand would not close.

"Seven," she said, with a gasp that turned into a laugh. "I counted seven. I'd like to write to your uncle. His song's exact."
