"Don't." He was laughing as well, helplessly, with his forehead against the wall. "Don't, he'd be insufferable."

He lifted the lamp from the ledge where he had set it and opened the shutter a hand's width. They stood a moment in a space like the inside of a bell. Somewhere ahead the water ticked faster.

The weeping stone was twenty paces on.

A ledge of black rock lay glazed with ice, and a skin of meltwater slid over it in a thin bright sheet and fell into a runnel below. The runnel went down into a dark with no bottom. A bound pair's winter line crossed the stone, one blue cairn at each end, and the light on the far one was as dim as a candle through a sleeve.

"Step on the wet," said Kunal, "where the water sleeps." He looked at it. "The dry ice is the glaze. The wet is the stone under it."

"One in five," said Ila.

"What is?"

"That the glaze takes a boot." She dealt in odds that way. She meant it as a number, and he took it as one. "I'd put it lower on the wet. I'd put it higher if you kept looking at your feet."

"I won't."

He sang as he went, to keep his eyes up. The tune was the same flat one, a little steadier. His boot came down in the bright skin of water, and it held, and so did the next. On the third step the dead hand slid off the wall. He did not drop. He put the palm flat to the stone and leaned his weight into it, and she saw his mouth go flat with the effort.

He reached the far cairn. The blue light climbed on his face.

She crossed on the wet, with his song in her ears. Her boot held. She came off the ledge into a long dry passage of loose stone, and the blue stones ran away ahead in a thin line, down into the mountain.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"Stop," said Kunal. "I want you to write this."

They had gone on perhaps three hundred paces. She had counted every one, and the passage was wide enough now to walk upright, with a floor of dry gravel that ticked under their boots. Far ahead the meltwater ran in the dark, and the cairn-light made a path of small blue seeds.

"Write what?"

"All of it." He sat down on the gravel with his back to the wall and held his hands open on his knees. "Get the sheet out."

She did, and the pencil lead warmed in her fist. His dead fingertips could not have pinched the pencil from his coat, so she had reached in for it herself, without a word, all the way down.

"First, a rule," said Kunal. "My uncle made it the week my father was buried. No prince shows the way to wife, son or friend. He said the way kills those who share it." He kept his eyes on the gravel, so she would not have to watch him say it. "I told you of the Eye already, and that was a crack in it. Today I'm breaking it with my feet. Here, in front of the jogini, on purpose. Write that I did."

"I'm not your wife."

"No." Something came and went at the corner of his mouth. "Friend will do for the rule. I haven't settled the rest."

She wrote it. Her line shook once, and she let it.

"From the cleft mouth, by your count, and you'll mend me where I'm out," said Kunal. "Eighty-one paces to the turn. It doubles back on the left. Forty to the slot, and the slot is seven breaths, out first. Twenty to the weeping stone, and you step on the wet, the third step is the bad one. Then three hundred and four to here." He looked up. "Ila, he gave me four lines of a song. I've had it all my life. Everything past the stone, I'm learning as we walk it, with you. If I'm out, correct me."

"You're not out." Her pencil went on. "You're not."

"Then it's yours. All of it. Whatever the hills say to me after."

He had not asked what she meant to do with it. She waited for the question, with her hand over the page. A man asks that to buy a thing, and no question came.

"You haven't said what you want for it," said Ila.

"No."

"Why?"

He turned his face up to the lamp. That was not the courtier's face. She had stopped being afraid of that one. This was the other, which looked younger than he was and a good deal more tired.

"Because I want to know what you do with it more than I want to own it," said Kunal.

The cairn hummed. The small stone beside her knee, no higher than her boot, held the note low and level, and her own fingertips were warm when she looked at them.

"That's a bad trade," she said, and her voice came out too light.

"It isn't a trade."

That was the worst of it. She wrote the next figure down and the line shook. She had traded her whole life, a name for a measure, a clause for a clause, and she knew where she stood on every ledger. This was a gift with nothing on the other side of it. She had no column for it.

"I'll draw it," she said.

"I know."

They went on.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The way ended in water.

No lake lay there. The stone fell away into a hollow wide as a hall, with a floor that sloped to a black edge. Beyond the edge the dark drank the lamp. The blue stones went down the slope in a neat line and into the water, and she could see them under it, small and steady, a long way down.

The sound came up cold. The ticking was gone. A slow, great pulse had replaced it, as if the water were breathing.

"It goes under," said Kunal.

"It goes down." She walked to the edge and put the lamp low. The cairns went on beneath the surface, a lit stair into black. "I can't say how far. If the water's still, it could be a pool. If it's moving, it's a river, and we'd need rope longer than twelve spans, and the spirits won't take that."

"Dive it?"

"With nothing but a lamp and a bound partner?" She tried to say it lightly, and managed it. "One in two we'd drown, and I'd rather have worse odds on something I could see."

He nodded slowly. "Then we need stores."

"Bread for a night, and oil for less." She shook the lamp and heard it slosh, half gone. "And a line of days, which we don't have. If we turn now, we're out by dawn. If we push on with half a lamp, we're not out at all."

That was the sum of it. She did it twice, and the sum had a worse line under it. Going back meant the slot again, and the slot had cost her more than the Ladder. She would do it with her scar dragging and a count of seven she did not trust herself to hold twice. The Meadow Cairn faced south, and the thaw was out there somewhere, soft or hard, and a day turned back was a day gone from the weeks she owed him. *If it runs before we're through.* The words sat in her head and would not move.

"We come back," said Kunal.

"We come back."

He sat on the slope with his back to a boulder. His shoulders had stayed up since the cleft, and now they came down. She sat opposite, took out the long sheet, and laid it on her knee under the lamp.

She drew.

The line went down thin and black on pale paper, with every count in its small square figure. The hairpin that turned you home. The slot, with *7 br.* beside it and a mark where the stone dragged her rib. The weeping stone. The gravel passage. The blue cairns marked as a dotted line, and the black hollow at its end, where the dots ran under the water and off the edge of the paper.

She lifted the pencil. Every figure closed. It was the best sheet she had ever made, and she knew by now what that was worth.

Kunal watched her hand, as he always did, with that slightly stunned look.

"Dhanu said to ask," he said. "After. Not before." His eyes stayed on the sheet. "I'm asking."

"What will I do with it."

"Yes."

She looked at the line. She thought of the survey office, and a key she had never been given. She thought of a small yellow star in a margin, and a man's gloves laid palms together on a stone.

"I drew it," said Ila. "I don't know yet whose it is."

The cairn beside her gave its note, steady and unbroken. Her fingertips stayed pink. The answer was true all the way down, which was the only comfort in it.

He breathed out slowly. It was not relief, and it was not disappointment. It was the sound of a man who had braced for a bargain and been handed none.

"That's the first true answer anyone's given me about a map," said Kunal.

"It's the only one I have."

She should tell him. The thought came in whole, with the lamp on his face. *There's a letter in my hem, and it says your grandfather walked this way with a warden, and the warden did not come home.* Hedged, even in her own head: the letter did not say he had walked this way, only that he had been guided. She had the first word of it in her mouth. Her hand lay flat over the stitches of the pattu.

Her oath stopped it first. She had sworn in a hall in Taravan to keep what the crown's papers held, and the queen had written it plainer still: *Tell no one Neshar's part.* To speak was to break both, here, on her knee, with the sheet still wet. She had broken one oath for him already, in her way. She did not know yet if she could break the second.

And there was the other reason, which was hers alone.

*Through the cleft, and the Eye, and whatever's under it.*

That was what she had said, with the rope humming, and she had meant every word. And they were not through. There was water under that dark and a stair going down into it. If she told him now, she would be paying a gift with a confession, a thing to square the account. He had said it wasn't a trade. She would not make it one.

She shut her mouth. Silence was free, and she hated what it cost.

"What?" said Kunal.

"Nothing yet," said Ila. "I'll tell you when I can pay it properly."

He looked at her a long moment. That morning he had paid for his own question with his fingers, and he had never asked her twice.

"All right," he said.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They came out at dawn.

The cleft let them go as quietly as it had taken them, into a grey light that turned the lake to a sheet of pewter. Ila stood on the ice lip with the sheet inside her coat and her legs shaking. She breathed the cold air and tasted nothing in it but morning. The rope hung slack and warm between her wrist and his.

Nobody had come for them. The far shore was bare. The cliff stood where it stood. Raghu's four had gone, or hidden, or were watching something else.

"Gauri will have bread," said Kunal. He sounded very tired. "She'll say nothing about it. She'll hand it over and say nothing."

"I'd marry her for that alone."

He laughed. Then he turned toward the lower end of the lake, where the shore folded in below the Drum and the lodging huts sat in their shelf of ground, and the laugh stopped.

Ila looked where he looked.

Every morning of her time on this lake she had seen a thread of smoke over the huts, grey and straight in the cold, where Gauri banked the fire. The huts stood in their fold of shore, small and dark and square.

There was no smoke.

# Chapter 24: Proof of Life

Kunal was running before he knew it, and the rope took him by the wrist and hauled him round.

"Eight spans," said Ila, behind him, hoarse. "Kunal. I'm coming. Don't pull."

He had pulled. He stood on the shore path with the frozen stones turning under his boots, and the huts were still a hundred paces off, small and dark in their fold below the Drum. No smoke. No goat bell. No Gauri at the door with her sleeves rolled and a rag in her hand, ready to say nothing and hand over bread.

"Walk fast," said Ila. "Don't run. If it's a trap, I'd like to see it before it sees us."

He walked fast, counting the hundred paces by the ache in his teeth. She kept every one at his shoulder, her breath loud, the rope between them swinging low.

The door of the second hut hung from a single leather hinge. Something heavy had split it down the middle, and the wood was pale and raw at the break. Inside, the blanket lay on the floor. The plank shelf where he and Ila had slept was empty and cold.

"Dhanu," he said. Not loudly. Then, loudly: "Dhanu!"

The lake answered with a low note and nothing else.

Dhanu's own hut stood a little apart, on the better ground. Its door was shut. Kunal went to it with the dead hand out in front of him like a man feeling for a wall in the dark, and then he stopped, because he had seen the snow.

It lay trampled in a wide ring before the sill. Many boots had been through it, and it had frozen again in ridges. In one place the ridges were dark. The stain was the width of a spread hand, the colour of old wine, crusted at the rim. Beside it, a sack lay slit and spilled. Dried apricots had been ground into the ice under heels, orange and brown, flat as dropped coins.

Gauri's apricots. They came to the huts by the pony load every autumn, and she handed them out like favours. He had eaten them off her palm at twelve.

"Don't touch it," said Ila quietly.

"I wasn't going to."

"You were." She did not say it unkindly. She crouched, rope trailing, and held a bare finger near the stain without quite touching it. "Not much. A cut, not a wound. A man could lose this and walk." She looked up. "A woman could."

She was giving him a measure to stand on. He took it, and hated that he needed it.

There was more. Two grooves ran from the doorway through the apricots and away over the snow, side by side and a boot's width apart. Between them, at long intervals, lay the scuff of a heel that had dug in and not held. Kunal followed the grooves to the end of the shore, where the stones gave way to the white of the lake.

They ran straight down to the Drum.

"They didn't carry him," said Ila. "Or her. Not all the way."

"They dragged them to the ice."

"Yes."

"Mini," he said.

He had the door of Dhanu's hut open before she answered. The shelf was bare. He dropped to his knees and looked under it, where a six-year-old fits and a grown man doesn't, and found dust and one knucklebone. He went out to the goat pen and the pony shed, calling her name, not loudly now, in the voice he used with children. He lifted the lid of the meal chest. He put his arm into the hay in the loft until the hay ran out.

Nothing. No small boots. No small voice saying *here, Kunal, you're so slow.*

"Kunal." Ila stood at the far corner of the pen, rope taut behind her. She pointed past the pony shed. A pony's tracks, older and half filled with blown snow, went away down the valley path and not toward the ice. "Her blanket's gone from the shelf, and her boots. Somebody put her on the pony before the boots came. One small rider."

His breath went out of him. Gauri. Gauri would have heard them on the shore and sent the child down the valley before she thought of anything else.

Then the other thought came up behind it, cold and quick. A pony track, snowed half over. A child alone on it, or a child caught at the first bend. He did not know which. He could not look at the lake without wondering, and he could not look away from the valley without wondering that, too.

He looked at the door of Dhanu's hut instead, and that was when he saw the paper.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


It was pinned to the lintel with an iron nail, driven in at an angle, hard. A scrap of pale hide, the kind herders use for tally marks. Under the nail, looped over its head by its thong, hung a small curved thing the length of a finger.

Dhanu's whistle.

Kunal had heard it on four hundred mornings. He knew how it sat in a man's palm and the smell of it, goat and smoke and Dhanu's skin. It was polished dark where a chest had worn it. Two long notes and one short: *come on, it's clear.* Three short: *turn back.* It had hung against Dhanu's breastbone since before Kunal could walk the ridge.

A man did not take that off over his head and leave it. It had been cut or torn away. The thong was frayed at one end.

He took it down with the good hand. He could not work the nail with the other, so Ila did, without a word, and drew out the hide and flattened it against the door with her palm.

The hill script was square, in charcoal. It was not Raghu's hand. It was a clerk's, or a man who wished to look like one.

*The surveyor's sheets, carried by the prince, to the Drum fold by moonrise tomorrow, or the two go under the ice.*

That was all.

He read it three times. By the third reading it had stopped being words and become a shape, and he could see the whole of it at once, like a ridge from the shoulder. *The two.* Not *the lame man.* Not *his sister.* Not *the child.* Whoever wrote it had not wasted ink on names, and they had left the whistle.

Two, and Mini somewhere down a valley path. Either she was out of their hands, and the note was counting right, or she was in no hand at all, uncounted, and nobody would be looking for her. He could not tell which, and the not knowing sat in him like a stone.

"They're alive," he said.

"The note says they are."

"The whistle says it." He closed his hand on the horn. His voice was steady, which surprised him. "Look at it. They took the one thing he'd never give. They can't show me a finger, so they showed me this. If they were dead there'd be nothing to trade, and no note."

"That's an inference."

"It's a good one."

"I didn't say it was bad." Ila stood very straight, her hand still flat on the hide. She was looking at the bottom of the scrap, below the last word, where a clean strip of leather lay bare. Her eyes moved along it and back, as they did along a chain. "Kunal. There's no walnut."

He had known it. He had known it from the first reading, in the part of him that heard the marsh-note under a stone before the foot came down. He had put the knowledge aside as one puts aside a bill.

"No," he said.

"You told me the rule. A walnut with red thread, or the word isn't his." She said it gently, the way she would tell a man his sum was out. "This isn't his word. It was never meant to be. It's theirs."

"I know whose it is."

"Then you know that if Dhanu had time, he'd have sent one. He gave you his walnuts. A man who hands out tokens keeps one back for himself. He'd have used it to say *it's a lie, don't come.* They didn't give him time."

*They didn't give him time.*

She had meant it as a fact, small and dry, to be laid on the table with the others. It went into him like the nail into the door.

He saw it. Dhanu in the doorway with the lamp behind him, the lame foot planted, hearing boots on the shingle. Reaching for the pouch. Not reaching quickly enough.

"No time to send it," said Kunal. "That doesn't make him dead."

"No."

"It makes him alive and taken."

"Yes," said Ila, after a moment. "That's what I said."

It was not what she had said. He let her have it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He walked down toward the Drum as far as the rope allowed, which was not far, and she came with him and gave him silence. The grooves ended at the lake's rim. The ice there was bare, wind-scoured and grey, and it took no print past the first yard. Still, the stones at the edge were scuffed, and a strip of blue wool lay caught between two of them. Gauri's. Or a stranger's.

Out on the Drum a long note rolled up from the depths and passed under his feet.

*Where the Drum answers, lie still till morning.*

He stood in the wind with the whistle in his fist and did the sum, because that was what she would have done, and because his own head would not do anything else.

Moonrise tomorrow. A day and a night and part of another. The fold lay at the far end of the lake, under the cliff, and a man could reach it in the time it took to eat. He would be there with her sheets, or without them. If without, they would be in the water.

He could not fight them. Raghu had eight bound pairs in the Hollow Moon, sixteen men, and one of them wrote in a good clerk's hand. He could not send for the council. Bhim would debate it. Dhanu's runner boy was a child, and every other boy was a day's walk off. He had his dead fingertips and a surveyor who had lied to him once, and told him so by dawn.

And he had what she carried. Five sheets with every turn of the hills drawn true, sewn into her pattu's seam; he had watched her stitch them in. The Stair. The Moon. The way into the Eye, past the weeping stone, down into the dark. He had put it into her hands himself last night, with his palms open. *Then it's yours. All of it.*

Last night he had meant it as the first free thing he had ever done.

Now it was a price.

He thought of it without flinching, which was the horror of it. It came to him whole, neat, and almost easy, as a clean line comes to a draughtsman. They wanted the sheets. He would give them the sheets. The princes' way for Dhanu. The hills' one door for one lame man who had taught him to throw knucklebones and let him lose.

Every argument Dhanu had made at the fire before the Eye stood up in him and went on standing. *A garrison is a house with soldiers in it, and the house goes where the way goes.* It was all true. He would still sell it.

His uncle's voice came back to him over a supper table, mild and reasonable, with a boy of nine on the bench beside him and the funeral meats still on the cloth. *Whoever knows the way owns the people, Kunal. Remember it. Everything else is manners.*

Twenty-four winters he had spent hating that sentence. He had built a council on its opposite. He had stood before the hills and said that no door belonged to one man.

And here he stood, with a whistle in his hand, ready to be the man who owned the way. Not to rule with it. Worse. To spend it. To count it out coin by coin on a frozen shore, as Pritam would have, and call it love.

*I'd rather be wrong that way than right in the way I've been.* He had said that at the fire before the Eye, with his hand on a walnut. He had not known that being wrong might come with a price in blood.

It did not feel like a defeat. That was what frightened him. It felt like a column of figures closing, every line agreeing with every other, and every creed he owned left behind him on the shingle.

He pulled the thong over his head. The whistle settled against his chest where Dhanu's had sat, and the cold of it went through his shirt like iron.

"Kunal," said Ila.

"I need you to listen to me."

"I've been listening for a quarter hour. You haven't said anything."

"I know." He turned. The rope swung between them. She stood three paces off with her arms wrapped across her coat, her hair crusted white at one temple where the lake had breathed on it. She looked small, and she looked like the person on the whole mountain who understood best what he was about to do. "I'll say it plainly, because you taught me to. They want your sheets, carried by me. They'll let them go if they get them. I have to believe it. I'll go to the fold tomorrow at moonrise and give them what they ask."

She did not answer at once. Her eyes dropped to his left hand, hanging at his side, the one hand whose fingertips could still tell on him. She looked at it as she looked at a closing chain.

The tips were pink.

"*I have to believe it,*" said Ila. "You didn't say *I believe it.* You didn't say *they will.* You said *if,* and then you said *have to.* Your hands let you say all of that, so it's true as far as it goes. It just doesn't go far." She lifted her eyes. "You don't think the trade holds."

"I think it's the only one there is."

"That's another sentence." A stillness came over her face, the one that came when a closure would not come out. "You'd give them the princes' way."

"I'd give them every turn I know. And every turn you drew." It came out level, and he was proud of that, and ashamed of the pride. "Last night I said it was yours. It is. I'm not taking it. I'm asking for it."

"Asking."

"For one night. For one man." He heard how the words sounded on the cold air. "Ila, I carry these knots in my teeth because I broke my promise to ask you nothing. I know what asking costs. I'm asking anyway. Dhanu told me I'd never given anything I couldn't take back. I'm giving this, and I can't take it back, and it's for him. I'm sorry. I know what it costs the hills. I know what it costs you."

"You don't."

"Then tell me."

"Not yet." She stood with her hand pressed flat to the hem of her pattu, where the letter lay. He had asked once, and paid for it, and not asked again. "Kunal, what happens to the route-keepers on the Stair, and the Moon, after Raghu has the sheets?"

"I don't know."

"You do. You've known since I said it. A man who draws a bearing for a captain isn't a witness he leaves standing. Dhanu's the one who knows where the lines run. If they have the sheets, they don't need Dhanu."

It was a fair argument, and the kind he would have made himself, an hour earlier, from the other side. He had no place to put it.

"If I go with nothing," he said, "they die for certain. If I go with the sheets, there's a chance. A bad one. I've done the odds." He swallowed. "One in three that they walk off that ice. Maybe less. It's all I have to buy."

"One in three isn't what I'd have said."

"What would you have said?"

She did not answer. She looked at the whistle on his chest, then at his face, and her mouth set in the line he had last seen when she measured the pen wall at Ewe's Rest and told Gauri it was a hand short.

The wind turned. Under the ice the Drum spoke again, a slow, deep stroke, and every stone on the shore rang with it. The rope stirred between them. He did not know what she would say. He knew only that every answer she had would be true.

Once, in a cleft, he had asked her to hand him a way. She had said *I drew it, I don't know yet whose it is.* It was the only honest answer he had ever heard about a map.

Now he needed another.

"Where are the sheets?" said Kunal.

Ila opened her mouth.

# Chapter 25: Burned

Ila could have said she did not know. It was a small lie, and her fingertips would have told him so in the time it took to blink.

She told him a truth instead, because truth was what she had.

"The Drum fold sits under a cornice," she said. "North shore, forty paces off the ice. The ledge hangs over the shingle like a lip. A man up there sees the whole fold, the lake, and both ways in. Two more rises look down on it from east and west."

Kunal said nothing. The whistle hung on his chest.

"I measured it from the mouth of the Eye, on the first morning, with a sight bar. The figures closed." She made her voice flat, the way she would for a clerk. "Three sightlines, Kunal. Nobody walks into that fold unseen, and nobody walks out unless the men on the ledge allow it. You'll hand them the sheets, and they'll kill you."

"That's not an inference."

"It is. It's a good one." She held his eyes. "You know the way in your head. A man who knows the way can share it. Raghu will know what to do with a man like that."

He looked at the lake. A long note rolled up through the ice and went on under their boots.

"Then I won't give them the true set," said Kunal slowly. His voice had changed. He sounded like a man picking up a tool he had sworn never to touch. "Draw them another. You can, in an hour. A wrong turn at the slot. A bad count at the weeping stone. Let them walk it for days."

"No."

"Ila."

"They'll test it." She said it quickly, to get the first part out before the second. "Raghu isn't a fool. He'll put Dhanu on the front of the rope and walk him down your wrong turn, and Dhanu will find out what a false line is in the dark, with his bad foot. That's the first reason."

"And the second?"

"I don't draw false lines." It sounded smaller aloud than it had in her head. "I never have. Not for the crown. Not for a bribe. Not once in eleven winters. A line is true or it isn't, and if I make one that isn't, then I don't know what I am. I'd sooner lose the sheets to a fire."

She heard what she had said and wished the word back.

He took it badly, and then he took it well. She watched both happen. His mouth twisted. Then his shoulders came down and he nodded, as though she had named a bearing and he had checked it.

"You'd say that if it were your sister on the ice."

"I'd say it and hate myself."

"Yes," said Kunal. "I know." He rubbed his face with the heel of the dead hand. "Then it's the true ones. I'll carry them. I asked you for them a quarter hour ago and I'm asking again. Hand them over, and I'll be at the fold by moonrise."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The wind dropped. The ice went quiet.

Five sheets lay sewn into the seam of her pattu, with a queen's letter below them in the hem. Last night he had put the princes' way into her palms and asked for nothing. This morning he asked for it back, and he was right to ask. She would have asked herself.

She did the sums the way she did all sums, in a straight line.

If she handed them over, Raghu had the Stair, the Moon, the Eye, and a cairn-lit road down into the mountain. Hari's goats would go on being driven up roads that had been drawn for someone else. Hari had scratched his warnings on stones for thirty winters. Raghu would not leave him standing to repeat them. And Kunal would walk onto the shingle under the cornice and be quietly done with.

If she said no, he would ask again. He would ask a third time at dusk. At the fold, in front of Raghu, his eyes would slide to her coat, as a man's eyes do toward whatever he is trying not to look at. Raghu would see it. A man with the way in his head was worth keeping alive. A man with the paper in his friend's lining was another matter.

But a man who thought the sheets were ash would not look at her coat.

Nor could she carry them to the fold. Raghu would search a surveyor on principle, and a woman with sewn seams was a risk. So the sheets would stay behind, in the second hut, and Kunal would stand on the shingle believing them gone.

The thought settled in her. It was also what she had refused, a minute ago, on principle. She had said she would not draw a false line. She had not said a word about her tongue.

Why not burn them, then, and make it true? She had offered fire herself. But the sheets were the only true record of these hills she would ever have: Hari's scratched warnings copied stone by stone, every turn Kunal had given her, and the map she meant to share with both courts at the same hour. Burn them and the hills went back to whoever held the ways in his head. And to burn them here, in front of him, would be one more thing he stood and watched go.

She thought of Meera at the drafting table in Garh Neshar, laying a left glove on the cloth with a thumb and two fingers and a gap where the ring and the little finger had been. *A great lie kills flesh,* Meera had said, quite cheerfully, while Rohan stared at his plate. *You'll know it's great when it's about something that matters.* Kunal had sat silent through two of those sessions. He had watched that glove. He knew exactly what one touch of her hand would teach him.

He waited with his hands open.

"They're gone," said Ila.

She said it too softly, so she said it again. "I burned them. This morning, when we came in. There were coals banked under ash. They're gone."

The cold took her hands first. It went in under the gloves like a blade laid flat, and her fingertips were gone before she finished the sentence. She folded her arms across her ribs and tucked both hands into her armpits as though she were only hugging herself against the wind.

Kunal went still.

"You burned them," he said.

"Yes."

"The Stair. The Moon. The Eye."

"All of them."

He looked at her as if she had spoken in a language he had once known. His face went through it. Disbelief came first, then something nearly like relief, the feeling of a man told that the weight he must lift was a trick of the light. Last came horror.

"You drew the Eye last night," said Kunal. "You had it at dawn. I watched you put it in your coat."

"I know what you watched."

"Then—"

"The coals were alive under the ash when we came in. You were shouting for Dhanu and your back was to me." She heard herself and thought, with the cold part of her that kept on measuring, that it was a thin story. She said nothing more. Silence was free, and every added word was one more she would pay for.

"There was no smoke," said Kunal.

"Banked coals don't give any."

He looked at her a moment longer. She saw him weigh it, find it light, and decide, with a small movement of his jaw, not to say so. He let it go. He chose it, and she saw him choose.

He stepped toward her. For one long moment she thought he would put out the good hand and take hers. She knew exactly what he would learn.

He stopped a pace off and put his hands in his coat.

"Why?" he said.

"Don't ask me why."

"I'm asking."

"Then I'm refusing," said Ila. "For free."

The word hung over the ice. He heard it and did not know what to do with it. It was not a lie, which was her whole hope. It was a locked door, and he was a man who had been raised among locked doors.

"All right," said Kunal, very quietly. "I'll look."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The second hut still smelled of Gauri's tea. Ila sat on the plank shelf and pulled her boots off.

The cold had reached her feet while she walked up the shore path, an inch at a time. First came a numbness like the end of a bad winter day, then a wooden feeling, as if the feet belonged to a chair. Only the smallest toes still answered, faintly, like people calling from another room. She unlaced the left boot and looked at the sock. The skin of her toes looked ordinary. That was the worst of it. She had expected it to look like what it was.

Behind her Kunal knelt at the hearth. He took a poker from the hook and stirred the ash, and grey dust rose and settled in the thin light from the door. His back was to her.

She did not have long. She worked her thumbnail under the stitches of the side seam, and they came loose with a small dry sound that seemed to fill the hut. Her fingers were no use. She did it with the heel of her hand and her teeth. The five sheets slid out in a thin folded packet, warm from her ribs. She slid them into the gap where the plank shelf met the wall, a hand deep and black with old smoke, and pushed them in until the edge vanished. The queen's letter stayed in the hem where it was.

When he looked round she was bent over the right boot, hair hiding her face.

"Bark," said Kunal. He lifted a clot of cinder in his dead fingers and crumbled it. He held a black curl to the light. "Or paper."

"Could be either."

"It's thin." He turned it. "It's thin enough." He put it down and turned over more, lips moving. He was counting. She knew the shape of that count, because it was hers. He was checking whether the ash was enough ash for five sheets, and it was not a question the ash could answer.

She pulled the boots on again, as tight as the laces would go, and tied them off in a double hitch. If she could not feel the ground, she would at least not lose the boot.

"Your hands," he said.

Her stomach dropped. "What about them?"

"They're cold. You came out of the Eye with them cold. You've been wrapping them in your coat since the shore." He said it mildly. "You should put them near the stove."

"There's no fire."

"No." He looked at the grey hearth. "No, there isn't."

He did not take her hands. He did not say *show me.* He sat back on his heels, and she watched him look at the ash and then at her bowed head. He had spent twenty years reading rooms, and he had put all of it down. He wanted to believe her more than he wanted to know. She would remember that whenever it hurt, and it was going to hurt.

"I'm sorry," said Kunal, and it startled her. "That I asked you to hand them over. That I asked twice. I said it was yours, last night. I meant it as a gift, not a trade." He rubbed his thumb along the poker's iron. "I was going to spend it at the first price anyone named. Maybe it's as well you got there before I did."

"Don't," said Ila.

"It's true."

"I know it's true." She tied the knot. Her fingers would not close on it, so she did it with her wrists. "That's why I'd rather you didn't."

He laughed once, wretchedly, and stood up and hung the poker on its hook.

"Then there's nothing to give them," he said.

"No."

"They'll want the sheets. Raghu will want to know where they are, and I'll tell him the truth and he won't believe it." He stood in the doorway with the light behind him, and she could not see his face. "Moonrise isn't the hour any more. They'll have a man at the shingle by then, with a spyglass. If I go tonight, at dusk, I'm one man with a rope and a prince's name and nothing in my hands. He can't search a thing that isn't there. And if Dhanu's been waiting a day and a night on the ice, I don't want him to wait another."

"Kunal."

"If I'm all there is," said Kunal, "I'll be all there is." The whistle shone on his coat. "I'll offer myself. They want the hills' way. I'm the one man who can walk it without a paper. Maybe that's worth something to a man who needs a door opened."

It was what she had hoped, laid out in the plainest words. She had meant it as a hope and not a plan.

"The rope," said Ila.

"Yes." He did not look at her. "The rope says you come."

"I know what the rope says."

"I'd leave you here if I could. You know I'd leave you here."

"I know."

She did. It was almost the only thing she knew. She stood up on feet that were no longer quite hers, and the floor held.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


They went down at the hour when the cliff swallowed the sun.

The shore had gone the colour of a bruise. Ice crept out from the stones in long grey fingers, and the Drum lay beyond, white and flat, with the first of the dusk stars pricking out above the far rim. Ila walked behind Kunal on the shingle and counted her steps under her breath, because it was what her feet did.

*Forty. Forty-one.*

Somewhere around the sixtieth pace the last small toes went quiet, and after that her feet told her nothing at all. She found the ground by looking at it. She set each boot down where it ought to go and trusted it.

Kunal held his shoulders as he had in the cleft, the whistle in one fist and the rope loose in the other. In the hut he had not asked again. The question rode his back all the same, and neither of them named it.

*Ninety. Ninety-one.*

Her hands were as dead as stones inside her sleeves. It was a mercy, in its way. She could not feel them shaking.

*I did not draw a false line,* she told herself. *Not one.* It was a ridiculous thing to hold onto. She clung to it all the same, one true coin in a purse of counterfeits, and she knew what it was worth.

A wind came up off the lake and smelled of iron and cold water. Below the shore, the ice answered it with a slow boom, and the pulse went through her boots and up her shins and ended somewhere near the knee, where the feeling stopped.

"Ila," said Kunal. "Are you with me?"

"Eight spans," she said. "I'm here."

"You've gone quiet."

"I'm counting."

"What?"

She looked down at her boots. They were not moving the way she had told them to. Her left foot came forward and set itself down, and she waited for the feel of it and nothing came. It was like standing on a drawing of a foot.

"Everything," said Ila. "I'm counting everything."

The shingle ended. The ice began. She put a boot on it, and it was not her boot any more, and the other one was nowhere, and the rope went slack as Kunal turned. She heard him say her name from a long way off. The lake rolled its drum-note up through the dusk, and the stars swung slowly over, and the grey ice came up to meet her.

# Chapter 26: The Drum Fold

Ila did not wake when he cut her laces, and that frightened him more than the ice had.

He had dragged her off the lake and laid her on the shingle with his coat under her head. The rope lay between their wrists in a slack loop. Dusk was going fast. Out on the Drum the ice held the last light like a pewter plate, and under the cliff, a short walk off, the fold waited with no lamp in it. He kept his back to it.

Her boots had frozen into a hitch he could not pick. He sawed the lace with his knife, the handle pinned in the heel of his dead hand, and worked the left boot off, then the sock, which came away stiff as bark.

The foot was white to the instep. Not pale. White as tallow, with a shine on it.

He put both palms around it.

The cold came up his arms the way water climbs a wick. He had known hill cold all his life, the honest kind that bites and lets go. This had a size to it. It poured into his wrists and kept pouring, and with it came a fact as plain as a stone set on a table: *a lie was told, and it was great.*

He learned only the size of it. What the truth was, the cold did not carry. It told him how heavy the hidden thing was, and that told him how much it mattered.

*They're gone. I burned them.*

"Don't," said Ila. Her eyes had opened. Her voice was thick. "Kunal. Your hands."

"Be quiet."

"It's mine. I paid for it."

"You did." He held on until the cold stopped arriving. His palms went past burning into something worse, and when he lifted them they were red and tight, with blisters rising along the lines of his fingers. "Now we've both paid."

The foot lay between them. It had pinked up, all but the edge. The two smallest toes were the colour of old candle-ends, with a dull grey bloom under the nails. He touched one with a knuckle. It gave back no warmth.

The cold had reached them before he did.

"Those two," he said.

"I know." She had pushed herself up on one elbow and was looking at her own foot as she would at a stranger's chain. "I couldn't feel them to know. I'd have guessed by dark."

He cut the right boot free and worked it off. The foot inside was stiff and blotched, but it pinked under his thumb, and the toes moved. He had not been able to check it on the ice. He let out a breath. One foot, then, and only the edge of it.

"You told me they were ash."

"I did."

"They aren't ash." He heard how level he sounded. It was the voice he used in council when he wanted to bleed a man quietly. "A great lie, and about the sheets. So you have them. You've had them sewn in your coat the whole morning while I knelt at that hearth counting cinders."

She said nothing. Silence was free, and he had taught himself to hate it in other people's mouths.

"Then I'll do the sum," said Kunal. "There's one person in these hills who'd want them kept from Raghu and from me. One person who has your oath. You've carried that letter sewn into you since the Fair and refused me twice, and now this." He pulled the sock over the ruined toes with the flat of his hand, and it was not gentle. "You'd let Dhanu die for your queen's map."

She took her hands out of her sleeves.

She held them up between them in the dusk, open, the way he had held his own at the fire before the Eye. The fingertips were pink. The nails were clear.

"They're not for the queen," said Ila. "Or Raghu. And they're not for trading you."

Her hands stayed warm.

It was a true answer, and it cost her nothing, and that was the trouble with it. He would have taken a lie. A lie he could have hated cleanly.

"Then what are they for?"

"That's free too." She glanced up at the cornice, and he saw her measure the distance. "Not here. There's a glass on that ledge, and a few hundred paces from now you'll stand in front of Raghu. Whatever I tell you, your face will carry it to him. I told you they were ash for that reason. Ask me when no one can see you."

"Dhanu is on that ice."

"I know where he is." Her voice shook once and steadied. "You asked once what I'd have said, if I'd put a figure to it. If you'd handed them over, I'd have said one in ten. Less. I'd have said it and watched you hate me."

"I might have been the one."

"Yes. You might." She looked at him. "I'd say it again."

He got his arm under her knees and the other behind her back, and stood, and the blisters split across his palms. He kept it off his face. A rope's length of cold air lay between him and the fold. He walked into it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The shingle under the cliff was a grey strip, forty paces from the ice to the rock. He had known it since he was nine, when Dhanu taught him to skip stones here. Now it had a lantern on it, shuttered to a slit, and men.

Two pairs stood on the cornice above, four dark shapes against the last of the sky, the rope between each pair hanging in a curve. One held something to his eye that caught the light. Spyglass. Kunal felt it settle on his chest like a thumb.

Raghu waited at the water's edge, bound at the wrist by eight spans of grey wool to a boy.

Kunal had met the captain twice, across the Garh Neshar hall, when Raghu wore the regent's bell on his shoulder and a courtier's smile. He wore no smile now. He was a lean man gone leaner, with ice in his beard and the careful stillness of someone who had not slept since the Snow Fair. The boy tied to him was perhaps twenty. Grey wool, a brass bell on the shoulder, a face too young for the cold on it. Pasang. Kunal knew the name from a Ranu story Meera had told, and could not now have said which story.

"Prince," said Raghu. "You've brought me a woman and no paper."

"I've brought you me."

"I asked for the sheets."

"They're ash." He said it flatly. He had settled it on the walk. It was a lie Raghu could not test, and it cost him nothing to say to Raghu, and it kept the man's search off her seams. Her breath was warm against his collar. "I'll give you every turn I know. I walked the Stair. I walked the Moon. I stood in the Eye, and I can walk it again. Take me. I'm the way you wanted. I don't need paper to open it."

"A man who can't produce a thing is an inconvenient man."

"Then I'm inconvenient."

Raghu studied him, as a clerk studies a coin. Kunal let his eyes go past the captain's shoulder, out onto the grey ice, and there they stopped.

Dhanu lay on the Drum, twenty paces out. He lay on his back with one arm flung wide, the lame foot turned in. A scrap of blue wool lay across his chest. He was entirely still.

Beside him, on her side with her knees drawn up, lay his sister Gauri. A strip of cloth was knotted across her mouth. Her wrists were bound before her, and a cord ran from her belt to an iron peg driven into the ice. Her eyes were open. They met his, and they were as dry as stones.

"Dhanu," said Kunal.

The lake gave him back a low note.

"Dhanu."

"He's resting," said Raghu.

Something in Kunal's chest dropped away from its footing. He had been ready to hear that Dhanu was hurt, or starved, or frightened. He had walked the whole shore with Ila's *one in ten* in his ear. He had not been ready for the flat fact of a man lying very still on ice.

"Captain," said Pasang, low. He was staring where Kunal stared. His voice cracked. "Sir. You told me the lame one was only..."

"The limping one went home this morning, Pasang," said Raghu, loudly, in the voice of a man reading a notice. "You saw him go. That is a farmer from the east fold. Keep your eyes on the prince."

The boy's mouth closed. He looked at the ice and then, quickly, away.

Ila's fingers dug into Kunal's shoulder.

"His hand," she breathed. "Raghu's. Look at his hand."

It was resting on the hilt of his sword, and the fingertips were white. They were white to the first joint, bloodless, like a row of peeled almonds.

Kunal looked at them and could make nothing of it. A lie, small, told to the boy at his wrist. The limping one went home. And Dhanu lay out there where anyone could see.

He could not do the sum. His head would not take it. Whatever Raghu was hiding from Pasang, it was not that a man was dead, because the whole shore could see that. It was something else, and the thing in front of him was Dhanu's open, frosted face.

"The sheets," said Raghu softly, "or the ice takes the woman. And I'll start with her, so you can watch."

He lifted two fingers to the cornice.

"Eleven paces left of the boom line," said Ila, clear and loud, into Kunal's ear, "then straight."

He ran.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Ila's soundings were in his legs before his mind caught up. He had stood at her shoulder on the Drum, the morning she opened the letter, and watched her chip her holes with his own knife and the iron spike, tapping the haft, listening for the ring. She had called every figure aloud. *Eight fingers. Eleven. Seven here, don't.* He had laughed at her. He had said nobody sounded the Drum, because the Drum could be heard.

Now he ran with her weight crushing his blisters and his heart beating in the burst skin of his palms, and her voice came steady as a chain-man's tally.

"Boom line, there, the pale seam, it's on your right. Eleven paces. Count them. Don't look at it."

He counted. Behind him a shout went up, Raghu's, then the dry slap of boots on shingle and then on ice. He kept his head front.

"Straight. Straight. The ice is fourteen here. It's good, Kunal. Hold the line."

Behind them a man yelled, "Cut across! Cut him off!" And a second answered, close, and the rhythm of the boots changed. They were coming on the chord, the short way, through the pale seam. The place where the Drum was thinnest was exactly where a man in a hurry would think it safest.

"Don't look," said Ila.

The ice spoke.

It was a single note, enormous, rolling up through the soles of his boots and into his teeth, and then it broke into a sound he had never heard from the Drum, a wet tearing like cloth ripped under water. A man screamed. Another screamed with him. The rope that tied them together dragged the second one under with the first.

Kunal slowed. He could not stop himself. Over his shoulder he saw two dark shapes in a black hole, white with spray, and a rope thrashing at the lip. Then the cornice men were shouting, and Raghu was shouting, and the whole shore was turned toward the break.

"Go on," said Ila, very quietly. "They're gone. Go on."

He did not go on. He veered.

Gauri lay twelve paces to his left, with the lantern's slit of light flung across her bound hands. She had pulled herself up on her elbows. Above the gag her eyes were wide and furious.

He dropped to one knee on the ice, Ila clamped against his chest. The knife was already out. He could not feel the handle. He pinned it with the heel of his dead hand, and sawed the cord at her belt, then the cloth between her wrists. The edge went through her skin as well as the wool, and she hissed. She tore the gag off herself.

"The huts," said Kunal. "Run. Don't stop."

She looked past him, at Dhanu, lying a few paces away with the blue wool on his chest. Her face was perfectly steady, and he had never seen anything so terrible.

"Gauri."

"I know," she said. It came out a rasp. She put one hand flat on her brother's coat, as a woman checks bread, and took it away. "Go."

She ran. She ran badly, bent over, her cut wrists held to her chest, her legs driving at the shingle. Behind her the shore men were wheeling toward the hole in the ice, and not one of them looked at a woman running the other way.

He should have gone to Dhanu. He knew it. He should have knelt, and shut the eyes, and said something, anything. Frost lay in the lashes. A scrap of blue wool lay across the chest. He had known this man thirty years.

"Kunal," said Ila.

"I know."

"We can't."

"I *know*," said Kunal, and ran.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


There was no word for the hour that came after. He ran along the shore until the shingle gave out, and walked when he could not run, and walked when he could not walk, with the rope dragging and the lantern shouts growing small behind. Ila gave him bearings in the dark. Once she said, "Put me down, you can't," and he said, "I can," and she said nothing more.

His hands had stopped being hands. They were two sheets of burning where something wet ran, and he held her with them anyway, because the alternative was to put her down.

They crossed the neck of rock between the Drum and the Eye by the blue stones, which he could see now, faint and steady, a thin chain pulling him on. Raghu's men were bound. They would see them too, if they came. He put the thought away. He kept his eyes on the next cairn, and on the next.

The cleft opened out of the cliff like a shut mouth beginning to speak. He carried her in past the lip and the ice hollow and set her down on the dry gravel, against the wall. Then he stood and looked at what they had.

Nothing. His pack lay at the hut, with the flint and the lamp oil and the last of the bread. Hers was with it. The cleft held rock, and cold, and the sound of water far down, and no fuel. He had pushed a prince's name and nothing in his hands onto a frozen lake, and here was the whole of what that bought.

Ila had her bad foot drawn up under her. She was shaking so hard her teeth clicked. She asked for nothing, not even warmth. She looked at him, and he saw her get ready to say something true.

"Don't," said Kunal.

"I only..."

"Don't say anything true to me tonight." He sat down against the opposite wall, with eight spans of wool between them and his blistered hands lying open on his knees like something he had borrowed. "I'm not sure what I'll do with it."

She shut her mouth. The rope lay slack across the gravel between them.

Behind them, on the ice, beneath a sky going black, Dhanu lay with his face turned up and nobody to cover it. Kunal sat in the dark and counted his own breathing, because it was all there was to count, and did not know yet whether he would hate her by morning, or only wish he could.

# Chapter 27: Past the Rope

The cleft had no fire, and by the second hour Ila could not feel her feet.

She had stopped trying to. She sat against the gravel wall where Kunal had set her, knees drawn up, hands pushed into her armpits. The cold in the cleft was dry and patient. It came up out of the rock and did not care whose it was.

The blue stones ran away into the dark like a string of beads. They were the only light. Kunal sat across from her with eight spans of undyed wool lying slack on the gravel between them. He had not spoken since he told her not to say anything true.

His hands lay open on his knees. In the faint light she could see the blisters had burst, and the palms shone wet. The fingertips on the right were grey. He did not tuck them away or blow on them. He looked at them the way she had seen him look at the Drum from the shore, as if they were a distance he was measuring.

She had told herself a number of things to pass the time. That the toes would go. That two toes were a fair price for a prince's life, and a fair price was a thing she understood. That he was in pain and she should let him be.

None of it moved the cold, and none of it moved him.

"Kunal."

He did not look up.

"You'll want to see to your hands. There's a strip left on my hem. I can't reach it. You could—"

"Ila." He said it quietly. "Don't."

She stopped.

After a while he got up. He did it slowly, a hand on the wall, the way a man gets up from a sickbed. He stood looking down the passage toward the mouth, toward the hollow under the ice lip and the lake beyond it. Dhanu's goat-horn whistle hung on his coat. He had lifted it off the lintel nail at the hut, with the note still pinned beside it, and he had not taken it off since.

Then he raised his left wrist and put the rope's knot to his teeth.

She understood what he was doing a moment too late. The wool was wet and the knot was hers, the warden's double hitch she had first tied at the Meadow Cairn with cold fingers and a good deal of hope. He worked it with his teeth and the heel of his hand. It took him a long time. She watched every turn of it come loose.

Her own end stayed tied at her wrist. His came free. He did not drop it. He wound the loose end twice round his fist and closed the fist on it.

"It ends nothing," said Ila. Her voice came out cracked. "You know it ends nothing."

"I know."

"Then—"

"Dhanu is out there on the ice with a scrap of wool on his chest and nobody has shut his eyes." He did not turn round. "His sister put her hand on him as if she were testing bread, and then she ran, because I told her to. Raghu is still there. I'm going to walk back."

"They're bound pairs on the cornice. Four of them, with a spyglass."

"Yes."

"You'll be one man with no paper."

"Yes."

He walked.

Ila watched his back go down the passage between the blue stones. The slack slid off the gravel and followed him in a long pale curve. At the sixth stone it began to straighten. At the eighth span it snapped taut, and she felt it at her wrist, a thin hard tug, with his fist at the far end of it.

The cold hit her then.

It was nothing like lie-cold. That had come in at the fingertips, a blade laid flat. This came from everywhere at once, as though the whole cleft had drawn a breath and held it. Her breath smoked. Her chest tightened. The heat she had been hoarding in her armpits drained out of her like water from a cracked jug.

The blue stones dimmed. The faint line that had run so steadily down the passage went thin, and thinner, and the stone nearest her guttered like a lamp in a draught.

His shoulders jerked. He stood at the end of the wool, not yet past it, and did not turn.

*One long pull,* she thought.

It was Meera's signal, one long pull for *come*, and every pair in the high valleys knew it. The rope was taut and she had it at her wrist. She could haul on it with both arms and her whole weight against the wall, and it would drag at his fist like a halter. It was not even a lie. It was only a pull. He would turn, because the rope would turn him.

She took hold of the wool with her swollen hands. It was a long way off from her body, and she held it a long time.

Then she let it go slack in her fingers.

It would have been easy to call it kindness. It was closer to arithmetic. If she hauled him back, she would have a man at her side who had been hauled, and he would sit across from her until the thaw knowing she had done it. She had spent every day since the Meadow Cairn learning this man's habits. He could bear a good deal, but he hated to be made to stay.

Far down the passage, his fist opened. The loose end of the wool fell and lay across the gravel like something shed. He walked on.

She filled her lungs. The air hurt going in.

"Kunal!"

His step did not break.

"I lied to keep you alive," said Ila. The cleft threw the words back at her, one at a time, down the long dark. "I told you the sheets were ash, and they weren't. I'd do it again."

Nothing happened. Her fingers in her armpits stayed numb, but they were only cold. No new chill came. The stones did not ring. The truth went out of her and the mountain let it.

"I'd do it again," she said, quieter, to herself. "Every time."

He was a shape between the blue stones, small now. She counted him. It was the only thing she could do. *Eleven spans. Twelve.* The cold had gone from a hand to a fist. Her teeth had begun to knock.

*Thirteen.*

She could not feel her legs at all now, only a heavy, drowned ache from the hip down. She had told him that at twice the length the path vanished. She had read it in every chronicle the survey office held and thought it quaint.

*Fourteen. Fifteen.*

He stopped.

His face was lost to her. His shape had stopped, the shoulders had come down, and he stood very still in the thin blue dark, with the passage opening ahead of him toward the lake and the dead and Raghu's lantern.

Nothing true was left that she hadn't said, so she did not call again.

Then he put his ruined hands to his own face.

A long time went by. The cold squeezed. Somewhere deep in the rock the meltwater ticked on stone, once, twice, a slow, hateful sound.

Kunal turned round.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


He came back slowly, like a man walking up out of deep water. The blue stones brightened as he passed them, one after another, and the iron fist of cold loosened in her chest by degrees. Her breath came easier. Her face was wet before she knew why, and then the tears went cold on her cheeks.

He stopped a pace from her and stayed standing.

"Your life is behind me," said Kunal. His voice was rough and very low. "That's the sum." He looked at his hands. "I'd have done it. I want you to know I wanted to."

"I know."

"Don't be kind about it."

"I'm not," said Ila. "I'm cold."

It startled a breath of laughter out of him, a cracked and ugly thing. He sat down heavily on the gravel. For a moment she thought he would put his head on his knees and stay that way.

"I don't forgive you," he said.

"I didn't ask you to."

"You lied to me. You lied about the one thing I begged you for. You stood there with your hands in your sleeves and said *gone,* and I believed it because I wanted to, and a man died on the ice while I knelt at a hearth counting cinders." He drew a breath. It shook. "You say you'd do it again. I believe you. That's what I can't—" He stopped.

"Say it."

"You'd do it again," said Kunal, "and you'd be right, and I hate it."

She had no answer to that. It was true, so she let it stand.

What she would remember after the thaw, when she turned the night over, was that he stayed without forgiving her. He reached out with one blistered hand, and with the other he took hold of her ankle. He drew her bad foot toward him through the gravel, as one drags a stray lamb closer to the fold.

"Kunal, the cold—"

"I know what it is. I've had it." He worked the boot off, the one he had pulled back onto her at the shingle, then the sound one, the laces already cut. He unbuttoned his coat and the shirt under it. Then he lay down on his side along the gravel, back to the wall, and pulled both her feet against the bare skin of his belly. He drew the coat shut over them.

She gasped. He was warm. He was warm like a hearth.

"Don't talk," he said into the dark. "I can't be kind and not talk to you at once. Pick one."

"Not talking."

"Good."

The hands lay on her ankles, burnt and rough, and held. His breath moved slowly under her heels. After a while his grip stopped being the grip of a man who held a thing and became the grip of a man who held on.

They lay like that for hours.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


At some point the dark went thin and she was awake, and the cold had changed.

Her feet hurt. That was good. A foot that hurt had a foot attached. She lay with her cheek on the gravel and her eyes open and watched the blue stones pulse, slow and faint, as the mountain breathed.

She thought of her grandfather.

It was not on purpose. She had spent years avoiding it, ever since she first read the chronicle entry over her father's shoulder. Warden Harish, who went out in a night storm to check the cairns for a lost party. Her father, over his clock and his one good eye, saying the gloves were like a pair set out for the morning. A flat stone on the slope above the third bend. Two gloves laid palms together, square to the edge, the fingers pointing the way he had been walking.

The gloves had come back to her in the queen's hand, plain as a receipt. A man did not take off his gloves at the end of a storm night by accident, and he did not lay them square by accident. It was a payment-sign. It said *here, this is given.*

Why anyone would choose it, she had not understood. Now she did, and it was worse. Her hands had gone past hurting, and all she wanted was to set them down somewhere square and be finished.

A flat stone lay beside her head. It had come out of the cleft wall at some point, a slab as long as her forearm, tilted a little toward the passage. She stared at it.

Slowly, with fingers she could not feel, she pulled off the left glove, then the right. She laid them on the stone. She turned them palm to palm, the left on the right, and set the fingers pointing down the passage, toward the dark, the way a person would walk.

Her hand stopped a finger's breadth above them.

It would be so easy to finish. The mountain was asking nothing she had not already half agreed to. She was cold, and she was alone in a way that had nothing to do with the man at her feet. He had said he did not forgive her, and he meant it. He held her because letting go would have killed her, and that was a different thing from love.

Her grandfather's answer lay on the stone in front of her. It was clear, and it was cold, and she was so tired.

She picked up the gloves.

Without ceremony, the way she would put a pencil back in her pocket. She worked her hands into the wool, one finger at a time, and sat with them in her lap.

*Not yet,* thought Ila. *I know that door. I'm not walking through it.*

Against her feet, Kunal's belly rose and fell. He had not stirred. She thought he was asleep, and then she thought he was not.

"What were you doing?" he said into the dark.

"Counting."

He said nothing. The rope lay across the gravel, its free end where he had let it fall. Neither of them touched it.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


The light came late.

First it was only a greyness at the end of the passage, where the ice lip hung over the hollow. Then it was a line. The blue stones went pale and ordinary in it. Her feet had swollen against Kunal's belly and ached in the long, outrageous way that meant blood. He had finally slept, mouth slightly open, one fist bunched against her ankle.

Boots sounded before she saw anything.

They were light. They came up from the hollow, picking over the glaze, a person who knew the way. Ila lay rigid. She could do nothing else. She had no knife and no feet. Kunal's hand closed on her ankle and she felt the whole length of him wake.

The shape in the cleft mouth was tall and broad. A shawl was wound round the head and shoulders. Both hands held something against her chest, a clay pot with a lid pierced in a ring of holes, and out of the holes came a thread of smoke that smelled of juniper.

"Gauri," said Kunal.

His voice broke on it.

She came in without rushing and knelt at the edge of the gravel and set the pot down on the flat stone, the one where the gloves had not stayed. The embers glowed orange through the holes. Ila felt the heat on her face like a hand.

The woman's wrists were wrapped in rag. Her eyes were red-rimmed and very clear. She looked at Kunal's burnt hands, and at the grey on Ila's bare ankle where the sock had come off, and said nothing about either of them.

"I came back along the shore in the dark," said Gauri. "The captain's men were gone from the fold by moonset. They took the boy's rope and what was left of their pairs, and they went up the Kuhl side." Her voice was level. It was the voice of someone who had cried everything out of herself in the night and had nothing but the next thing to do. "I sat with my brother a time."

"Gauri," said Kunal. "I should have—"

"No."

She said it so flatly that he stopped.

Gauri looked at the two of them in the first light, the prince with his shirt open and the surveyor in her coat, and a rope lying between them untied.

"I turned his coat back," she said. "To close his eyes. That's how I found his side, where the shirt was cut." She drew a slow breath. "Dhanu was dead before they wrote it."

Nobody moved. In the pot the embers ticked and settled.

"Then there was never a trade," said Ila.

But Kunal had already gone white to the lips, one hand shut over the whistle he had taken off the nail.

# Chapter 28: Off a Cold Man

"Raghu's clerk wrote it," said Gauri. "A thin man with ink on his cuff. By lantern, at first light, on a scrap off my brother's own tally-hide." She kept her eyes on the pot. "He read it back twice, to hear if it sounded like a man in a hurry."

The whistle lay against Kunal's breastbone on its frayed thong. He could not feel it with his dead fingertips, only its weight. It was small and warm from his skin, and it was not his.

"Tell it," he said.

"There isn't much." Gauri lifted the lid a finger's width and juniper smoke rolled out, sharp and clean. She set Ila's bare foot on a fold of her own shawl, close to the heat, and began to chafe the ankle with her broad thumb without being asked. "They came to the huts at moonrise, while you were still in the Eye. Raghu, the boy Pasang, the four from the cornice. They wanted names. Every keeper from the Stair to the Kuhl cut. Every runner. Every house that feeds a pair."

"Mini?" said Ila.

"At my sister's. I had her on the pony and down the valley path before they reached the door. She thinks it was a game." Gauri's thumb kept working. "She'll ask for her father's string."

"And Dhanu."

"Dhanu gave them his ewes." Her mouth moved. It was not a smile. "By name. Oldest first, with their faults. The clerk had written eleven before he understood."

Ila made a small sound. Kunal said nothing.

"They took my brother out to the seam," said Gauri. "The black water, where the ice won't hold. They put a rope under his arms and let him down into it, and hauled him up, and asked again. He was not a young man. His heart stopped before the moon cleared the Kuhl shoulder." The thumb went on at the ankle. "That was the first night. Before there was a note to write."

The embers ticked.

"They took the whistle off him after," she said. "I watched them do it."

Kunal's hand had come up on its own and closed on the horn. The grey tips felt nothing.

"It was looped over the nail," he said. "By the thong. I took it down and thought, *he's sent it so I'll know it's him.*" He heard himself breathe. "There was no walnut."

Gauri looked at him then.

"He gave me two. A boy with a red-thread walnut is Dhanu's word, and Dhanu stands behind whatever comes with it. This came with a whistle and no walnut, and I told myself a man with a blade at his back doesn't stop to find his tokens." His voice sounded far off, like someone else's in the next room. "There was no walnut because there was no Dhanu."

"No," said Gauri.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She left them soon after, without ceremony, as she did everything. She set a skin of water by Ila's knee, pushed two flat loaves out of her shawl onto the stone, and went to sit at the cleft mouth with her back to them and her face to the lake.

Kunal stayed where he was. The sum he had refused all night had finally come and sat down in front of him, and he made himself read it.

"A ransom is for a man who is alive," he said. "Raghu never wanted a trade. He wanted a prince on that ice at dusk, standing where he was put. I moved the hour myself, so Dhanu wouldn't wait another night." He turned his dead hand over. "He'd already stopped waiting."

"Kunal—"

"Let me finish, or I won't." He kept his eyes on the fire. "A man who thinks he's bargaining stands still. I hand over the sheets, I stand where he points, and the glass on the cornice finds my chest."

"I told you to look at his hand," said Ila.

"You did. I looked and couldn't read it." He shook his head. "On the shore he told the boy, loud, with his palm on his sword, that the limping one had gone home that morning. His fingertips went white. I took it for a lie aimed at us."

"It was a lie," said Ila. "I saw that much. I didn't know whose it was for."

"The boy's. Pasang is bound to him. Raghu needed him calm and holding the rope, and a boy who knew he was guarding a dead man's trade would not have held it." He made himself look up. "The white fingers were the whole answer. Dhanu hadn't gone home. He was already under that scrap of wool."

Ila said nothing. She had known the shore was lying and had carried it alone.

"So I said *take me,*" said Kunal, "and when he lifted two fingers I ran. With you on my arm, calling the ice." He drew a breath that shook. "The pair who came after us went through at the seam. That was the short way, the way a man in a hurry thinks is safe. It would have been mine."

Her hands lay on her knees in the ember light.

"Your lie cost you two toes," he said, "and it kept me alive. It didn't cost Dhanu his."

"Nothing could have."

"No. I know that now." He looked at the fire. "I couldn't know it then. You couldn't have told me."

"I could have said it was a trap."

"You'd have said it with nothing to show, and I'd have told you the sum was mine." It hurt less than he had expected, which was its own kind of shame. "You asked me twice about the letter and I made you pay for the asking. So you went alone, with your hands in your sleeves, and carried the cold, because you couldn't trust me to listen." He stopped. "I wouldn't have listened."

Ila did not say *thank you,* or *it's all right.* Her mouth opened, shut, and opened again.

"No," she said. "I don't think you would."


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


She pulled her pattu round and sat up straighter, a woman about to hand over a ledger. He watched her do it.

"I said I'd tell you when I could pay it properly," said Ila. "I'm early. I know."

"You don't owe me—"

"I do. I owed it you before the Eye." She laid both hands flat on her knees, palms down, where he could see every finger. "Watch them."

He watched.

"The letter is the queen's, in her own hand, under the archive seal. It's about my grandfather. Warden Harish, of the Shilak post. The chronicle says he went out in a night storm to check the cairns and was carried down at the thaw." Her voice was level. "The letter says the storm was an excuse. He was guided to the Under-Cairn, beneath the Shilak Cairn, where the Old Bargain is paid. Prince Udai of Neshar guided him. And he did not come home. He was found at the thaw on the slope above the third bend, with his gloves folded on a flat stone, palm to palm, square, the fingers pointing the way he'd gone."

"The gloves your father spoke of."

"Yes."

"Why does the queen tell you?"

"Because the next spring the crown wrote the Closing Law, so nobody would cross in winter and ask what had happened to a warden. She wants what's under the cairn found. She wants it read. The crown has never dared. The letter says what is owed is written down there." She breathed. "And it says one more thing. *Tell no one Neshar's part.*"

The embers shifted. He had known, he found. He had known since she laid her left hand flat on her coat at the lakeside cairn. He had only not wished to.

"You swore an oath," he said slowly. "For the commission."

"The archive oath. And the queen's order sits on top of it." She said it as she would give a distance. "Telling you breaks both. It may cost me the Chief Surveyor's seal. I've worked for it since I was nineteen. I'm choosing it."

He understood what she had just spent, and that she had set it down without asking for change.

"The sheets," he said.

"In the gap behind the plank shelf in the second hut. All of them. I didn't burn anything." She held his eyes. "I'm sorry for the lie. I'm not sorry I told it."

"I sifted that hearth with my back to you." He almost laughed, and it came out wrong. "I stood three paces from them."

"A hand deep. You'd have found them if you'd looked at the wall."

"I was looking at the ash. That's what you meant me to do."

"Yes."

She was about to say the next thing. He saw her mouth begin to shape it, the soft thing, the *it was long ago, it wasn't you, it was his time.*

"Udai walked him up the princes' way," said Kunal, before she could. "My grandfather. Sixty winters ago, in the snow, to the place under the mountain. And let him pay." He kept his voice flat, because it was that or none. "My family walked your grandfather to his death, Ila, and buried it for sixty winters."

"Kunal. He was—"

"Don't make it smaller. Not for me." He looked at his burnt palms. "Pritam told me the story over supper the week after my father's funeral. Udai showed the way once, to one guest, and was never the same. *The way kills those who share it.* He made it the family rule that night. No prince shows the way to wife, son or friend. I was nine. I thought it was about my grandfather's grief." He made a sound in his throat and pressed the heel of his hand to his mouth. "It was about a grave. The guest died, and my family built a rule round the hole so nobody would look in."

"You didn't know."

"I didn't. I'd have been the next man to keep the rule." He lowered the hand. "Yet I'm still sitting here."

She did not answer, and he found he did not need her to.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


"I said you'd let Dhanu die for your queen's map," said Kunal. "In the voice I use on men I mean to bleed. I knew what it would do when I said it. I was wrong, and I said it anyway."

"I know."

"I'm not asking you to forget it."

"I won't." She held his eyes. "I lied to you because I didn't want to argue it out. I could have made you hear me, or tried, with every figure I had. I decided you wouldn't, and decided it for you. It happened to be true. It wasn't mine to find out that way." Her fingers lay still. "I'm not saying that to be forgiven."

"Good. I told you I don't forgive you." He turned his ruined hands palm up between them. "I'll amend it. I don't excuse you, and I don't excuse me. I see what you did. I see how you did it, and why, and what it cost."

"That's enough."

"It isn't," said Kunal. "But it's true."

Silence was free. For once he didn't hate it. He sat in it and felt it hold weight.

"The strip on your hem," he said. "The one you offered."

She had it torn off in a breath. She bound his right palm with it, knotting it flat, her warm fingers quick and sure over the broken skin. The wool of the pattu brushed his wrist. When she tied it off she held on a moment longer.

"Do you still want to go under the mountain?" he asked.

"Yes."

"With your foot."

"With my foot." She turned her head toward the dark passage, where the blue stones lay faint along the gravel. "My grandfather paid something, and somebody wrote it down. I would rather read it than be told it. I've spent my life drawing what other people could see. I want to read the one thing nobody drew."

"The Meadow Cairn won't wait for Dhanu to be buried."

"No."

"Then I'll take you." He held out his bare right wrist. "Tie it."

She took up the undyed wool where it lay across the gravel. Her fingers did the warden's double hitch, two turns, a tuck, a locking half-turn, clean on his wrist, as she had done it at the Meadow Cairn with more hope than she had now. Her end was already tied. The rope lay between them like a line drawn on a sheet.


::: {.scene-break}
\* \* \*
:::


Gauri came back when the light had gone grey. She had a lamp under her arm, a skin of oil and a cloth sack that smelled of bread. She set them down in a row, the way a woman lays out a trader's goods, and looked anywhere but the rope.

"I'll stay at the huts," she said. "Till the thaw."

"Gauri," said Kunal.

"I've laid stones on my brother at the fold. The ground won't take him till the thaw, and he'd not like to be carried." Her eyes were dry. "And someone has to keep the hearth lit for the next pair through. He'd have said so. He'd have said it was no use hating a lamp."

"Let me send someone."

"Send nobody." She said it gently. "Go and do what you've gone for."

He undid the whistle. It took him some time, with the thong stiff and the heel of his hand clumsy against the knot. He held it out.

She looked at it. She did not take it.

"Keep it," said Gauri. "Two long and a short. Blow it when it's clear."

He closed his hand round the horn.

"The pen wall at Ewe's Rest," he said. "Ila showed you where the north side was short. Footed, it'll hold this spring." He had no idea why he was saying it, only that it was the nearest solid thing. "The first pair of lambs born there, whoever's they are. I'll send them to you. The first spring pair."

The rope lay across his wrist. As the words left him it moved against his skin, a low, level hum, as even as a drawn string. It ran up through his arm and into his chest, and across the gravel Ila's head came up. She felt it too.

Gauri heard it. He saw her hear it.

"Then I'll expect them," she said. She picked up the shawl, folded it, and laid it across Ila's feet.
